# 33. Sūrat al-Aḥzāb

**73 verses.** Medinan. It takes its name from the word **ٱلْأَحْزَاب** (*al-aḥzāb*) that occurs in verses **20** and **22**: "confederated groups, companies come together".

This sūra is one of the densest and most misunderstood texts in the Qurʾān. Dense, because things that look very far apart stand side by side in a single sūra: a scene of siege, a regulation about adoption, a code of conduct for a household, a trust the heavens shrank from carrying. Misunderstood, because some of its verses, cut loose from their context, have become the material of quite different arguments.

The method to be followed in this commentary is this: **to read the sūra by its own internal logic.** And that logic stands in the first sentence of the fourth verse:

> **مَّا جَعَلَ ٱللَّهُ لِرَجُلٍ مِّن قَلْبَيْنِ فِى جَوْفِهِۦ** — *"God has not made two hearts inside any man."* (33/4)

This sentence is not giving a piece of anatomy. It lays down a **principle**, and the rest of the sūra consists largely of applications of that principle: **changing the name of a thing does not change its reality.**

- Saying to a wife "you are my mother" does not make her a mother (4).
- Calling a child "my son" does not make him a son by birth (4-5).
- Saying at the moment of a siege "our houses are exposed" does not turn flight into a legitimate excuse (13).
- Saying "we have believed" does not change where the heart is looking in the moment of danger (12-19).
- And from the other side: just as a man's being called Zayd b. Muḥammad did not really make him a son, so the consequences of that are put right not by a name but by an **act** (37, 40).

From beginning to end the sūra measures **the distance between a name and a reality.** I record this as my own reading; but its ground is the text itself: the sentence *ذَٰلِكُمْ قَوْلُكُم بِأَفْوَٰهِكُمْ* ("that is your saying with your mouths") in the fourth verse is a key to the reading, placed at the head of the sūra.

## The name of the sūra

The root analysis of the word *ḥizb* was made at `058-mucadele.md` (58/19 and 58/22, *ḥizb al-shayṭān* / *ḥizb Allāh*). I do not repeat here what was recorded there; I add to it.

What was established there is this: the root **ح-ز-ب** carries both the sense "a community gathered around a purpose" and the sense "the pressing of an affair", and what joins the two senses is this: people gather around a shared distress. A *ḥizb* is not a blood tie; it is a **chosen** belonging.

The name of this sūra is the plural of that word: **al-Aḥzāb** — "the groups". And there is a nicety worth noting in the giving of this name to this sūra:

**The sūra does not call the besieging side by any one name.** It gives no tribal name, no name of a leader. The word it uses says **what that side is**: companies separate from one another, insufficient on their own, able to form a force only by coming together. That is, even in defining the enemy the name says that the enemy is **a temporary composite**.

And indeed the twenty-fifth verse will describe the dissolving of this composite, and the verb used there will be **رَدَّ** — to turn back. What came together dispersed; the word itself had already announced this.

## Historical frame: what we know, and from where

I apply here the three-layered frame set up at `048-fetih.md`, because in this sūra the risk of confusion is higher. The sūra **refers to** a military event from the ninth verse to the twenty-seventh, and to a family event in the thirty-seventh — it narrates neither. The narrative comes entirely from the biographical reports.

### What the text of the Qurʾān says

The whole of what can be drawn from the sūra as fact about the siege section (9-27) is this:

- **Armies** (*junūd*) came upon those addressed; a **wind** and **armies unseen** were sent against them (9).
- Those who came came **from above and from below** (10).
- Eyes swerved, hearts reached the throats, and all manner of surmises were entertained about God (10).
- The believers were **tested** and **shaken with a violent shaking** (11).
- The hypocrites and those with sickness in their hearts said that the promise given was a delusion (12).
- One party said, "O people of **Yathrib**, there is no place for you to stay, go back" (13).
- Another party asked leave, alleging that their houses were **exposed**; the verse records that this was a cover (13).
- There was a **pledge** given earlier: "we shall not turn our backs" (15).
- One party held others back and said to their brothers, "come to us" (18).
- When the danger passed, that same party spoke with sharp tongues (19).
- One party supposes the *aḥzāb* have not gone; if they came, it would wish to be out in the desert among the bedouin (20).
- The believers said, when they saw the *aḥzāb*, that their faith increased (22).
- There are **men** who kept the pledge they gave; some have died, some are waiting (23).
- The disbelievers were turned back **with their rage**, having gained no good (25).
- Those of the People of the Book who supported them were **brought down from their strongholds**; some were killed, some taken captive (26).
- Their land, their dwellings, their possessions and **a land they had not set foot on** were given in inheritance (27).

What the text says about the Zayd section (37):

- There is a person whom God had favoured and whom the Prophet had also favoured, and his **name is Zayd**.
- The Prophet said to him, "keep your wife with you, and be mindful of God".
- The Prophet concealed within himself something that God was to bring out, and was wary of people; the verse says that the one to be wary of is God.
- When Zayd had ended his tie with that woman, God married her to the Prophet.
- The ground is given openly: **so that there should be no difficulty for the believers regarding the wives of their adopted sons.**

### What the Qurʾān does not say

**The only place-name in the sūra is *Yathrib* (13)** — and that from the mouths not of the believers but of those calling for a return. The word "trench" (*khandaq*) **does not occur in the Qurʾān.** The name "Medina" occurs twice in this sūra (at 60, *al-madīna*), but not in the account of the siege.

None of the following is in the text: how many days the siege lasted; the existence, the shape, or the proposer of a dug entrenchment; which tribes the besiegers were; their numbers; the names of the commanders; the name of the group mentioned in verse 26; in which year the siege occurred; how many were killed; who "those who kept their pledge" in verse 23 were.

In the account of Zayd what is missing from the text is even more striking: the woman's name; how the marriage was made; how it ended; **what** it was that the Prophet "concealed within himself"; the sequence and the duration of the events.

All of this comes from the reports, and on some points the sources differ from one another.

### What the reports recount

In the classical works of exegesis and biography it is widely reported that verses 9-27 came down after a combined siege directed at Medina **in the fifth year of the Hijra.** The account is transmitted in outline as follows: that a force based in Mecca, joining with certain surrounding tribes and with a group expelled from Medina, besieged the city; that for defence a **trench** was dug along the open side of the city; that the siege lasted some weeks; that provisions ran short and the weather was cold; that news spread within the city of a breakdown in relations with a group bound by treaty; and that in the end a violent wind tore up the tents and the besiegers dispersed.

**In this commentary I use this account with the note "it is transmitted", and without entering into detail.** I give no tribal name, no personal name, and no numbers. The reason is the measure recorded at `049-hucurat.md` and at `048-fetih.md`: **attaching the name of a tribe or a community to a verse can be read as a ruling about people who today bear that name or are traced to that lineage.** The wording of the Qurʾān does not do this; the verse speaks not of a tribe but of "those who came", "those who said", "those who kept their pledge".

**Verses 26-27 require a further note as well**, and it will be given in detail beneath those verses: the text recounts a **single event** that took place upon a **breach of treaty**, concerning one community, within the law of war of that day. No general ruling about a religion or a lineage is drawn from these verses. The note in `STYLE.md` that "no wholesale ruling is issued about an ethnic or religious group" does its heaviest work in this sūra just there.

For the account of Zayd it is reported in the sources that Zayd was adopted by the Prophet and was for a time called "Zayd b. Muḥammad"; and that after the regulation in verses 4-5 this ascription was removed. Into the details about how the marriage was made and how it ended I **do not enter**; I explain the reason at verse 37.

### How these two layers should be used

The four measures set down at `048-fetih.md` hold here too; I repeat them briefly and add a fifth.

**One. The reports are necessary**, because the sūra does not narrate an event, it **refers to** one. Only the reports supply the context.

**Two. The reports do not carry the same degree of certainty as the Qurʾān.** The details are given with the note "this is how it is transmitted", and no detail is made a condition of a verse's meaning.

**Three. The human states the sūra describes do not depend on the reports.** The swerving of the eye, the heart at the throat, the manufacture of excuses, the voice that rises once the danger has passed — these can be read and recognised with no information brought in from outside.

**Four. No wholesale ruling will be issued about any group.** When the sūra says "the hypocrites" it names not an identity but a **behaviour**; the measure recorded at `063-munafikun.md` holds here too: hypocrisy is by definition something that cannot be seen from outside, and therefore these verses cannot be used to identify anyone else.

**Five — particular to this sūra.** The sūra contains verses about the Prophet's family, his wives and his household (6, 28-34, 37, 50-53, 55, 59). Some of these verses stand at the centre of the oldest disagreements in Islamic history. The path followed in this commentary is this: **the disagreement is not concealed; it is given in a table, with its grounds, and impartially; no preference is imposed; no insinuation is made against any group; no polemic is entered.** The data the text gives (wording, context, form) is recorded separately — but it is also recorded that a datum of the text does not by itself settle a debate.

## The structure of the sūra

| Block | Verses | Subject | What closes the block |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Opening: whom one follows** | 1-3 | Mindfulness of God; not obeying the disbelievers and the hypocrites; following the revelation; reliance | *wakīlā* |
| **The principle: name and reality** | 4-5 | No two hearts in one body; *ẓihār*; adoption; the preserving of the tie of lineage | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |
| **The re-founding of the bond** | 6 | The precedence of the Prophet; the maternal standing of his wives; kinship | *masṭūrā* |
| **The covenant** | 7-8 | The firm pledge taken from the prophets; the truthfulness that will be asked of the truthful | *ʿadhāban alīmā* |
| **Siege I: the scene** | 9-11 | The wind and the unseen armies; the eyes, the hearts, the surmises; the test and the shaking | *zilzālan shadīdā* |
| **Siege II: the anatomy of an excuse** | 12-20 | The promise counted a delusion; "there is no place to stay"; "our houses are exposed"; the reminder of the pledge given; sharp tongues; *ashiḥḥa* | *illā qalīlā* |
| **Siege III: the other side** | 21-24 | *Uswa ḥasana*; the response of the believers; the men who kept their pledge; a two-branched outcome | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |
| **Siege IV: the dissolving** | 25-27 | Being turned back in rage; the bringing down of the supporters; inheritance | *qadīrā* |
| **The household I: a choice** | 28-29 | Two roads set before the wives: the life of this world, or God, His Messenger and the hereafter | *ajran ʿaẓīmā* |
| **The household II: standing and measure** | 30-34 | A twofold responsibility; "you are not like any of the women"; speech, house, prayer, alms, obedience; *taṭhīr*; remembering what is recited | *khabīrā* |
| **The general principle** | 35 | Ten paired attributes; the longest parallel enumeration of man and woman | *ajran ʿaẓīmā* |
| **Application: Zayd** | 36-40 | The removal of the option; the account; the ground; *khātam al-nabiyyīn* | *ʿalīmā* |
| **Direction: remembrance and messengership** | 41-48 | Much remembrance; glorification morning and evening; *ṣalāt*; five attributes; paying no heed to injury | *wakīlā* |
| **The household III: regulations** | 49-52 | Divorce without a waiting period; rulings particular to the Prophet; the easing of the turn; the limit | *raqībā* |
| **The household IV: conduct** | 53-55 | Not entering without leave; not lingering over talk; asking from behind a screen; those within the degrees of kinship | *shahīdā* |
| **Ṣalāt and injury** | 56-58 | The ṣalāt of God and of the angels; the ṣalāt of the believers; the two directions of injury | *mubīnā* |
| **Being recognised** | 59 | *Jilbāb*; the verse's own ground: to be recognised and not injured | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |
| **The order of the city** | 60-62 | Those who spread baseless news; *sunnat Allāh* | *tabdīlā* |
| **The Hour** | 63-68 | That its time is unknown; the turning of the faces; "we obeyed our masters" | *laʿnan kabīrā* |
| **Speech** | 69-71 | Do not be like those who injured Moses (Mūsā); *qawlan sadīd* | *fawzan ʿaẓīmā* |
| **Closing: the trust** | 72-73 | The offering of the trust; the shrinking of the heavens; man's taking it up; the outcome | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |

Three things in this structure are worth noticing.

**One. The sūra opens with a principle and is centred on the application of that principle.** The fourth and fifth verses abolish the ascription of the adopted son; verses thirty-six to forty recount a concrete consequence of that abolition. The thirty verses in between — siege, household, general principle — are the ground that binds the two together. **The sūra has set the place where it lays down the ruling thirty verses away from the place where it applies it.** This is not disorder; it is an order that lets the principle settle before the application. I record it as my own reading.

**Two. The word *heart* stands along the spine of the sūra.** Its first occurrence is in the fourth verse (*qalbayn* — two hearts), and there it states an **impossibility**. Then it comes one after another in the siege section: hearts reach the throats (10), in hearts there is sickness (12), hearts are filled with *taẓunnūn* (10). And it continues with *taṭhīr* in the thirty-third verse and with *qulūbikum wa qulūbihinna* in the fifty-third. **The sūra opens indivisibility with the heart and carries on purification with the heart.**

**Three. There are two notices of "sunnat Allāh"** (38 and 62), and both come after a regulation. The sūra presents the rulings it brings not as "new" but as **the continuation of a way that has always run.**

## Four axes running through the sūra

Before coming to the verses let me record the four threads that touch the whole sūra; each will be treated separately at the relevant verse.

**The first axis: name and reality.** 4, 5, 13, 37, 40. The distance between the name given to a thing and the thing itself. The founding idea of the sūra.

**The second axis: injury (أ-ذ-ي).** The word occurs in the sūra with unusual density: 48, 53 (twice), 57, 58, 59, 69. **And each time it has a different face:** injury done to the Prophet, the discomfort the Prophet felt but could not voice, slander against the believers, the harassment women met with in the street, and what a past prophet suffered from his people. The sūra describes hurting as a social fact.

**The third axis: speech (ق-و-ل and its kin).** *Qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* (4), *lā takhḍaʿna bi'l-qawl* (32), *qawlan maʿrūfā* (32), *qawlan sadīdā* (70). The sūra makes the manner in which a thing is said a matter of ethics.

**The fourth axis: the house (بيت).** The word occurs again and again in the sūra: *buyūtunā ʿawra* (13), *qarna fī buyūtikunna* (33), *ahl al-bayt* (33), *buyūtikunna* (34), *buyūt al-nabiyy* (53), *buyūtihinna* (in the context of 55). **The sūra begins at the houses of a city under siege and goes as far as the door of a prophet's house.** In this sūra the house is the name both of what is to be protected and of the boundary that must be kept.

## The basmala

The analysis of the basmala was made at al-Fātiḥa; it is not repeated here. The verse numbers are counted without the basmala.

---

## 33/1-3 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ ٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ وَلَا تُطِعِ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ وَٱلْمُنَٰفِقِينَ · وَٱتَّبِعْ مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ · وَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ وَكِيلًا

*Yā ayyuha'l-nabiyyu'ttaqi'llāha wa lā tuṭiʿi'l-kāfirīna wa'l-munāfiqīn, inna'llāha kāna ʿalīman ḥakīmā · Wa'ttabiʿ mā yūḥā ilayka min rabbik, inna'llāha kāna bimā taʿmalūna khabīrā · Wa tawakkal ʿala'llāh, wa kafā bi'llāhi wakīlā*

"O Prophet! Guard yourself against God; do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites. God is Knowing, Wise. · Follow what is revealed to you from your Lord. God is aware of what you do. · Rely on God. God suffices as a trustee."

### يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ — the choice of address

At `073-muzzemmil.md` the Qurʾān's forms of address to the Prophet were set out in a table; what was recorded there was this: the address **يَا أَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ** calls not to the person but to the **office**.

This sūra opens with that address and repeats it **five times** within the sūra: 1, 28, 45, 50, 59. **A command follows all five**, and all five begin a different block of the sūra. That is, the address is the sūra's section-marker.

| Place | What follows |
|---|---|
| **33/1** | Taqwā, and whom one is not to obey |
| **33/28** | The choice to be set before the wives |
| **33/45** | The enumeration of the attributes of messengership |
| **33/50** | A regulation particular to the Prophet |
| **33/59** | What is to be said to his wives, his daughters and the believing women |

**A fact of word order worth recording:** three of the five addresses (28, 50, 59) concern the Prophet's household and family. The sūra uses the form that calls to the office most often in the places that have to do with the **house**. This shows that the affairs of the household are placed not as a piece of private life but as **a part of the office**. I record this as my own reading.

### ٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ — the saying of "guard yourself" to a prophet

**Root: و-ق-ي.** To protect oneself, to take a shield for oneself. The analysis of the root was made at `002-bakara.md` (2/2, *hudan li'l-muttaqīn*); I do not repeat it here.

What is striking here is the one addressed. Saying to a prophet "guard yourself against God" may look unnecessary at first sight. But the sense of the root corrects that impression: *taqwā* is not a moving away from a sin but **a search for protection.** And the search is required not according to a level but **according to a situation.**

Indeed it is enough to look at what comes immediately after the command: *"do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites."* That is, *taqwā* here is not an abstract call to piety; it is the name of **a concrete answer to be given in the face of a pressure.**

### وَلَا تُطِعِ — "do not obey"

**Root: ط-و-ع.** The concrete sense of the root is **willingness**; as was recorded at `048-fetih.md` and at `096-alak.md`, *ṭawʿ* is the opposite of *karh* (compulsion).

**And this explains why the prohibition is framed with this verb.** The verse does not say "do not do what they say"; it says "do not **fall in willingly** with them". That is, what is forbidden is not something done under compulsion; it is an alignment a person enters by his own consent.

**And the command *lā tuṭiʿ* occurs twice in the sūra:** here (1) and in the forty-eighth verse. Between the two lie the twenty-seven verses of the siege section and the nineteen verses of the household section. **That is, the sūra frames all the sections in between by repeating the same command at its two ends.** In the forty-eighth verse an addition will be made to the command: *wa daʿ adhāhum* — "pay no heed to their injury."

### The disbelievers and the hypocrites side by side

The two groups are named within the same prohibition, and this announces in advance the whole shape of the sūra: in the siege section too the two will stand side by side — one from outside (9-10, 25), the other from inside (12-20).

**But what the two words point to is not the same.** As recorded at `002-bakara.md`, the root ك-ف-ر is **to cover**; the root ن-ف-ق, as recorded at `063-munafikun.md`, is **to have two doors.** One names an open refusal, the other a hidden way out.

**And the reason the two are joined in the same prohibition stands in the verb of the verse:** *obedience.* A person may fall in with either of them for the same reason — pressure, interest, the desire to fit in. That is, the verse is not likening the two groups to one another; it is saying that **falling in with them** is the same thing.

Per the method (`STYLE.md`) I record: both of the words named here are **attributes**, not the name of a community. Whoever bears that attribute is the one the verse speaks of.

### وَٱتَّبِعْ — the counterpart of the prohibition

The second verse leaves no gap. The first verse said "do not follow"; the second verse says "follow **this**".

**This is a recurring structure in the Qurʾān's way of framing a prohibition, and it is worth recording:** when one direction is closed, another direction is put in its place. The gap that is closed is not left empty.

**تَتَّبِعْ / ٱتَّبِعْ** — **root ت-ب-ع**: to go behind someone, to follow his track. The word presupposes a **precedence**: the one followed is in front, the one following behind.

### مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَيْكَ — the imperfect

The verb is not perfect (*ūḥiya* — has been revealed) but **imperfect** (*yūḥā* — is being revealed). The form conveys an action in progress.

**And this describes the sūra's own situation:** the sūra consists of verses that came down in the middle of a siege, a family, a city. Revelation is named here not as a completed text but as something **coming down into the midst of events.**

### وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ وَكِيلًا — the pattern

The construction **كَفَىٰ بِـ** in Arabic states a sufficiency, and the *bāʾ* at its head is among the particles the lexicographers count as *zāʾida* (added for emphasis): it carries a stress like "sufficient indeed is God".

**وَكِيل — root و-ك-ل.** To commit a matter to another; a *wakīl* is the one to whom the matter is left. From the same root, *tawakkul* — to leave the matter to him.

**The nicety in the word order of the verse:** in the same sentence the verb comes first (*tawakkal*), then the attribute that is the object of that verb (*wakīlā*). That is, the command and the ground of the command are from the same root. **"Leave it to Him; because the one to whom things are left is He."**

### Context

The three verses set up a sequence, and the sequence cannot be reversed:

| Order | Command | Its function |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Ittaqi* | The inner state — the search for protection |
| 2 | *Lā tuṭiʿ* | Towards the outside — not falling into line |
| 3 | *Ittabiʿ* | Direction — what is to be followed |
| 4 | *Tawakkal* | Leaving the outcome |

**The fourth step meets the cost of the first three.** Not falling in with the disbeliever and the hypocrite exacts a price; for that price to be bearable, the outcome must be capable of being left somewhere. The rest of the sūra — the siege section above all — will look precisely at the moment when that price is paid.

### What this says to the present

The prohibition of the first verse, said in a contemporary idiom, is this: **aligning oneself with a group's expectations out of an anxiety to look good.**

The verse depicts this as a pressure coming from two different sources: those who openly oppose, and those who work from inside to soften. And the second is usually the more effective, because it speaks the same language.

**The measure the verse gives is not a call to resistance but a call to a *direction*:** not "do not follow", but follow **this**. The most durable form of standing against something is not standing against it, but being attached to something else.

---

## 33/4 — مَّا جَعَلَ ٱللَّهُ لِرَجُلٍ مِّن قَلْبَيْنِ فِى جَوْفِهِۦ وَمَا جَعَلَ أَزْوَٰجَكُمُ ٱلَّٰٓـِٔى تُظَٰهِرُونَ مِنْهُنَّ أُمَّهَٰتِكُمْ وَمَا جَعَلَ أَدْعِيَآءَكُمْ أَبْنَآءَكُمْ ذَٰلِكُمْ قَوْلُكُم بِأَفْوَٰهِكُمْ وَٱللَّهُ يَقُولُ ٱلْحَقَّ وَهُوَ يَهْدِى ٱلسَّبِيلَ

*Mā jaʿala'llāhu li-rajulin min qalbayni fī jawfih, wa mā jaʿala azwājakumu'llāʾī tuẓāhirūna minhunna ummahātikum, wa mā jaʿala adʿiyāʾakum abnāʾakum, dhālikum qawlukum bi-afwāhikum, wa'llāhu yaqūlu'l-ḥaqqa wa huwa yahdi'l-sabīl*

"God has not made two hearts inside any man. He has not made your wives, of whom you make the ẓihār declaration, your mothers. He has not made your adopted sons your sons. These are your sayings with your mouths. God says the truth, and He guides to the way."

### The founding verse of the sūra

This verse is the most important place in the sūra, and the reason lies less in its content than in its **function.** The verse lays down the principle on which every ruling that follows will rest.

There are three sentences, and all three are built on the same pattern: **مَا جَعَلَ ٱللَّهُ** — "God has not made…".

| Clause | The claim | The reality |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Two hearts in one body** | There can be two centres inside a person | There cannot — a person is not divisible |
| **2. Ẓihār** | A word makes a wife a mother | It does not — motherhood comes by bearing |
| **3. The adopted son** | A word makes an adopted son a son by birth | It does not — lineage is founded by birth |

This table was also set up at `058-mucadele.md` when ẓihār was treated; I take it from there and **I rely on that treatment.**

**But there is a further question that must be asked here: why are these three things in the *same* sentence?**

The answer lies in the structure the three share: **all three are a claim about reality established by speech.** The first is a way of thinking, the second a legal act, the third a social institution. But all three make the same mistake: **they erase the difference between saying and being.**

And the verse closes all three with a single ruling: *dhālikum qawlukum bi-afwāhikum.*

### مَّا جَعَلَ ٱللَّهُ لِرَجُلٍ مِّن قَلْبَيْنِ فِى جَوْفِهِۦ

The sentence must be opened word by word, because every word in it has been chosen.

**جَعَلَ — root ج-ع-ل.** To bring a thing into a state, to render, to arrange. Its difference from *khalaqa* (to create) is this: *khalq* conveys bringing into being out of nothing, *jaʿl* conveys giving a quality or a function to what already exists. **That the verb chosen here is *jaʿl* is significant:** the verse is not giving a piece of information about creation, it is speaking about a matter of **status.** "God did not give this thing this function."

**لِرَجُلٍ — "for a man".** Since it comes indefinite and in a negative context, it conveys **generality**: "for no man at all". That the word is *rajul* is explained in two ways in classical exegesis: (a) the context concerns matters founded by the man's act, such as ẓihār and adoption; (b) *rajul* here stands for "human being" as a kind. **That this does not mean the ruling leaves woman outside** is clear from the fact that those addressed in the second sentence are again men.

**قَلْبَيْنِ — the dual.** Two hearts.

**فِى جَوْفِهِۦ — "inside him, in his cavity".** **Root: ج-و-ف.** *Jawf* — the inner cavity of a thing; the inside of the body. *Ajwaf* — that which is hollow.

**It is worth pausing on why this note is added.** "He has not made two hearts" would have sufficed; the addition of "inside him" looks like a repetition. But the repetition does a piece of work: it says that **the place where the heart is, is a single enclosed volume.** That is, the matter is not the existence of two organs; it is that **two centres cannot be found in a single cavity.**

The conception of the heart recorded at `049-hucurat.md` works here too: in the Qurʾān the heart is depicted as an **enclosed volume** — it is sealed, it is locked, sickness is found within it, rust lies upon it. This verse states the **singleness** of that volume.

### What the sentence says, and what it does not say

Care is needed here, because two kinds of interpretation circulate about this sentence.

**What it does not say:** This sentence is not giving a piece of anatomy and cannot be presented as a "scientific miracle". The note in `STYLE.md` that "no hunting for scientific miracles is done" works here exactly. The verse is not reporting the number of the human heart; it is already using the word heart in the sense of **the centre of consciousness** — indeed the same sūra will say a few verses later "those in whose hearts is sickness", and there too no organ will be meant.

**What it says:** There cannot be **two separate centres of orientation** inside a person. And this connects directly with the context of the verse: a woman cannot be both wife and mother; a child cannot be both another man's son and your son. **One body, one centre.**

**In classical exegesis several explanations are transmitted about the setting of this sentence:**

| Explanation | What it says | Its link with the verse |
|---|---|---|
| **The impossibility of hypocrisy** | Faith and denial do not lodge in one heart | Verse 1 of the sūra had named the hypocrites; 12-20 will describe them at length |
| **An introduction to ẓihār and adoption** | The sentence is an image set up for the two sentences that follow | Word order: three sentences in the same pattern |
| **An answer to a particular saying** | It is transmitted that someone in that milieu, boasting of his intelligence, said "I have two hearts inside me" | It rests on a report; its details are not certain |

**The third explanation has been given with the note "it is transmitted"; it is not presented as certain knowledge.** And this must be recorded: the first and second explanations do not exclude one another. **The function of the sentence within the sūra is the same whichever explanation is taken:** the principle of indivisibility.

### تُظَٰهِرُونَ مِنْهُنَّ — ẓihār

**Root: ظ-ه-ر (back).** What ẓihār is, why it is framed with the word "back", its legal consequence in the pre-Islamic period, the three-step procedure the Qurʾān brought and the regulation of the expiation **were treated in detail at `058-mucadele.md` (58/1-4). I rely on that treatment here and do not repeat it.**

Of what was recorded there I recall only what is needed to understand this verse: *ẓihār* was a man's saying to his wife "you are to me as my mother's back"; in that order this saying left the woman suspended, **neither divorced nor married.** Sūrat al-Mujādala removed that suspension.

**What al-Aḥzāb does is different, and complementary.** Al-Mujādala brought the **regulation** (the invalidity of the saying + the expiation). Al-Aḥzāb ties the **reason why** the saying is invalid to a principle: because there are not two hearts in one body.

**A note on word order:** in al-Mujādala the verb is *yuẓāhirūna min nisāʾihim* (from their women), here it is *tuẓāhirūna minhunna* — and before it comes *azwājakum* (your wives). **Al-Aḥzāb uses the word "wife".** That is, the verse names the object of ẓihār not as "woman" but as **the one bound by the tie of marriage.** What the saying sets out to break is thereby plainly visible: a bond.

### أَدْعِيَآءَكُمْ — adopted sons

**Singular: دَعِىّ (*daʿiyy*). Root: د-ع-و.** To call, to summon, to name, to invite. From the same root: *duʿāʾ*, *dāʿī* (the one who calls — it will be used of the Prophet in verse 46 of this sūra), *daʿwā* (a claim), *iddiʿāʾ*.

**The word itself carries the ruling within it.** *Daʿiyy* means "the one called a son" — that is, in the root of the word there is **calling**, not **bearing.** Even in naming the adopted child, the Arabic language said that he is attached by a relation of **name.**

This supports the argument of the verse from within the language: **the name of the adopted son is already "the one called".** And the verse will correct exactly this in the next verse: *udʿūhum li-ābāʾihim* — "**call** them by their fathers." The same root, the same verb; only the direction changes.

### On the institution of adoption

It is transmitted in the classical sources that in pre-Islamic Arabia the institution of *tabannī* (adoption) was widespread, that the adopted child was called by the name of the one who adopted him, that he inherited from him, and that his tie with his own lineage was in effect severed. **This is a fact known beyond that period as well:** similar institutions are found in many ancient legal orders.

What the verse does is not to abolish the institution altogether but to abolish **the false lineage the institution set up.** The fifth verse will say this openly: the child may be cared for, looked after, counted a brother — but **his father is his father.**

### ذَٰلِكُمْ قَوْلُكُم بِأَفْوَٰهِكُمْ — the seal of the ruling

The ruling common to the three sentences.

**بِأَفْوَٰهِكُمْ — "with your mouths".** The finding recorded at `058-mucadele.md` about this note holds here too and is worth repeating: **speech is said with the mouth in any case; to state this separately looks like a repetition. But the repetition does a piece of work — it says that the saying *stays in the mouth*, that it does not pass into reality.**

To this I add one thing. The Qurʾān uses this pattern in other places too, and in all of them it does the same work: *"They say with their mouths what is not in their hearts"* (al-Fatḥ 48/11); *"That is their saying with their mouths"* (al-Tawba 9/30). **Each time, a gap between the saying and the reality is being announced.**

The plural of **فَم** is *afwāh*. And the naming of the organ stresses that speech is a **place of exit** — not a place of arrival.

### وَٱللَّهُ يَقُولُ ٱلْحَقَّ — the contrast

The last two sentences of the verse set up a contrast, and the contrast works **at the level of the verb.**

| | Subject | Verb | Object | Where it stays |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Man** | *qawlukum* | to say | ẓihār, tabannī | *bi-afwāhikum* — in the mouth |
| **God** | *wa'llāhu* | *yaqūlu* | *al-ḥaqq* | *yahdi'l-sabīl* — on the way |

**Both sides speak; but the speech of one stays in the mouth, while the speech of the other opens a way.**

**ٱلْحَقّ — root ح-ق-ق.** That which is fixed, which comes about, which stands in place. The concrete sense of the root was treated at `069-hakka.md`. **The word's place in this verse is particularly apt:** *ḥaqq* is "that which is fixed" — and the whole concern of the verse is that fixity cannot be established by speech.

**يَهْدِى ٱلسَّبِيلَ — "He guides to the way".** The verb has taken its object directly, without the particle *ilā* (*yahdi'l-sabīl*). The lexicographers count this among the verb's uses in which it can take the object directly.

**And the function of the sentence within the sūra is this:** when something is abolished, the question arises of what will be put in its place. The verse places the note of **guidance** immediately after the sentence that does the abolishing. The fifth verse will name that way.

### What this says to the present

What this verse says to the present is bound to none of its three examples. What it is bound to is the **principle.**

**Giving a thing a new name does not change the thing.** This was recorded at `058-mucadele.md`; here one further step is possible.

The verse also says **why** naming does not work: because a name is a matter of the *mouth*, while reality is a matter of the *body*. The distance between the two does not close.

And the verse also shows whom naming harms: **in ẓihār, the woman; in tabannī, the child.** In both examples the one who fixes the name pays no cost; the cost is paid by the one on whom the name is fixed. **Naming is, most of the time, a tool in the hand of the stronger.**

I record this as my own reading. The datum standing in the text is this: in both examples the agent of the verb is the man, and the one who bears the consequence is someone else.

---

## 33/5 — ٱدْعُوهُمْ لِءَابَآئِهِمْ هُوَ أَقْسَطُ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ فَإِن لَّمْ تَعْلَمُوٓا۟ ءَابَآءَهُمْ فَإِخْوَٰنُكُمْ فِى ٱلدِّينِ وَمَوَٰلِيكُمْ وَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ فِيمَآ أَخْطَأْتُم بِهِۦ وَلَٰكِن مَّا تَعَمَّدَتْ قُلُوبُكُمْ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا

*Udʿūhum li-ābāʾihim huwa aqsaṭu ʿinda'llāh, fa-in lam taʿlamū ābāʾahum fa-ikhwānukum fi'l-dīni wa mawālīkum, wa laysa ʿalaykum junāḥun fīmā akhṭaʾtum bihī wa lākin mā taʿammadat qulūbukum, wa kāna'llāhu ghafūran raḥīmā*

"Call them by their fathers; that is more equitable in God's sight. If you do not know their fathers, then they are your brothers in religion and your friends. There is no blame on you for what you have done in error; but what your hearts have intended is another matter. God is Forgiving, Merciful."

### The application of the fourth verse

The preceding verse made an **abolition**. This verse says what is to be done after the abolition. And there are four steps, in order:

1. **The basic rule:** call him by his father.
2. **If the father is unknown:** brother and friend.
3. **A wrong done in the past:** no liability.
4. **The measure:** the intention of the heart.

### ٱدْعُوهُمْ — the same root, the opposite direction

The verb is from the root **د-ع-و** and is from the same root as *adʿiyāʾ* in the preceding verse. **In the fourth verse the root named a false ascription; in the fifth it establishes the true one.**

This is a nicety to be recorded at the level of word order: **the verse does not forbid the faulty act; it turns its direction.** It does not say "do not call", it says "call **thus**". A name will be given to the child; only the name will be tied to the right place.

**لِءَابَآئِهِمْ — the particle *lām*.** The *lām* here conveys belonging: "as belonging to their fathers". That is, the ascription is not a preference but a statement of **membership**.

### أَقْسَطُ — root ق-س-ط

This root was treated in detail at `049-hucurat.md` (49/9, *aqsiṭū*) and its use with the opposite sense was recorded at `072-cin.md`. I rely on that treatment; I bring here only what is needed for this verse.

What was recorded there:

- **The concrete sense of the root: a portion, a share; and the giving of the share as it is due.** *Qisṭ* — a share. *Muqsiṭ* — the one who gives the share rightly.
- **In Form IV (*aqsaṭa*) the root conveys justice, in the bare form (*qasaṭa*) injustice** — it is among the examples the lexicographers mention under the heading *aḍdād*.
- **The difference between *ʿadl* and *qisṭ*:** *ʿadl* looks to the scales (are the two sides equal), *qisṭ* looks to the share (has each received what is his).

**And this distinction works exactly in this verse.** Why does the verse say **أَقْسَط** (the one that gives the share more rightly) rather than *aʿdal* (more just)?

Because what is at issue is not a matter of equalising but a matter of a **share**. Ascribing a child to his father is not giving something to anyone or taking something from anyone; it is showing rightly **whose share belongs to whom.** Lineage is less like a piece of property than like a **portion**: a person's share in the place he came from.

**That the word comes in the elative (comparative) pattern is also worth noting.** The verse does not say "this is just and the other is injustice"; it says "this is **more** correct". **The pattern makes a correction without accusing the other side** — and indeed, in the continuation of the verse liability for what was done in the past will be lifted. Pattern and ruling agree with each other.

**عِندَ ٱللَّهِ — "in God's sight".** The function of the note is this: the ground of this regulation is not social utility. The verse does not say "this is more useful" or "this is more orderly". **It says who it is that lays down the measure.**

### فَإِن لَّمْ تَعْلَمُوٓا۟ ءَابَآءَهُمْ — where the father is unknown

The verse makes not an exception but a **filling of a gap.** For as soon as the rule is laid down a question arises: what of the child whose father is unknown?

**فَإِخْوَٰنُكُمْ فِى ٱلدِّينِ** — "your brothers in religion". **وَمَوَٰلِيكُمْ** — "and your friends, your close ones".

**مَوَالِي — singular مَوْلَى. Root: و-ل-ي.** To be near, to be adjacent. This root is the same as that of *mawlā* at `047-muhammed.md` (47/11, *mawla'lladhīna āmanū*) and of *awlā* in verse 6 of this sūra — we shall come to it a few lines below.

**The word *mawlā* covers a wide field in Arabic**, and this comes from the sense of "nearness" in its root: master, the one who frees, the one freed, friend, helper, neighbour, cousin, ally by treaty. **The word conveys not a hierarchy but an *adjacency*; the hierarchy comes from the context.**

**What the verse does here is striking and worth recording.** When the tie of lineage was removed, a gap could have been left: the child might have belonged nowhere. The verse closes that gap — but **not by inventing a false lineage; by naming a real bond.** Brotherhood and being a *mawlā* are bonds that are made; they are not bonds that come by birth, and they carry no claim to take the place of bonds that come by birth.

**That is, the verse does not remove the relationship; it puts the right name to the relationship.** The founding principle of the sūra is at work here too.

### وَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ فِيمَآ أَخْطَأْتُم بِهِۦ

**جُنَاح — root ج-ن-ح.** A wing; and to lean to one side, to incline. *Junāḥ* is a swerving to one side from what is right — from this comes the sense "sin, liability".

**أَخْطَأْتُم — root خ-ط-أ.** *Khaṭaʾ* — an unintended mistake. When this root was treated at `069-hakka.md`, the distinction between **خَطَأ** (unintended error) and **خِطْء** (deliberate sin) was recorded, and this very verse was given as the example. I rely on that treatment.

This sentence has two possible extents, and both are mentioned in classical exegesis:

| Extent | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Retrospective** | There is no liability for ascriptions made before this regulation came |
| **Prospective** | Nor is there liability hereafter for a wrong ascription made by a slip of the tongue or in ignorance |

**The two do not exclude one another** and the wording of the verse is open to both. I do not impose a preference.

### وَلَٰكِن مَّا تَعَمَّدَتْ قُلُوبُكُمْ — where the measure lies

**تَعَمَّدَ — root ع-م-د.** *ʿAmūd* — a post, a pillar; the upright element that holds a thing up. *ʿAmada* — he turned towards a thing, he intended it. *Taʿammud* — doing a thing with intent.

**The link between the concrete sense of the root and intention is striking:** the pillar is the point a building **takes its direction from** — everything rests on it. *Taʿammud* is the point of support behind an act.

**And the place where the measure is set is the *heart*:** *mā taʿammadat **qulūbukum***. What is looked at is not the error the tongue makes but the intention the heart forms.

**This closes a ring with the fourth verse**, and this is the most elegant fact of word order to be recorded here:

- The fourth verse: **"there are not two *hearts* in one body"** — the heart, the place of indivisibility.
- The fourth verse: **"this is what you say with your mouths"** — speech stays in the mouth.
- The fifth verse: **"what your hearts have intended is another matter"** — liability lies not in the mouth but in the heart.

**That is, within two verses the sūra transfers authority from the mouth to the heart.** The mouth cannot establish reality; nor does the mouth bear the liability. Both are tied to the heart.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the arrangement of the three expressions within two verses.

### On the ruling that preserves the tie of lineage

This verse is counted among the basic texts on which the regulations concerning the preservation of lineage in Islamic law rest. **This commentary does not enter into legal detail**; it records only the frame that emerges from the wording of the verse:

- **Lineage is not established by speech and is not undone by speech.** It is established by birth.
- **A child is ascribed to his real father.** This has been characterised as "more correct" (*aqsaṭ*).
- **If the father is unknown**, there is no question of the child belonging nowhere: a tie of brotherhood and of being a *mawlā* is established.
- **Caring for, raising and protecting a child are not forbidden.** What is forbidden is **the alteration of the child's lineage.**

Proceeding from this frame the schools have developed detailed rulings on the child's care, inheritance, the degrees of kinship, and guardianship (*walāya*), and these rulings differ from one another. **These differences are not the subject of this commentary, and no preference is adopted here.**

### What this says to the present

The measure of the verse works today in places very different from the institution out of which it arose.

**One. A child's right to know where he comes from.** The verse protects the knowledge: the note *"if you do not know their fathers"* presupposes that knowing is the norm and not knowing the exception. **The Qurʾān here stands against the erasure of a piece of knowledge.**

**Two. The difference between caring for a child and altering who he is.** In abolishing the second the verse does not merely preserve the first; it also gives it a name: *ikhwān*, *mawālī*. **No false bond is needed in order to care.**

**Three. The measure of the word *aqsaṭ*.** A regulation has been characterised as "more correct", not as "obligatory" or "useful". And since *qisṭ* is the word that indicates a share: **a person's share in his own past is a right that belongs to him.**

---

## 33/6 — ٱلنَّبِىُّ أَوْلَىٰ بِٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ مِنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ وَأَزْوَٰجُهُۥٓ أُمَّهَٰتُهُمْ وَأُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَرْحَامِ بَعْضُهُمْ أَوْلَىٰ بِبَعْضٍ فِى كِتَٰبِ ٱللَّهِ مِنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُهَٰجِرِينَ إِلَّآ أَن تَفْعَلُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰٓ أَوْلِيَآئِكُم مَّعْرُوفًا كَانَ ذَٰلِكَ فِى ٱلْكِتَٰبِ مَسْطُورًا

*Al-nabiyyu awlā bi'l-muʾminīna min anfusihim wa azwājuhu ummahātuhum, wa ulu'l-arḥāmi baʿḍuhum awlā bi-baʿḍin fī kitābi'llāhi mina'l-muʾminīna wa'l-muhājirīna illā an tafʿalū ilā awliyāʾikum maʿrūfā, kāna dhālika fi'l-kitābi masṭūrā*

"The Prophet is nearer to the believers than their own selves (has more right over them). And his wives are their mothers. And blood relations are, in the Book of God, nearer to one another than the believers and the emigrants — except that you do a kindness to your friends. This is written in the Book."

### The place of the verse in the sūra

This verse is the direct continuation of verses 4-5, and seeing this is necessary in order to understand it.

The fourth and fifth verses **removed two bonds**: the false motherhood set up by ẓihār, the false sonship set up by tabannī. The sixth verse **establishes two bonds**: the precedence of the Prophet and the maternal standing of his wives. And then it puts **real kinship** in its place.

**That is, the sūra takes the bonds apart and lays them out again.** And the order is significant: first what is false is removed, then the place of what is real is fixed.

### أَوْلَىٰ — the key word

**Root: و-ل-ي.** The concrete sense of the root is **nearness, adjacency**. *Walī* — the one who is near; *walāya* — the authority and responsibility born of nearness; *mawlā* — it occurred in the preceding verse; *tawallī* — to turn towards or to turn one's back (since it is a word of direction, it goes both ways); *awwal* — according to some lexicographers from the same root, "the foremost, the nearest".

**أَوْلَىٰ** is in the elative pattern: "nearer / having more right".

**And this is the side of the word that resists translation.** No single English word carries it, because in Arabic it says two things at once: **nearness** and **precedence.** The one nearest to a thing is the one with the most say over it.

**That the word occurs twice in this verse is also worth noting:**

| Place | Who | Compared with |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *al-nabiyyu* | *min anfusihim* — the believers' own selves |
| **2** | *ulu'l-arḥāmi baʿḍuhum* | *min al-muʾminīna wa'l-muhājirīn* — the believers and the emigrants |

**The same word orders two different relationships.** And there is no tension between the two; they speak of different fields.

### ٱلنَّبِىُّ أَوْلَىٰ بِٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ مِنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ — the explanations of its extent

**The extent of this sentence has been debated in classical exegesis.** I give the disagreement in a table. **I construct no legal conclusion and I do not impose a preference.**

| Explanation | What it says | Its ground | The limit to be recorded |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Precedence of ruling and of call** | A command of the Prophet is preferred over the person's own choice | The wording of the verse; verse 36 of the sūra (*mā kāna li-muʾminin… al-khiyaratu*) | This does not fix the extent of the call; in which matters it holds has itself been debated |
| **Compassion and care** | The Prophet looks to the good of the believers more than they do themselves | The expression that follows in the same verse, "his wives are their mothers" — the tie of compassion in motherhood | This reading conveys not an authority but a **quality** |
| **Debt and liability** | The burden a believer leaves behind belongs to the Prophet | Some reports are transmitted to this effect; their details are not given here | It rests on a report; it is not explicit in the wording itself |
| **Inheritance (the view that counts it abrogated)** | It is said that the sentence at first brought a precedence in inheritance, and that this was then regulated by the *ulu'l-arḥām* clause in the same verse | The subject of the second half of the verse is inheritance and kinship | The claim of abrogation is disputed; no preference is adopted in this commentary |

**Two notes about this table:**

**One.** These explanations do not entirely exclude one another. More than one may be true at once; the wording of the verse imposes no restriction.

**Two.** In this commentary **no legal conclusion at all is drawn** from this sentence. The explanations have been conveyed; which of them has been made the ground of which ruling, and the divergences of the schools on the question, are not the business of this place.

### مِنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ — the work the word order does

What the sentence compares is striking. The verse does not say "the Prophet is nearer to you than your father, your mother, your tribe". What it sets opposite him is **the person himself**.

**This is the furthest point a sentence of comparison can go in the Arabic language.** Beyond the comparison no term is left: there is nothing nearer to a person than himself.

**And the choice of the word *nafs* is also worth noting.** *Nafs* is used in the Qurʾān both for "life, soul" and for "the person himself". The sentence uses both senses: "than your lives" and "than yourselves".

### وَأَزْوَٰجُهُۥٓ أُمَّهَٰتُهُمْ — the maternal standing of the wives

**This sentence is directly connected with the fourth verse, and if the connection is not recorded the verse remains half understood.**

The fourth verse had said: a word does not make a woman a mother — *"God has not made your wives, of whom you make the ẓihār declaration, your mothers."*

The sixth verse says: the Prophet's wives are the **mothers** of the believers.

**At first sight there seems to be a tension. There is none — and seeing the difference is the whole point of the verse.**

| | Ẓihār (verse 4) | This verse (6) |
|---|---|---|
| **Who says it** | A man, with his own mouth | God, in His Book |
| **The verb** | *jaʿala* negated: "He has not made" | *azwājuhu ummahātuhum* — a nominal sentence, a ruling |
| **What it establishes** | Nothing; the saying stays in the mouth | A **standing** |
| **Its extent** | The whole of motherhood is claimed | Limited — see below |

**That is, the fourth verse was saying "human speech cannot establish a status"; the sixth verse shows who the party is that *does* establish a status.** The two do not contradict; together they say exactly the principle of the sūra: **status depends on the authority of the one who fixes the name.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that two verses use the same semantic field (motherhood) with two different subjects.

**The extent of this maternal standing** has been treated in classical exegesis, and there is a broad record on these two points: (a) the aspect of honour and respect; (b) the ruling in verse 53 of the sūra, *"nor that you should marry them"*. **Whether this standing extends to other aspects, such as inheritance and the degrees of kinship, has by contrast been debated, and no ruling is issued in this commentary.**

### وَأُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَرْحَامِ — kinship

**ٱلْأَرْحَام — singular رَحِم. Root: ر-ح-م.** And the concrete sense of the root is the **womb** — the organ in which the child is carried in the mother. From the same root come *raḥma*, *al-Raḥmān*, *al-Raḥīm*.

**This link of roots is one of the most striking word-webs in the Qurʾān, and it was treated at `001-fatiha.md`.** What was recorded there was this: in Arabic the name of mercy is derived from the name of an organ; **mercy is the name of the bond between the one who carries and the one carried.**

**And in this verse the word works in exactly that sense:** *ulu'l-arḥām* — "those who have wombs", that is, those who come from the same womb, those who share a tie of blood.

**In view of the sūra as a whole this choice of word is worth noting:** the fourth and fifth verses had removed the false lineage. The sixth verse names the real lineage with an **anatomical** word. Against what is established by speech is set what is established by an organ.

### مِنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُهَٰجِرِينَ — set against what

The sentence sets kinship **against two groups**: the believers and the emigrants.

**The historical background of this is transmitted in the classical sources as follows:** that there was a practice of pairing as brothers between those who emigrated to Medina and the inhabitants of the city, and that this brotherhood for a time gave rise to a relationship of inheritance; it is transmitted that this verse tied inheritance to the tie of blood.

**I give this account with the note "it is transmitted" and do not enter into its legal consequence.** What emerges from the text is this: the verse does not deny the value of bonds that are made — indeed, immediately afterwards it opens a door to them.

### إِلَّآ أَن تَفْعَلُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰٓ أَوْلِيَآئِكُم مَّعْرُوفًا — the door left open

**There is a balance in the structure of the verse that is often missed.**

The sentence set a limit (kinship comes first), then immediately opened an exception: *"except that you do a kindness to your friends."*

**مَعْرُوف — root ع-ر-ف.** What is known, recognised, what everyone agrees to be good. The word will occur twice in this sūra (6 and 32), and in both places it will convey a **measure**.

**And what is striking is this:** the verse does not put a prohibition in the place of what it removes. The order of inheritance is tied to kinship, but **the possibility of doing good is left open.**

**This is a behaviour repeated throughout the sūra** and worth recording: the fifth verse, in removing the lineage of the adopted son, left the tie of brotherhood and of being a *mawlā*; the sixth verse, in giving precedence in inheritance to kinship, left the door of kindness. **In removing a ruling, the sūra leaves the human relationship standing behind it.** I record this as my own reading.

### كَانَ ذَٰلِكَ فِى ٱلْكِتَٰبِ مَسْطُورًا

**مَسْطُور — root س-ط-ر.** *Saṭr* — a line, a row, a sequence. *Masṭūr* — written line by line. From the same root *asṭār* (lines) and *asāṭīr* (what the ancients wrote or said — according to some lexicographers traced to the same root).

**The concrete sense of the word contains an *order*:** a line is not a random piece of writing but writing set in a sequence.

**And the verse ends with this word.** It says that the bonds established are not arbitrary, that they are written according to an order. **This agrees with the principle of the sūra:** the difference between what is said with the mouth and what is written on a line was established in the fourth verse.

### What this says to the present

The three sentences of the verse describe three different bonds, and the order of the three gives a measure for today.

**One. The tie of blood does not take the place of the chosen bond; but neither is it erased.** The verse recognises both and gives each its own field. This leaves both the attitude that says "blood is everything" and the attitude that says "we choose our bonds" standing alone.

**Two. Kindness can be done without a legal obligation.** The last sentence of the verse does not leave empty the field that lies outside an order — it leaves that field to *maʿrūf*. **The law does not close off the field of ethics.**

---

## 33/7-8 — وَإِذْ أَخَذْنَا مِنَ ٱلنَّبِيِّـۧنَ مِيثَٰقَهُمْ وَمِنكَ وَمِن نُّوحٍ وَإِبْرَٰهِيمَ وَمُوسَىٰ وَعِيسَى ٱبْنِ مَرْيَمَ وَأَخَذْنَا مِنْهُم مِّيثَٰقًا غَلِيظًا · لِّيَسْـَٔلَ ٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ عَن صِدْقِهِمْ وَأَعَدَّ لِلْكَٰفِرِينَ عَذَابًا أَلِيمًا

*Wa idh akhadhnā mina'l-nabiyyīna mīthāqahum wa minka wa min Nūḥin wa Ibrāhīma wa Mūsā wa ʿĪsa'bni Maryam, wa akhadhnā minhum mīthāqan ghalīẓā · Li-yasʾala'l-ṣādiqīna ʿan ṣidqihim, wa aʿadda li'l-kāfirīna ʿadhāban alīmā*

"And when We took from the prophets their pledge, and from you, and from Noah (Nūḥ), Abraham (Ibrāhīm), Moses (Mūsā) and Jesus (ʿĪsā) son of Mary (Maryam). We took from them **a firm pledge**. · So that He may ask the truthful about their truthfulness. And He has prepared for the disbelievers a painful punishment."

### The place where the context seems to shift

On a first reading these two verses look unconnected with the five that precede them. After a regulation about adoption and kinship there is a sudden move to a pledge taken from the prophets.

**The link lies in the concept of "the word given"**, and its function within the flow of the sūra is this:

The fourth and fifth verses said that **the word that comes out of a man's mouth** makes nothing real. The seventh verse shows **when a word is binding**: when the party who takes it is God, and when the word is *ghalīẓ* (heavy, firm).

**That is, the sūra does not devalue the spoken word; it settles when a word is weighty.** I record this as my own reading.

And there is a second link as well: **a pledge given will be recalled in the siege section (verse 15)** — *"they had earlier given God their pledge: that they would not turn their backs."* The seventh verse lays the ground for that reminder. The sūra begins the giving of pledges with the prophets and brings it down to the believers.

### مِيثَٰق — root و-ث-ق

To trust, to be firm, to bind. *Wathīqa* — a document; *withāq* — a bond, a strap; *mīthāq* — an agreement made fast.

**The concrete sense of the root is a *bond*** — and this agrees with the whole web of "bonds" in the sūra: the tie of lineage (4-5), the tie of kinship (6), the tie of a pledge (7).

### مِّيثَٰقًا غَلِيظًا — "a thick word"

**غَلِيظ — root غ-ل-ظ.** Its concrete sense is **thickness, hardness**: what is not thin, what is stiff. *Ghilẓa* — thickness. The Qurʾān uses the word in its concrete sense too: *"a thick chain"*, *"hard-hearted"* (*ghalīẓa'l-qalb* at Āl ʿImrān 3/159).

**The construction occurs in three places in the Qurʾān:**

| Place | For what |
|---|---|
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/21** | Marriage — the pledge women take from men |
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/154** | A pledge taken from a community |
| **al-Aḥzāb 33/7** | The pledge taken from the prophets |

**That these three stand side by side is worth recording.** As was recorded at `065-talak.md`, marriage is one of three things the Qurʾān describes as a "thick word" — and the other two are prophethood and a collective covenant.

**Describing a word as "thick" does this in the language:** it makes the word measurable like an object. A thin word snaps easily; a thick word does not. **That is, the verse depicts bindingness not as a matter of *ethics* but as a matter of *material*.**

### Five names and their order

The verse first uses a general expression (*mina'l-nabiyyīn* — from the prophets), then names five: **you**, Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus.

**There are two points of word order:**

**One. The one addressed is named not in historical order but at the very head.** *Wa minka* — "and from you" — comes before Noah, although Noah is named at the beginning of the chain of prophethood (as was recorded at `071-nuh.md`). **That is, the order is not chronological but rhetorical.** The one being spoken to has been brought forward.

**Two. This group of five recurs in the Qurʾān.** The same five names occur together at al-Shūrā 42/13 as well — there it is said *"He has laid down for you of religion what He enjoined upon Noah, and what We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses and Jesus"*. **In both verses the same five names, in the same frame.** It is common in the exegetical tradition to call this group *ulu'l-ʿazm*; **the extent and the ground of the term are disputed, and no ruling is issued here.**

**And one further note:** Jesus is named here too by the Qurʾān's invariable designation — *ibn Maryam*, "son of Mary". That the Qurʾān always ascribes him to his mother takes on a particular weight when it is read together with the fact that this comes immediately after the discussion of lineage in the fourth and fifth verses. **Lineage is not something the Qurʾān passes over undiscussed in anyone's case.**

### لِّيَسْـَٔلَ ٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ عَن صِدْقِهِمْ — the one who will be asked

**This sentence is the most striking place in the verse, and it is usually passed over quickly.**

The sentence says: a pledge has been taken — **so that the truthful may be asked about their truthfulness.**

**صِدْق — root ص-د-ق.** Truthfulness; and the concrete side of the root is **firmness**: *rumḥun ṣadq* — a firm spear. From the same root *ṣadaqa*, *ṣadāq* (the bridal gift), *taṣdīq*, *ṣiddīq*.

**Note: the one who is asked is the *ṣādiq*.** The verse does not say "so that He may ask the liars about their lies". **Those to whom the questioning is addressed are the truthful.**

**This is a reversal worth recording.** The expected sequence was: a pledge was taken so that those who do not keep it may render account. Instead the verse says that it is **those who keep it** who are asked.

Several explanations are transmitted for this expression in classical exegesis:

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Responsibility for delivering the message** | The prophets will be asked whether they delivered the message |
| **Testimony** | The answer of the truthful constitutes evidence against the other side |
| **The raising of the measure** | Truthfulness is not an arrival but a state that is called to account |

**I record the third explanation particularly, because the word order of the verse supports it:** in the sentence *ṣādiqīn* and *ṣidqihim* occur twice from the same root. That is, the question is framed not as "were you truthful?" but as "what became of your **truthfulness**?" Truthfulness is set down not as an adjective but as a **work** for which an account is rendered.

I record this reading as my own reading; its ground is the twofold repetition of the root.

### Context: the last sentence of the eighth verse

*"And He has prepared for the disbelievers a painful punishment."*

The verse ends at two poles: on one side the truthfulness asked of the truthful, on the other the recompense prepared for those who cover. And **this twofold structure is the herald of the siege section:** from the ninth verse the same two sides will come on stage — those who keep their pledge (23) and those who cover (25).

### What this says to the present

**The weight of a word depends not on who gives it but on what it is given to.**

The verse says that what makes a word "thick" is one of the parties. Put into today's language: the bindingness of an undertaking comes less from the intention of the one who gives it than from **to whom** the undertaking is given.

And the second measure is more concrete: **truthfulness is itself something for which an account will be asked.** The matter does not end when a person counts himself "honest"; the verse says that the honest too will be questioned. **An attribute is not an excuse.**

---

## 33/9 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ ٱذْكُرُوا۟ نِعْمَةَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ إِذْ جَآءَتْكُمْ جُنُودٌ فَأَرْسَلْنَا عَلَيْهِمْ رِيحًا وَجُنُودًا لَّمْ تَرَوْهَا وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ بَصِيرًا

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanu'dhkurū niʿmata'llāhi ʿalaykum idh jāʾatkum junūdun fa-arsalnā ʿalayhim rīḥan wa junūdan lam tarawhā, wa kāna'llāhu bimā taʿmalūna baṣīrā*

"O you who believe! Remember the favour of God upon you: when armies came against you and We sent against them a wind and armies you did not see. God sees what you do."

### The opening of the siege section

From the ninth verse to the twenty-seventh a block of nineteen verses begins. **And the opening word of the block is a command: *udhkurū* — remember.**

This also says what the block will not do: **the event will not be narrated, it will be recalled.** The one addressed already knows the event. The work of the text is not to relate the event but to show what is to be read from it.

Indeed throughout the section no event at all is narrated in sequence. What is related is this: a coming, a wind, the state of the eyes, the state of the hearts, the sentences that were said, the increase of faith, the return in rage.

**That is, the section is not a military but an *inward* record.** I record this as my own reading; its ground is that not a single military movement is described in the section.

### نِعْمَةَ ٱللَّهِ — the naming of it as "favour"

**Root: ن-ع-م.** Softness, ease; *naʿīm*, *niʿma*, *anʿām* (livestock — from their soft tread), *nuʿmā*.

**And the naming of the event as a "favour" has the same structure as what was recorded at `048-fetih.md` for a comparable situation:** the name of a hardship is fixed from its outcome, not from how it looked at the time.

What the addressee lived through was a siege: hunger, cold, fear, the suspicion of betrayal. **The verse calls this a "favour".** And it gives its ground at once: the armies that came were dispersed by a wind that was sent.

**But note: what is named a "favour" is not the outcome alone.** The word order of the verse shows this: *"remember the favour: **when armies came against you**"* — the **coming** too is included in what is to be remembered. Afterwards the sūra will say that this siege sifted the believers (11: *hunālika'btuliya'l-muʾminūn*). **That is, the favour takes in the sifting itself.**

### جُنُود — "armies"

**Root: ج-ن-د.** A thing gathered together, compacted; *jund* — a force gathered. For the concrete sense of the root the lexicographers mention the word *janad* (hard, stony ground); that which has been gathered and hardened.

**The finding recorded at `048-fetih.md` returns here to its source:** *jund* does not mean "soldier"; it covers any kind of force gathered for a purpose — it need not be human. **And this verse is the clearest evidence for that finding in the Qurʾān:** the word occurs twice in the same sentence, and the second is used of **the wind and the unseen ones.**

**The symmetry in the word order is worth recording:**

| | What came | What was sent |
|---|---|---|
| **The word** | *junūd* | *rīḥan wa junūdan* |
| **Visibility** | Seen | *lam tarawhā* — which you did not see |
| **The verb** | *jāʾatkum* — came to you | *arsalnā ʿalayhim* — We sent against them |

**The same word is used on both sides.** The verse does not belittle the force of the other side — it calls that too *junūd*. But it sets against it something else bearing the same name.

### فَأَرْسَلْنَا — the change of subject

The verse begins in the third person with *"remember the favour of God"*, then suddenly moves to the first person plural: *arsalnā* — "We sent".

**This is the *iltifāt* (a shift in the direction of address) so often seen in the Qurʾān.** And its function here is this: to bring forward the agent of the verb. The sentence does not say "a wind blew"; it says "**We sent**".

And at the end of the verse it returns to the third person: *wa kāna'llāhu*. That is, the sentence describes a circle.

### رِيحًا — the singular

**Root: ر-و-ح.** And under this root fall *rīḥ* (wind), *rūḥ* (spirit), *rawḥ* (ease, relief) and *rāʾiḥa* (scent). The common point: **moving, carrying air.**

**That the word comes in the singular is recorded in classical exegesis.** In the Qurʾān the wind comes generally in the **plural** (*riyāḥ*) in contexts of mercy, and generally in the **singular** (*rīḥ*) in contexts of punishment. Examples: *"That He sends the winds as bearers of good news…"* (al-Rūm 30/46 — *riyāḥ*); *"So We sent upon them a howling wind"* (Fuṣṣilat 41/16 — *rīḥan ṣarṣaran*).

**This distinction should not be presented as a rule** — there are exceptions to it in the Qurʾān. But it has been recorded by the lexicographers as a tendency, and here too the singular is used.

**The verse does not give the quality of the wind:** not its force, not its direction, not its duration. **It says only that it was sent.** The reports speak of a cold wind that tore up the tents; **I give this detail with the note "it is transmitted" and do not make it a condition of the verse's meaning.**

### وَجُنُودًا لَّمْ تَرَوْهَا — "armies you did not see"

**This is the place in the verse that must be read most carefully.**

**In classical exegesis it is widely said that this expression means the angels.** This rests on the mention of angels elsewhere in the Qurʾān as an element of help (al-Anfāl 8/9, Āl ʿImrān 3/124-125).

**But the wording of the verse gives no name**, and this must be recorded. The only thing said is that the force sent **was not seen.**

**And the form *lam tarawhā* must be dwelt on:** the verb is not "cannot be seen" (*lā turā*) but "**you did not see**" (*lam taraw*). That is, the expression issues no ontological ruling about the invisibility of the thing; **it says that the one addressed did not see it.**

**This distinction is worth recording, and I give it as my own reading:** the verse says that the person inside the event **cannot see the whole of what is helping him.** The one who lived through the siege saw the wind; he did not see the order that sent the wind.

**And this binds the ninth verse to the tenth:** in the tenth verse it will be said that the eyes swerved. That is, within two verses the sūra touches twice on the matter of seeing — one the unseen help, the other the swerving gaze.

### وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ بَصِيرًا — the verse-ending

**بَصِير — root ب-ص-ر.** To see; and beneath the root lies **discernment** (*baṣīra*).

**The choice of the verse-ending agrees with the verse, and this is a fine example of the Qurʾān's consistency in choosing its verse-endings:** throughout the verse the matter of seeing was treated (*lam tarawhā*), and the verse ends with the attribute **Seeing**. **The one addressed could not see; the verse closes by saying who it is that sees.**

### Historical background

It is widely transmitted in the classical sources that this verse and what follows it came down after a combined siege directed at Medina in the fifth year of the Hijra. The main lines of the account were given in the introductory section of the sūra; **no tribal name, no personal name and no numbers are given.**

**There is a historical observation that can be made independently of the text, and it is worth recording:** the breaking up of coalitions besieging a city is a very familiar fact in military history. Coalitions are formed around a shared aim, but their costs are paid separately. The longer a siege lasts, the higher each component's own cost rises and the lower the value of the shared aim falls. **The parting comes not from the fighting but from the duration.**

This is an observation from the social sciences, not a claim of the verse. **The verse ties the event to a wind and to what is unseen; I am recording here a historical pattern, not dressing the verse in an explanation.** Per the note in `STYLE.md` that "no forced connection is made", I keep the two apart.

---

## 33/10 — إِذْ جَآءُوكُم مِّن فَوْقِكُمْ وَمِنْ أَسْفَلَ مِنكُمْ وَإِذْ زَاغَتِ ٱلْأَبْصَٰرُ وَبَلَغَتِ ٱلْقُلُوبُ ٱلْحَنَاجِرَ وَتَظُنُّونَ بِٱللَّهِ ٱلظُّنُونَا۠

*Idh jāʾūkum min fawqikum wa min asfala minkum wa idh zāghati'l-abṣāru wa balaghati'l-qulūbu'l-ḥanājira wa taẓunnūna bi'llāhi'l-ẓunūnā*

"When they came upon you from above you and from below you; when the eyes swerved and the hearts reached the throats, and you entertained all manner of surmises about God."

### The most concentrated descriptive verse in the sūra

This verse is the shortest and most concentrated place in which the Qurʾān describes a community under siege. There are four clauses, and all four look to the **body**: direction, eye, heart, surmise.

**And the order is worth noting:** it moves from outside inwards. First the place (from where did they come), then the eye (the organ that looks outward), then the heart (the organ within), then the surmise (what is not seen).

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the arrangement of the four clauses.

### مِّن فَوْقِكُمْ وَمِنْ أَسْفَلَ مِنكُمْ — two directions

**فَوْق** — above. **أَسْفَل — root س-ف-ل**: to be below. *Suflā* — that which is lower.

**Naming the two directions together conveys the *completeness* of the siege.** One of the ways in Arabic of saying that a thing comes from every direction is to name two opposite directions — the remaining directions enter by implication.

**In classical exegesis the geographical correspondence of these two directions is dwelt on**, and it is transmitted that the besieging forces approached from two different sides of the city. **I do not enter into detail**; the wording of the verse gives only two directions.

**But there is a linguistic note:** *min fawqikum* is built as a construct, while *min asfala minkum* is built with a separate *min*. The lexicographers record this asymmetry; it has to do with *asfal* being a diptote (a noun that does not take certain inflectional endings). It produces no difference in meaning.

### وَإِذْ زَاغَتِ ٱلْأَبْصَٰرُ — the swerving of the eyes

**زَاغَ — root ز-ي-غ.** To deviate from straight, to swerve, to slip out of place. The Qurʾān uses the root of the heart as well: *"Those in whose hearts is deviation…"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/7 — *zaygh*); *"When they swerved, God made their hearts swerve"* (al-Ṣaff 61/5).

**And the use of the root here for the eye is striking.** The word describes the eye's **loss of focus**: the gaze cannot fix on anything, it slides.

**This is a physiologically accurate description of fear, and it is worth recording.** Under intense threat the gaze loses its steadiness; as the eye tries to scan for the threat it can rest at no point. The verse records this not as a moral fault but as a **state** — indeed in the same verse the believers are not censured, and in the next verse it is said that "they were tested".

**And the choice of the word *abṣār* links up with the verse-ending of the previous verse:** the ninth verse ended with *baṣīr*. **The same root, within two verses, with two different subjects:** God is *baṣīr*, and the *abṣār* of the people swerve.

I record this link as my own reading; its ground is the repetition of the root in two consecutive verses.

### وَبَلَغَتِ ٱلْقُلُوبُ ٱلْحَنَاجِرَ — the hearts reaching the throats

**This expression was treated at `040-gafir.md` at 40/18, and I rely on that treatment.** What was recorded there:

- **حَنَاجِر — singular حَنْجَرَة: the throat.**
- The scene is bodily, and can be compared with the scene of the departing soul that rests on *ḥulqūm* (the gullet) at `056-vakia.md`, 56/83.
- Both scenes point to **the place through which voice and breath pass.**

**Now the real work: the comparison of the two occurrences.** The construction occurs in only two places in the Qurʾān, and the two are entirely different scenes.

| | **Ghāfir 40/18** | **al-Aḥzāb 33/10** |
|---|---|---|
| **Wording** | *al-qulūbu **ladā**'l-ḥanājir* | *balaghati'l-qulūbu'l-ḥanājir* |
| **Particle / verb** | *ladā* — "beside, level with" (a nominal sentence) | *balagha* — "reached, arrived at" (a verbal sentence) |
| **The scene** | **The resurrection** — *yawm al-āzifa* | **A siege** — a historical moment |
| **Time** | Future; not yet happened | Past; happened and being recalled |
| **Those addressed** | Those being warned (*wa andhirhum*) | Those who believe (*yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū*) |
| **The accompanying state** | *kāẓimīn* — swallowing, making no sound | *zāghati'l-abṣār* — the eyes swerving |
| **The outcome** | The reckoning | *ibtilāʾ* and *zilzāl* (11) |

**Three things emerge from this comparison, and I record all three:**

**One. The difference in the verb carries a difference in time.** In Ghāfir a nominal sentence is used (with *ladā*) — a **state** is depicted, a fixed condition. In al-Aḥzāb a verb is used (*balaghat*) — a **movement** is described: the heart rose and arrived there. The resurrection scene is static, the siege scene is in motion.

**Two. The same bodily state comes about from two different causes.** In one the cause is the reckoning itself, in the other a military threat. **And the Qurʾān uses the same expression for both.** The meaning of this is: the Qurʾān describes the fear of the resurrection with a fear a person knows — and it also records an instance of that fear actually lived through in history. **That is, not a metaphor but a scale: two intensities of the same state.**

**Three. What comes afterwards is different.** In Ghāfir the scene is followed by *swallowing* (silence); in al-Aḥzāb it is followed by **speech** — from the twelfth verse onwards the sentences begin. At the resurrection no speech is left; under siege speech multiplies. **And the whole diagnosis of the sūra will be built upon those utterances.**

I record this third finding as my own reading.

### وَتَظُنُّونَ بِٱللَّهِ ٱلظُّنُونَا۠ — "surmises about God"

**Root: ظ-ن-ن.** This root was treated in detail at `048-fetih.md` (48/6, *ẓanna'l-sawʾ*) and at `049-hucurat.md` (49/12, *ijtanibū kathīran mina'l-ẓann*). I rely on that treatment.

What was recorded there: **surmise is the zone between certainty and doubt** — in Arabic it can mean both "to suppose" and (in some contexts) "to know for certain"; the context decides.

**In this verse the word has three features:**

**One. The verb is imperfect (*taẓunnūna*).** The three preceding clauses are perfect (*jāʾūkum*, *zāghat*, *balaghat*); this clause is imperfect. **The word order conveys a continuity:** the coming, the swerving, the reaching are each a moment; the surmising is an inner activity that runs on through those moments.
