**Two. *Al-ẓunūnā* — plural and with the definite article.** "Surmises" — of every kind. **Its coming with the article of definiteness (*al-*) carries the sense of surmises that are known, recognised.** That is, the verse does not enumerate the content of the surmises but assumes that the one addressed knows them.

**Three. The alif at the end of the word.** *Al-ẓunūnā* — there is an alif at the end (likewise *al-Rasūlā* in verse 66 and *al-Sabīlā* in verse 67). **This is one of the places where there is a difference of variant reading:** some of the readers read this alif both in continuous recitation and at a pause, some only at a pause, some not at all. **It does not change the meaning; it is a difference of reading bound up with the harmony of the verse-endings.** In the script of the muṣḥaf the alif is written.

### The most striking aspect of the verse

**The verse records surmises entertained about God — and it does so in a section addressed to those who believe.**

This must be recorded. The twelfth verse will separately relate what the hypocrites said; the tenth verse takes in **everyone inside the address.**

**This attitude of the Qurʾān is consistent, and it was recorded at `002-bakara.md` at 2/214:** there too, at the point where the hardship reaches its limit, it was stated explicitly that the one who asks *"when will God's help come?"* is **the messenger and those who believe with him**, and **the question was not censured.**

**The same thing happens here:** the surmise is counted a fault but is not made a ground for excommunication. Indeed the next verse will name the event a **test** — that is, the state recorded is the sifting itself.

I record this as my own reading: **the Qurʾān depicts the inward faltering of the moment of hardship not as something that takes a person out of faith, but as the place where faith is tried.**

---

## 33/11 — هُنَالِكَ ٱبْتُلِىَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ وَزُلْزِلُوا۟ زِلْزَالًا شَدِيدًا

*Hunālika'btuliya'l-muʾminūna wa zulzilū zilzālan shadīdā*

"There the believers were tested and shaken with a violent shaking."

### A short verse, a heading for a block

A verse of eight words, but it closes the whole description of the preceding verse with **a single name**: this was an *ibtilāʾ*.

### هُنَالِكَ — "there"

The adverb of far demonstration: "there, at that point". *Hunā* (here) → *hunāka* (there) → *hunālika* (right there — the furthest degree).

**That the word conveys place and not time is worth recording.** The verse does not say "then", it says "**there**". And this links up with the spatial description of the preceding verse: those who came from above and from below, a place besieged.

**The lexicographers record that *hunālika* is sometimes used in the Qurʾān in a temporal sense too** (example: *"There every soul will experience what it sent ahead"* — Yūnus 10/30). The two uses combine in this verse: the besieged place and the constricted moment are the same thing.

### ٱبْتُلِىَ — root ب-ل-و

To try, to test; and the concrete sense of the root is **to wear out**: *baliya'l-thawb* — the garment wore out. *Balāʾ* — a trial; *ibtilāʾ* — a testing.

**The "wearing out" sense of the root explains what a test is, and it is worth recording:** to test a thing is to use it until it is worn and to see what holds. A test lays a cost upon the thing tested.

**And that the verb comes in the passive:** *ubtuliya* — "they were tested". The agent is not named. The verse does not explicitly name the one who tests; but in the context of the sūra there is no ambiguity. **The passive form here stresses the position of the one addressed: to be the party tested is not to do something but to undergo something.**

### وَزُلْزِلُوا۟ زِلْزَالًا شَدِيدًا — a shaking

**Root: ز-ل-ز-ل.** A quadriliteral root formed by the repetition of two syllables — in Arabic such reduplicated roots generally convey **a repeated movement**: to shake, to jolt, to move out of place.

**This root was treated as the name of a sūra at `099-zilzal.md`**, and this very verse was already mentioned there as an example: *"Here it is not the earth that is shaken but people — the situation during the siege of the Trench is being described. The root can slide from a physical shaking to a psychological one."*

**And the same verb was treated at `002-bakara.md`, 2/214.** It is essential to rely on that treatment, because the parallel between the two verses is striking.

| | **al-Baqara 2/214** | **al-Aḥzāb 33/11** |
|---|---|---|
| **Who** | *alladhīna khalaw min qablikum* — those before you | *al-muʾminūn* — the addressees themselves |
| **Time** | Past generations; mentioned as an example | The moment the addressee is living through |
| **The preceding state** | *al-baʾsāʾu wa'l-ḍarrāʾ* — poverty and harm | *zāghati'l-abṣār, balaghati'l-qulūb* — eye and heart |
| **The verb** | *zulzilū* | *zulzilū zilzālan shadīdā* |
| **What was said** | *matā naṣru'llāh* — "when will God's help come?" | *taẓunnūna bi'llāhi'l-ẓunūnā* — surmises |
| **The answer** | *alā inna naṣra'llāhi qarīb* | *hunālika'btuliya* — a naming |

**What emerges from this comparison is this, and I record it:**

**Al-Baqara 2/214 was the *theory* of this verse. Al-Aḥzāb 33/11 is the moment in which that theory came about.**

Al-Baqara had said: did you suppose you would enter the Garden without what came upon those before you coming upon you? And it had related that they were shaken. **Al-Aḥzāb uses the same verb of the addressee himself.** What al-Baqara said "will come upon you too" has come.

**And there is a difference, and it too must be recorded:** in al-Baqara the shaking was given **without qualification** (*zulzilū*); in al-Aḥzāb it is reinforced with a cognate accusative and an adjective: *zulzilū **zilzālan shadīdā***.

**مَفْعُول مُطْلَق (the cognate accusative)** — the repetition of the verb's verbal noun in order to reinforce the verb. In English it corresponds to a construction such as "they were shaken with a shaking" or "shaken as never before". **In Arabic this is one of the strongest forms of emphasis.**

And on top of it comes an adjective: **شَدِيد — root ش-د-د**: to bind, to draw tight; from this "violent, harsh".

**That is, the verse gives the shaking with a threefold emphasis:** verb + verbal noun + adjective. The places where the Qurʾān uses all three together are few.

### The function of the verse in the sūra

This verse is the **sentence of diagnosis** in the siege section, and it is what makes everything after it possible.

The tenth verse had described a state. The eleventh verse gives that state a **name**: *ibtilāʾ*. And the moment the name is given, the state ceases to be a fault and becomes a **process**.

**And this agrees with the founding principle of the sūra, but works in the opposite direction.** The fourth verse was saying that a name a person gives does not change reality. Here a name is given and **that name does not change reality, it explains it** — because the party who fixes the name is a different one.

I record this as my own reading.

**One further note on word order:** the verse says *al-muʾminūn*. The next verse will say *al-munāfiqūna wa'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun*. **That is, the sifting is done between these two verses.** The eleventh verse says that the believers were shaken; the twelfth relates what others said. **The same shaking gives two different results** — one is shaken and stands, the other is shaken and speaks.

### What this says to the present

**To be shaken is not to leave faith.**

The verse says this plainly: the party that is shaken is named **al-muʾminūn**. The verse calls them by no other name.

And this comes out at the same place as what was recorded for 2/214 at `002-bakara.md`: **the question itself is not censured.** The Qurʾān depicts the faltering of the moment of hardship not as the opposite of faith but as the **cost** of faith.

**Its practical counterpart is this:** a person's being shaken at a hard moment does not annul what he has carried up to that moment. The measure is not whether there was a shaking; it is what was done after the shaking. The sūra will show this in the twenty-second and twenty-third verses.

---

## 33/12 — وَإِذْ يَقُولُ ٱلْمُنَٰفِقُونَ وَٱلَّذِينَ فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ مَّا وَعَدَنَا ٱللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُۥٓ إِلَّا غُرُورًا

*Wa idh yaqūlu'l-munāfiqūna wa'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun mā waʿadana'llāhu wa rasūluhu illā ghurūrā*

"And when the hypocrites and those in whose hearts is sickness were saying, 'God and His Messenger promised us nothing but delusion.'"

### A note on the description of the hypocrite

**The root ن-ف-ق and the phenomenon of hypocrisy were analysed at `063-munafikun.md`; a long section was also given to it at `002-bakara.md` (2/8-20). I rely on those treatments here and do not repeat them.**

Of what was recorded there, what is needed here:

- **The root is connected with the hidden second exit-hole in the burrow of the field mouse (*nāfiqāʾ*).** *Nifāq*, by the word's own logic, is **the state of having two doors.**
- **The second mouth is not always open; it is broken through only when needed.** Hypocrisy is not a continuous act but a possibility held in reserve.
- **These verses cannot be used today to identify particular persons or groups.** The sūra gives the description of an attribute, and the attribute is by definition not visible from outside.

**What does al-Aḥzāb add to al-Munāfiqūn and al-Baqara?**

Al-Baqara was a psychological description, al-Munāfiqūn a behavioural one. **Al-Aḥzāb gives a *moment*.** It does not describe; it **makes them speak.** From the twelfth verse to the twentieth, sentences are relayed one after another:

| Verse | The saying relayed |
|---|---|
| **12** | *"God and His Messenger promised us nothing but delusion"* |
| **13** | *"O people of Yathrib, there is no place for you to stay, go back"* |
| **13** | *"Our houses are exposed"* |
| **18** | *"Come to us"* |
| **20** | (What passes within them) the supposition that the *aḥzāb* have not gone |

**Five sentences, and all five said inside a siege.** This section of the sūra examines hypocrisy not as a concept but as **what is said under pressure.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the section is built by the technique of direct quotation.

### وَٱلَّذِينَ فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ — the second group

**The verse names two groups**, and the distinction must be recorded: *al-munāfiqūn* and *alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍ.*

In classical exegesis it has been debated whether these are the same group or two different ones:

| View | Its ground |
|---|---|
| **The same group, named twice** | The conjunction sometimes comes to list another attribute of the same thing; both display the same behaviour |
| **Two separate groups** | The proper function of the conjunction is to distinguish; moreover the expression "sickness in the heart" may also cover one who has faith but a weak one |

**I do not impose a preference.** But I record a datum of the text in favour of the second view: **the sūra uses these same two expressions side by side in the sixtieth verse as well, and there it adds a third group** (*al-murjifūn*). When three separate expressions are listed, it becomes a stronger possibility that the distinction is deliberate. This is not a preference but an observation.

**مَرَض — root م-ر-ض.** Sickness. This metaphor was treated at `002-bakara.md` (2/10), and the finding recorded there was this: **for the disbelievers a seal is used, for the hypocrites a sickness.** A seal conveys closure, a sickness conveys **progression** — an untreated illness does not stay where it is.

**And that finding is confirmed in this sūra:** at the moment of the siege the sickness progresses and turns into speech.

### مَّا وَعَدَنَا ٱللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُۥٓ إِلَّا غُرُورًا — the structure of the sentence

**A structure of restriction (*ḥaṣr*):** *mā … illā* — "nothing other than…". The same pattern was treated at `058-mucadele.md`.

**And the pattern does a particularly hard piece of work here.** The sentence does not say "the promise was not fulfilled"; it says the promise **was nothing other**. That is, what is denied is not the content of the promise but its **nature**.

**غُرُور — root غ-ر-ر.** And the concrete sense of the root must be dwelt on: **to be deceived, to be heedless**; and *ghirra* — inattention, heedlessness. *Gharra* — he deceived someone by exploiting his heedlessness.

**The concrete side of the root:** the lexicographers record that the word *ghirr* is used for "inexperienced, green" — one who has not yet learned to be deceived.

**The Qurʾān uses the word in a characteristic place:** *"The life of this world is nothing but the goods of delusion"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/185 — *matāʿu'l-ghurūr*); *"Let the great deceiver (*al-gharūr*) not deceive you about God"* (Luqmān 31/33).

**And there is a reversal here, worth recording:** the Qurʾān generally describes **this world** as deluding. In this sentence the same word is used of **what has been promised.** That is, those speaking turn the measure the Qurʾān sets exactly upside down: for them the deceiving party is the unseen promise, and the real party is the tangible situation.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the contrast between the word's general use in the Qurʾān and its use here.

### The matter of the promise

**There is a concrete expectation behind the sentence**, and this is needed in order to understand the verse.

The sources transmit that a promise of victory or of expansion was voiced before and during the siege. **I do not enter into detail.** But the logic of the sentence needs no detail: there is a promise, the siege appears to show its opposite, and those speaking draw a conclusion from that appearance.

**And this is, as a matter of logic, an error, and the error has a name: *the hasty verdict*.** The situation in the middle of a process is read as the outcome of the process. The sūra will give the outcome in the twenty-fifth verse: the besiegers turned back in their rage.

**But what the sūra does is not merely to say "they were wrong".** In the twenty-second verse it will show that other people looking at the same scene drew **exactly the opposite** conclusion: *"This is what God and His Messenger promised us."*

**Two groups see the same thing. One says "delusion", the other says "promise".** And in both sentences the same word is used: **وَعَدَ**.

| | **Verse 12** | **Verse 22** |
|---|---|---|
| **Who** | The hypocrites and those with sickness in their hearts | The believers |
| **What they saw** | The same siege | The same siege |
| **What they said** | *mā waʿadana'llāhu… illā ghurūrā* | *hādhā mā waʿadana'llāhu wa rasūluh* |
| **The outcome** | — | *wa mā zādahum illā īmānan wa taslīmā* |

**This is the sharpest contrast the sūra sets up, and it is the spine of the whole siege section.** The ten verses between the two fill in the space between these two sentences.

**And what must be recorded is this: the difference is not one of knowledge.** Both sides know the same thing, see the same scene, live through the same danger. The difference lies in **the conclusion drawn from the same data.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two verses use the same verb (*waʿada*).

### What this says to the present

**The worst moment in the middle of a process is not the outcome of the process.**

The error the verse records is as simple as that, and as familiar today. An assessment made at a moment of difficulty carries the pressure of that moment; and that pressure distorts the scale.

**The real nicety in the verse's sentence is that those speaking are not lying.** What they say fits their observation as of that moment: there is a siege, and the promise is nowhere in sight. **The error lies not in the observation but in the haste to draw a verdict from the observation.**

---

## 33/13 — وَإِذْ قَالَت طَّآئِفَةٌ مِّنْهُمْ يَٰٓأَهْلَ يَثْرِبَ لَا مُقَامَ لَكُمْ فَٱرْجِعُوا۟ وَيَسْتَـْٔذِنُ فَرِيقٌ مِّنْهُمُ ٱلنَّبِىَّ يَقُولُونَ إِنَّ بُيُوتَنَا عَوْرَةٌ وَمَا هِىَ بِعَوْرَةٍ إِن يُرِيدُونَ إِلَّا فِرَارًا

*Wa idh qālat ṭāʾifatun minhum yā ahla Yathriba lā muqāma lakum fa'rjiʿū, wa yastaʾdhinu farīqun minhumu'l-nabiyya yaqūlūna inna buyūtanā ʿawratun wa mā hiya bi-ʿawra, in yurīdūna illā firārā*

"And when a party of them said, 'O people of Yathrib! There is no place for you to stay, go back.' And another company of them was asking leave of the Prophet, saying, 'Our houses are exposed.' They were not exposed; they wanted only to flee."

### The anatomy of an excuse

**This verse is one of the places where the Qurʾān lays bare most clearly the manufacture of excuses**, and it deserves to be dwelt on in detail.

There are **two separate groups** and **two separate tactics** in the verse:

| | **The first group (*ṭāʾifa*)** | **The second group (*farīq*)** |
|---|---|---|
| **What it does** | Calls out to others | Asks leave of the Prophet |
| **Its address** | *yā ahla Yathrib* | — |
| **Its sentence** | *lā muqāma lakum* — "there is no place" | *inna buyūtanā ʿawratun* — "our houses are open" |
| **Its ground** | An assessment of the situation | Personal necessity |
| **The verse's answer** | — (passed over in silence) | *wa mā hiya bi-ʿawra* — an open refusal |
| **The diagnosis** | — | *in yurīdūna illā firārā* |

**That the two groups are named with two different words must be recorded.**

**طَآئِفَة — root ط-و-ف.** To go round something (*ṭawāf* is from the same root). *Ṭāʾifa* — a cluster that circles round a thing; from this, "a band of people". **In the root of the word there is *circulating*** — not fixed, but in motion.

**فَرِيق — root ف-ر-ق.** To separate, to divide. *Farīq* — a company that has split off; *firqa*, *furqān* are from the same root. **In the root of the word there is *separating*.**

**And the distribution of the two words over the two groups agrees with the sense of the verse:** one circulates about and speaks (*ṭāʾifa*), the other asks leave in order to separate itself from the community (*farīq*). **The words announce the behaviours in advance.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the concrete senses of the two roots.

### يَٰٓأَهْلَ يَثْرِبَ — the use of the name

**This is the only place in the Qurʾān where the name "Yathrib" occurs**, and whose mouth it comes from matters: **those calling for flight.**

The city's name in the Qurʾān is *al-Madīna*, and it occurs under that name in the sixtieth verse of this sūra. **In the same sūra, the same city, two different names.**

**Does this mean anything?** Classical exegesis draws attention to the distinction. I keep within what the wording gives and record this as a **datum of the text**: the sūra names the city by its old name in the mouths of those calling for flight, and as *al-Madīna* in its own narration.

**Beyond this I frame no ruling** — there is no negative judgement about the name Yathrib itself in the wording of the verse.

**A linguistic note:** the word *ahl* means "the inhabitants of a place, the possessors of a thing", and in the thirty-third verse of this sūra it will come again in an entirely different construction: *ahl al-bayt*. The same root, an entirely different extent.

### لَا مُقَامَ لَكُمْ — "there is no place to stay"

**مُقَام — root ق-و-م.** To rise, to stand, to be fixed.

**And here there is a difference of variant reading, and it changes the meaning:**

| Reading | Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **مُقَام** (*muqām*, with *ḍamma*) | Noun of place / mīmī verbal noun, from Form IV (*iqāma*) | "There is no place or possibility of residing" — of staying on |
| **مَقَام** (*maqām*, with *fatḥa*) | Noun of place, from the bare root (*qiyām*) | "There is no place to stand" — a place of standing upright |

**Both readings are found among the readers.** The difference in sense is fine, and both fit the context: one says "there is no staying here", the other "there is no holding on here". **I do not impose a preference.**

**The rhetorical force of the sentence lies here:** the negative particle *lā*, at the head of a nominal sentence, conveys **the negation of the genus** (the *lā* of generic negation) — "no place of any kind". That is, the sentence claims not a possibility but a **certainty**.

**And the verse gives no answer to this sentence.** While the second group is answered (*wa mā hiya bi-ʿawra*), the first is not. **This is a fact of word order and can be read in two ways:** either the falsity of the sentence will be exposed by the event itself (verse 25), or the verse makes its real diagnosis by way of the second group. **I do not speak with certainty here.**

### إِنَّ بُيُوتَنَا عَوْرَةٌ — the word of the excuse

**This is the most striking word in the verse.**

**عَوْرَة — root ع-و-ر.** And the semantic cluster of the root is wide; what joins all of it is **a lack, an opening, an absence of protection**:

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **أَعْوَر** (*aʿwar*) | Blind in one eye — lacking on one side |
| **عَارِيَة** (*ʿāriya*) | A thing borrowed — not permanently owned |
| **عَوْرَة** (*ʿawra*) | What must be covered, what must not be left exposed; and the unprotected, open side of a structure |
| **عَوَار** (*ʿawār*) | A flaw, a defect in goods |

**The sense of the word in this verse is concrete:** the houses are undefended — their walls low, their doors weak, or unprotected in the layout of the settlement.

**The root occurs only here in this sūra.** In Sūrat al-Nūr, by contrast (24/31 and 24/58), it occurs in the context of privacy; there too the same family of senses is at work: what must be covered, what must not be left exposed.

**And the choice of the word constitutes the force of the excuse.** Those speaking do not say "we are afraid". **They put their families forward.** Because the word *ʿawra* carries both defencelessness and privacy at once, the excuse takes on a moral covering: "we are thinking not of ourselves but of those we have left behind."

**This is the most important thing that can be said about the anatomy of an excuse, and I record it:** a good excuse makes the speaker look like the protector not of his own interest but of someone else.

The finding recorded at `048-fetih.md` for the excuse at 48/11 (*shaghalatnā amwālunā wa ahlūnā* — "our possessions and our families kept us busy") — that the excuse makes the person passive — goes one step further here: **here the excuse does not merely make the person passive, it makes him look *self-sacrificing*.**

### وَمَا هِىَ بِعَوْرَةٍ — the form of the refusal

The verse refuses the excuse **directly**, and the language of the refusal is striking.

**The structure مَا … بِـ:** in the Ḥijāzī dialect the *mā* that governs like *laysa* is reinforced by taking a *bāʾ* at the head of its predicate. The same structure (*mā hunna ummahātihim*) was treated at `058-mucadele.md`.

**And the parallel between the verses is worth recording:**

| Place | The sentence | What is denied |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Mujādala 58/2** | *mā hunna ummahātihim* | The claim "my wife is my mother" |
| **al-Aḥzāb 33/4** | *mā jaʿala… azwājakum… ummahātikum* | The same claim, with the agent named |
| **al-Aḥzāb 33/13** | *wa mā hiya bi-ʿawra* | The claim "my house is exposed" |

**All three do the same work: they annul a reality established by speech with a statement of fact.** And all three are in the same sūra or in the same family of subjects.

**This is the application of the sūra's founding principle within the siege section**, and I record it: the fourth verse had said "speech does not establish reality"; the thirteenth applies the same principle to an excuse. **An excuse too is a piece of speech, and it too stays in the mouth.**

### إِن يُرِيدُونَ إِلَّا فِرَارًا — the diagnosis

Again a structure of *ḥaṣr*: *in … illā*. "They want nothing but to flee."

**فِرَار — root ف-ر-ر.** To flee. On the concrete sense of the root the lexicographers record this: *farra'l-dābba* — he examined the animal's teeth (he opened its mouth to tell its age). From this comes the sense "to bring to light", and its link with fleeing is that the one who flees exposes himself.

**This derivation is not certain; some lexicographers assign the two senses to separate roots. I record it but build no meaning upon it.**

**What the sentence does:** the verse names the desire behind the excuse. **And that it is able to do so comes from the distinction the sūra has been drawing from the outset:** the word that leaves the mouth and the intention in the heart are different things (verse 5: *mā taʿammadat qulūbukum*).

**That is, the measure laid down in the fifth verse — liability lies in the intention of the heart — turns in the thirteenth verse into an instrument of diagnosis.** I record this as my own reading.

### What this says to the present

**Excuses have a recognisable structure, and the verse shows that structure.**

There are three elements:

**One. An excuse rests on something that could be true.** The houses really could have been undefended. The force of an excuse comes not from its not being a lie but from **its standing on verifiable ground.**

**Two. An excuse frees a person not from responsibility but from choice.** Saying "I am fleeing" is a choice; saying "my house is exposed" is a necessity. **The same act becomes two different things in two different sentences.**

**Three. An excuse puts someone else forward.** He is asking not for himself but for his family.

**And the verse's answer annuls all three elements with a single sentence:** *wa mā hiya bi-ʿawra.* Once the ground is refuted, the structure falls.

**One further note, per the method (`STYLE.md`):** the verse does not say **who** it was that spoke this sentence — it gives no name, no tribe. And as recorded at `063-munafikun.md` this is a deliberate choice: **the description lasts, the exposure passes.** This verse cannot be used today to identify anyone; it can only be read in order to recognise the anatomy of an excuse — **and one's own excuses first.**

---

## 33/14-15 — وَلَوْ دُخِلَتْ عَلَيْهِم مِّنْ أَقْطَارِهَا ثُمَّ سُئِلُوا۟ ٱلْفِتْنَةَ لَءَاتَوْهَا وَمَا تَلَبَّثُوا۟ بِهَآ إِلَّا يَسِيرًا · وَلَقَدْ كَانُوا۟ عَٰهَدُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ مِن قَبْلُ لَا يُوَلُّونَ ٱلْأَدْبَٰرَ وَكَانَ عَهْدُ ٱللَّهِ مَسْـُٔولًا

*Wa law dukhilat ʿalayhim min aqṭārihā thumma suʾilu'l-fitnata la-ātawhā wa mā talabbathū bihā illā yasīrā · Wa laqad kānū ʿāhadu'llāha min qablu lā yuwallūna'l-adbār, wa kāna ʿahdu'llāhi masʾūlā*

"If the city had been entered upon them from all its sides and they had then been asked for *fitna*, they would surely have done it, and would have delayed over it but little. · Yet they had earlier given God their pledge: that they would not turn their backs. And the pledge given to God is asked about."

### The fourteenth verse: a supposition

The verse sets up a **hypothetical condition**: *law* — the particle of a condition that did not come about. This particle was treated at `049-hucurat.md` 49/5: *law* generally conveys a condition that has not come about.

**And the function of the condition is worth recording:** by supposing something that did not happen, the verse measures what a group was ready for. This is not an accusation; it is **a test of durability.**

**أَقْطَار — singular قُطْر. Root: ق-ط-ر.** The edge, the side, the region of a thing. *Quṭr* — a diameter, the line between two opposite points of a circle; and one side of a place. *"If you can pass beyond the *aqṭār* of the heavens and the earth, then pass"* (al-Raḥmān 55/33) — there too the same word means "the sides, the limits", and it was treated at `055-rahman.md`.

**دُخِلَتْ — passive.** "Had been entered". The agent is not named; the verse does not make it its subject who would enter.

**ٱلْفِتْنَة — root ف-ت-ن.** The concrete sense of the root is **to melt metal in fire and separate out what is pure**: *fatana'l-dhahab* — he put the gold into the fire. From this come both the sense "a trial" and the sense "turmoil, sedition".

**The sense of the word in this verse has been debated in classical exegesis:**

| Explanation | What is meant |
|---|---|
| **Turning back from religion** | If they had been asked to give up faith |
| **Civil war / fighting against the community** | If they had been asked to change sides against their own community |
| **General sedition** | If they had been asked to take part in any work of disorder |

**I do not impose a preference.** The wording of the verse gives the word with the definite article (*al-fitna*), that is, it points to something the addressee knows; but it does not open up its content.

**وَمَا تَلَبَّثُوا۟ بِهَآ إِلَّا يَسِيرًا** — **تَلَبَّثَ, root ل-ب-ث**: to stay in a place, to linger, to wait. *Yasīr* — little, easy.

**The sentence says: they would not have delayed.** That is, the matter is not one of putting up a resistance and giving way in the end; it is **the absence of any resistance at all.**

**And this completes the diagnosis of the thirteenth verse:** there the intention behind the excuse was determined (to flee); here the depth of that intention is measured.

### The fifteenth verse: the pledge recalled

**وَلَقَدْ كَانُوا۟ عَٰهَدُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ مِن قَبْلُ** — "they had earlier given God their pledge".

**عَهْد — root ع-ه-د.** To turn back to a thing again and again, to watch over it, not to neglect it. *Taʿāhada* — he inspected a thing regularly. **The concrete sense of the root is a *continuity***, and from this comes the sense "covenant, agreement": a pledge is not something said once and left, but something continually watched over.

**And this joins with the note at the end of the verse:** *wa kāna ʿahdu'llāhi masʾūlā* — "the pledge given to God is asked about." **The root itself was already saying that it would be asked about:** an *ʿahd* is a thing watched over.

**The link with the seventh verse:** at the head of the sūra a *mīthāqan ghalīẓā* had been taken from the prophets, and its ground was *"so that the truthful may be asked about their truthfulness"*. **In the fifteenth verse the same structure is repeated for ordinary people:** a pledge has been given and will be asked about.

**That is, the sūra begins the idea of a covenant with the prophets and brings it down to the community.** I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two verses set up the same frame (pledge + being asked).

### لَا يُوَلُّونَ ٱلْأَدْبَٰرَ — the content of the pledge given

**يُوَلُّونَ — root و-ل-ي.** It meets us for the third time in this sūra (*mawālī* in verse 5, *awlā* in verse 6, *yuwallūn* here).

**And the "turning towards" sense of the root works here in reverse:** *wallā*, in Form II, means to turn one's back, to turn away. **That the root goes in two directions — both approaching and departing — is seen in three separate words in this sūra.**

| Verse | Word | Direction |
|---|---|---|
| **5** | *mawālīkum* | Nearness — a bond |
| **6** | *awlā* | Nearness — precedence |
| **15** | *yuwallūna'l-adbār* | Departure — turning the back |

I record this web as my own reading; its ground is that the three words are from the same root.

**ٱلْأَدْبَٰر — singular دُبُر. Root: د-ب-ر.** The back, the end of a thing. *Tadbīr* is from the same root: to think of the **back** (the outcome) of a matter. This root was treated at `047-muhammed.md`.

**The construction is a recurring pattern in the Qurʾān in the context of war:** *"Whoever turns his back that day…"* (al-Anfāl 8/16).

### وَكَانَ عَهْدُ ٱللَّهِ مَسْـُٔولًا — the verse-ending

**مَسْـُٔول — root س-أ-ل.** That which is asked about. And that the word comes in the passive participle is worth noting: **what will be asked about is the *pledge*.** Not the person.

**This is a point of word order and it carries meaning:** in Arabic it could equally have been said "they will be asked about it". Instead the verse makes the pledge the subject. **The pledge becomes an object called to account.**

This pattern is used elsewhere in the Qurʾān too: *"And fulfil the covenant; the covenant will be asked about"* (al-Isrāʾ 17/34 — *inna'l-ʿahda kāna masʾūlā*). **The same structure, the same word.**

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the fourteenth verse is uncomfortable and true: a person's durability is measured not by the size of the pressure he has met, but by looking one step beyond it.**

The verse supposes a situation that did not come about and says what would have happened under that supposition. **That is, the measure lies not in what was lived through but in what could be lived through.**

**This is something a person cannot know about himself**, and the verse gives it not as a warning but as a record. No one knows what he will do under the next pressure; the only thing known is what pledge was given.

**And the measure of the fifteenth verse comes out of this:** durability cannot be measured, but **the pledge is on record.** And that is what will be asked about.

---

## 33/16-17 — قُل لَّن يَنفَعَكُمُ ٱلْفِرَارُ إِن فَرَرْتُم مِّنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ أَوِ ٱلْقَتْلِ وَإِذًا لَّا تُمَتَّعُونَ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا · قُلْ مَن ذَا ٱلَّذِى يَعْصِمُكُم مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ إِنْ أَرَادَ بِكُمْ سُوٓءًا أَوْ أَرَادَ بِكُمْ رَحْمَةً وَلَا يَجِدُونَ لَهُم مِّن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ وَلِيًّا وَلَا نَصِيرًا

*Qul lan yanfaʿakumu'l-firāru in farartum mina'l-mawti awi'l-qatli wa idhan lā tumattaʿūna illā qalīlā · Qul man dha'lladhī yaʿṣimukum mina'llāhi in arāda bikum sūʾan aw arāda bikum raḥma, wa lā yajidūna lahum min dūni'llāhi waliyyan wa lā naṣīrā*

"Say: If you flee from death or from being killed, fleeing will not profit you; and then you will be given enjoyment but a little. · Say: Who is it that can protect you from God if He wills harm for you, or if He wills mercy for you? They will find for themselves, apart from God, neither friend nor helper."

### The arithmetic of flight

In the thirteenth verse the diagnosis had been made: *in yurīdūna illā firārā*. The sixteenth verse takes the same word and does a piece of arithmetic: **does fleeing work?**

**And the verse's answer is not moral but arithmetical.** It does not say "fleeing is bad". It says "**it will not profit you**".

**نَفَعَ — root ن-ف-ع.** To yield benefit; the opposite of *ḍarar* (ض-ر-ر), harm.

**لَّن** — the particle that negates the future absolutely: "never".

### إِن فَرَرْتُم مِّنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ أَوِ ٱلْقَتْلِ — two separate things

**The verse uses two words, and the two are different:**

| Word | Root | What |
|---|---|---|
| **ٱلْمَوْت** | م-و-ت | Death — an end that comes of itself |
| **ٱلْقَتْل** | ق-ت-ل | Being killed — the act of an agent |

**The function of the distinction is this:** the one who flees is fleeing the second. By setting the two side by side the verse says that what is being fled from is not a **kind** but an **end**. Fleeing the second does not remove the first.

**وَإِذًا لَّا تُمَتَّعُونَ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا** — **مُتْعَة / تَمْتِيع, root م-ت-ع**: to derive benefit from a thing; and *matāʿ* — goods enjoyed temporarily.

**The root already contains *transience*** — and the verse adds to it *qalīlā* (a little). **That is, what flight gains is a small quantity of something that is transient in the very root of the word.**

**The sentence draws up an account, and I record it:** what is obtained by flight is, at best, a slight prolongation of something already transient. The verse does not forbid flight; **it calculates its profit.**

### مَن ذَا ٱلَّذِى يَعْصِمُكُم — the interrogative pattern

**مَن ذَا ٱلَّذِى** — a strong interrogative pattern in Arabic; "who is it that…" **It is used for questions that have no answer** (the rhetorical interrogative).

**عَصَمَ — root ع-ص-م.** And the concrete sense of the root is **to hold, to grip fast**: *iʿtaṣama bi'l-ḥabl* — he clung to the rope. *ʿIṣma* — being protected. *Maʿṣūm* — the one protected.

**The concrete sense of the root builds the picture of the verse:** the verse does not say "who will defend you"; it says "**who will hold you**". The one who flees is going from one place to another; the verse looks for a hand that will hold him.

### إِنْ أَرَادَ بِكُمْ سُوٓءًا أَوْ أَرَادَ بِكُمْ رَحْمَةً — the breaking of the symmetry

**This is the finest point in the verse, and it is usually passed over quickly.**

The sentence names two possibilities: harm and mercy. **But the question fits only one of them.** The question "who will protect you from Him?" makes sense for harm; for mercy it makes none — nobody wants to be protected from mercy.

**Several explanations are given for this asymmetry in classical exegesis:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **The mention of the opposite (*muqābala*)** | In Arabic the opposite of a thing is named in order to complete the picture; the ruling belongs to the first |
| **The second verb attached to an implied question** | "Or if He wills mercy for you, who can prevent it?" — the question is implicitly repeated |
| **The widening of the scope** | The sentence means "nothing can decide apart from Him"; by naming the two extremes the whole field is closed |

**The third explanation is supported by the sentence at the end of the verse:** *"They will find for themselves, apart from God, neither friend nor helper."* Two more words: *walī* and *naṣīr*.

**I do not impose a preference.** But I record this: **the naming of the second possibility (mercy) changes the tone of the verse.** If the sentence had said only "you cannot escape", it would have been a threat. With the addition of mercy the sentence turns into a statement of **scope**: flight is a flight from the good as much as from the bad.

I record this reading as my own reading.

**وَلِيًّا وَلَا نَصِيرًا** — again the root و-ل-ي (for the fourth time in this sūra) and the root **ن-ص-ر**. The difference between the two: *walī* is protection born of nearness, *naṣīr* is help in act. **One a position, the other an action.**

### What this says to the present

**The verse gives flight not a moral but a calculated answer**, and this is a method worth recording.

There are two ways of keeping a person in a hard place: to say that fleeing is bad, or to show that fleeing does not work. **The verse chooses the second.**

And its ground stands in the verse's own logic: **flight carries an assumption about *where* the thing fled from is.** The person assumes that the danger is in one place and not in another. The verse removes that assumption.

**Its present-day form is this:** what is gained by fleeing a difficulty is almost always measurable and almost always small — *illā qalīlā*. Flight does not solve the matter; **it postpones it and raises its cost.**

---

## 33/18-19 — قَدْ يَعْلَمُ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُعَوِّقِينَ مِنكُمْ وَٱلْقَآئِلِينَ لِإِخْوَٰنِهِمْ هَلُمَّ إِلَيْنَا وَلَا يَأْتُونَ ٱلْبَأْسَ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا · أَشِحَّةً عَلَيْكُمْ فَإِذَا جَآءَ ٱلْخَوْفُ رَأَيْتَهُمْ يَنظُرُونَ إِلَيْكَ تَدُورُ أَعْيُنُهُمْ كَٱلَّذِى يُغْشَىٰ عَلَيْهِ مِنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ فَإِذَا ذَهَبَ ٱلْخَوْفُ سَلَقُوكُم بِأَلْسِنَةٍ حِدَادٍ أَشِحَّةً عَلَى ٱلْخَيْرِ أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ لَمْ يُؤْمِنُوا۟ فَأَحْبَطَ ٱللَّهُ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ وَكَانَ ذَٰلِكَ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ يَسِيرًا

*Qad yaʿlamu'llāhu'l-muʿawwiqīna minkum wa'l-qāʾilīna li-ikhwānihim halumma ilaynā, wa lā yaʾtūna'l-baʾsa illā qalīlā · Ashiḥḥatan ʿalaykum, fa-idhā jāʾa'l-khawfu raʾaytahum yanẓurūna ilayka tadūru aʿyunuhum ka'lladhī yughshā ʿalayhi mina'l-mawt, fa-idhā dhahaba'l-khawfu salaqūkum bi-alsinatin ḥidādin ashiḥḥatan ʿala'l-khayr, ulāʾika lam yuʾminū fa-aḥbaṭa'llāhu aʿmālahum, wa kāna dhālika ʿala'llāhi yasīrā*

"God knows those among you who hold others back and those who say to their brothers, 'come to us'. They come to the fighting but little. · They are grudging towards you. When fear comes you see them looking at you, their eyes rolling like one overcome by the swoon of death. And when the fear has gone they lash you with sharp tongues, grudging of any good. Those have not believed, and God has brought their deeds to nothing. That is easy for God."

### The most detailed description of behaviour in the sūra

These two verses are the psychological summit of the siege section. And what is striking is this: **the verse does not conduct an interrogation of belief; it records a series of behaviours.**

Five behaviours are enumerated, and all five are observable:

1. Holding others back (*muʿawwiqīn*).
2. Calling their brothers (*halumma ilaynā*).
3. Coming to the fighting but little.
4. Freezing and staring at the moment of fear.
5. Speaking with a sharp tongue once the fear has passed.

**At the end there is a ruling (*lam yuʾminū*), but the road leading to the ruling is entirely behavioural.** I record this as my own reading.

### ٱلْمُعَوِّقِينَ — those who hold back

**Root: ع-و-ق.** To delay a matter, to obstruct, to hold back. *ʿĀʾiqa* — an obstacle; *taʿawwuq* — delay.

**Pattern: the active participle of Form II (*muʿawwiq*).** This form generally conveys **intensity and repetition.** That is, the word does not mean "one who obstructs once" but **one who is continually occupied with the work of obstructing.**

**مِنكُمْ — "from among you".** **This note is important and, per the method (`STYLE.md`), must be stressed:** the verse says that those who hold back are **from within the community.** That is, not a threat coming from outside; a mechanism working from within.

**And precisely for that reason these verses cannot be used as an instrument of identification.** What is from within is not visible from without. The measure recorded at `063-munafikun.md` holds here too.

### هَلُمَّ إِلَيْنَا — the sentence of the call

**هَلُمَّ** is in Arabic a **noun-verb** (a word carrying verbal meaning but not inflected like a verb): "come, come here". In the Ḥijāzī dialect it is used without distinction of singular and plural; in the Tamīmī dialect it is inflected.

**The choice of the word is worth noting:** short, warm, inviting. Not a command but a call.

**And those the call addresses: *li-ikhwānihim* — "to their brothers".** The verse records that those calling use the language of brotherhood.

**This shows how the holding back works, and is worth recording:** no one says "leave it, run". What is said is "come to us". **That is, the holding back is presented not as a call to abandon but as a call to *join*.** The person is not leaving a place; he is being invited to another.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the direction of the verb (*ilaynā* — towards us, not away from him).

### ٱلْبَأْس — "the fighting"

**Root: ب-أ-س.** Violence, hardship, difficulty. It occurred at `002-bakara.md` 2/214 as *al-baʾsāʾ* (poverty, hardship coming from outside) and was treated there. *Baʾs* — the violence of war; *baʾīs* — severe.

**Using this word in place of "war" names war not as an event but as a *violence*.**

### أَشِحَّةً عَلَيْكُمْ — root ش-ح-ح

**This word is the centre of the verse, and it occurs twice.**

**Root: ش-ح-ح.** And the sense of the root is something sharper than the English "miserliness". The definition the lexicographers record is this: **shuḥḥ is miserliness combined with greed** — that is, both not giving what one has and wanting what another has.

| Word | The difference |
|---|---|
| **بُخْل** (*bukhl*) | Not giving what one has |
| **شُحّ** (*shuḥḥ*) | Not giving what one has **+** wanting what another has; and this becoming a settled trait |

**In the classical dictionaries the difference of *shuḥḥ* from *bukhl* is explained by its having become a *disposition*.** *Bukhl* is a behaviour, *shuḥḥ* a character.

**Its other occurrences in the Qurʾān show the weight of the word:**

- *"And whoever is guarded against the *shuḥḥ* of his own soul — it is they who attain deliverance"* — **وَمَن يُوقَ شُحَّ نَفْسِهِۦ فَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُفْلِحُونَ** (al-Ḥashr 59/9; al-Taghābun 64/16). **This verse was treated at `059-hasr.md` and at `064-tegabun.md`.**
- *"Souls have been made ready for *shuḥḥ*"* (al-Nisāʾ 4/128 — *wa uḥḍirati'l-anfusu'l-shuḥḥ*).

**And in this verse the word occurs *twice*, with two different objects:**

| Place | The construction | Grudging towards what |
|---|---|---|
| **The beginning of verse 19** | *ashiḥḥatan **ʿalaykum*** | Towards you — not joining, not lending support |
| **The end of verse 19** | *ashiḥḥatan **ʿala'l-khayr*** | Towards the good — in the context of asking for a share of the spoils |

**The same word frames the verse from its two ends.** And what lies between? **The coming and the going of fear.**

**This is the most elegant structure in the verse, and I record it:** the *shuḥḥ* does not change; the only thing that changes is the presence of fear. When fear comes the *shuḥḥ* appears as freezing, when fear goes it appears as aggressive speech. **That is, the verse says that two different behaviours come out of a single disposition.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the repetition of the word at the two ends of the verse.

**Note:** the root ش-ح-ح had not been treated in detail earlier in this commentary; it is opened up here. The word had been touched on in the contexts of al-Ḥashr 59/9 and al-Taghābun 64/16.

### تَدُورُ أَعْيُنُهُمْ — the rolling of the eyes

**دَارَ — root د-و-ر.** To turn, to go round. *Dāʾira*, *dawr*, *dār* (a house — an enclosed place) are from the same root.

**And the link of this description with the tenth verse is direct:**

| Verse | Who | The eye |
|---|---|---|
| **33/10** | Everyone under siege, believers included | *zāghati'l-abṣār* — the eyes swerved |
| **33/19** | The *muʿawwiqīn* | *tadūru aʿyunuhum* — their eyes roll |

**Both verbs describe the eye's loss of focus. But there is a difference, and the difference must be recorded:**

- *Zāgha* — **it deviated, it slid.** A single swerve.
- *Dāra* — **it turned, it went round.** A continuous, circular movement.

**And the verse states the second difference openly: *yanẓurūna ilayka*** — "they are looking at you". **In the tenth verse the gaze had no direction; here it has one: turned towards the Prophet.**

**That is, the same physiological state describes two different things in two different places.** In the tenth verse, fear itself; here, **an expectation inside the fear.** The gaze is looking for deliverance in a person.

I record this distinction as my own reading; its ground is the difference between the two verbs (*zāgha* / *dāra*) and the note *ilayka*.

### كَٱلَّذِى يُغْشَىٰ عَلَيْهِ مِنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ — the comparison

**The root غ-ش-ي** was treated at `092-leyl.md`, and this verse was already mentioned there as an example: *"Besides the night's covering, the Qurʾān uses this root for the overspreading of fear and of the loss of consciousness."* I rely on that treatment.

The concrete sense of the root is **to cover, to overspread**. *Ghishāwa* — a veil. *Yughshā ʿalayh* — a covering came over him; that is, **he fainted.**

**The picture of the comparison:** a person taken by the swoon of death. The eyes open, but not seeing; the body there, but absent.

**And the choice of the comparison agrees with the context of the verse:** the sixteenth verse had said that fleeing from death would not work. **The nineteenth verse shows that those who want to flee are already in a state resembling death.** What they flee from is upon them as they flee.

I record this as my own reading.

### سَلَقُوكُم بِأَلْسِنَةٍ حِدَادٍ — sharp tongues

**سَلَقَ — root س-ل-ق.** On the concrete sense of the word the lexicographers record several things: *salaqa'l-laḥm* — he threw the meat into boiling water (he scalded it); *salaqa bi-lisānih* — he wounded someone with his tongue, he was harsh with him.

**What links the "scalding" sense of the root with the sense "to hurt with the tongue" is heat.** Speech burns where it touches.

**This word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

**حِدَاد — singular حَدِيد. Root: ح-د-د.** To be sharp; a limit. *Ḥadīd* — iron (treated as the name of a sūra at `057-hadid.md`); *ḥadd* — a limit, a penalty; *ḥādd* — sharp.

**That is, the verse gives the tongue two qualities: scalding and sharp.** One is heat, the other cutting. **The Qurʾān likens the way speech does harm to two separate physical acts.**

**And a link must be made with `049-hucurat.md`:** that sūra was from beginning to end about wounding with speech (mockery, nicknames, backbiting, surmise). Al-Aḥzāb touches the same subject here but from a different angle: it records **when speech rises** — after the danger has passed.

### فَإِذَا جَآءَ ٱلْخَوْفُ … فَإِذَا ذَهَبَ ٱلْخَوْفُ — two conditions, two behaviours

**The word order of the verse sets up a symmetry:**

| Condition | Behaviour | Direction |
|---|---|---|
| *jāʾa'l-khawf* — fear came | *tadūru aʿyunuhum* — the eyes roll | Withdrawal inward, freezing |
| *dhahaba'l-khawf* — fear went | *salaqūkum bi-alsinatin ḥidād* | Attack outward |

**And the bond between the two behaviours is established by the verse's final diagnosis: *ashiḥḥatan ʿala'l-khayr*.**

**In classical exegesis the setting of this expression is the claim laid to the gain obtained after the danger has passed.** That is: absent at the moment of danger, foremost at the moment of division.

**And this is an observation about human behaviour, and it is recognisable today too:** the one who does not carry the risk is the most insistent about sharing the outcome. The verse records this not as a moral lesson but as a **pattern**.

### أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ لَمْ يُؤْمِنُوا۟ — where the ruling stands

At the end the verse issues a ruling: *lam yuʾminū* — "they have not believed".

**A note is needed on how this ruling is to be read.**

**One.** The ruling concerns **those who bear the attributes** enumerated in this verse. Per the method (`STYLE.md`): this is not the name of a group but the description of an attribute.

**Two.** The ruling is **a ruling God gives about an inward state**, not a conclusion people may draw. At the head of the verse it had already said *"God knows"* (*qad yaʿlamu'llāh*). **That is, the knowing party has been stated explicitly.**

**Three.** This does not mean that one person may issue the same ruling about another. The word order of the verse itself prevents it: a section that begins with *yaʿlamu'llāh* ends with *lam yuʾminū*. **The subject of the ruling has been set at the outset.**

**فَأَحْبَطَ ٱللَّهُ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ** — **the root ح-ب-ط.** The concrete sense the lexicographers record is striking: *ḥabiṭati'l-dābba* — the animal ate a harmful plant, its belly swelled and it died. **That is, *ḥabṭ* is fullness seen from outside and death seen from inside.**

**This sense of the root fits the verse exactly:** the deeds are there, they are visible; but they are empty inside. This root was treated at `047-muhammed.md`.

### What this says to the present

**There are two measures, and both are concrete.**

**One. The two faces of *shuḥḥ*.** The verse shows that a single disposition produces two opposite behaviours: withdrawal at the moment of danger, a claim of right at the moment of safety. **And what joins the two is that the person exposes himself to no cost at all.**

Its present-day counterpart is easily seen inside a shared undertaking, a team, a family: **the one who is invisible at the moment of risk and the loudest at the moment of division.** The verse binds these two states with a single word.

**Two. The moment when the voice rises.** The verb *salaqūkum* comes **after** the fear has gone. And this is a note about when speech hardens: **the sharpest form of criticism most often comes after the danger has passed.**

This is not a prohibition of criticism. What the verse records is not the content of the criticism but its **timing**: the voice that stayed silent at the moment of danger sharpening afterwards.

---

## 33/20 — يَحْسَبُونَ ٱلْأَحْزَابَ لَمْ يَذْهَبُوا۟ وَإِن يَأْتِ ٱلْأَحْزَابُ يَوَدُّوا۟ لَوْ أَنَّهُم بَادُونَ فِى ٱلْأَعْرَابِ يَسْـَٔلُونَ عَنْ أَنۢبَآئِكُمْ وَلَوْ كَانُوا۟ فِيكُم مَّا قَٰتَلُوٓا۟ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا

*Yaḥsabūna'l-aḥzāba lam yadhhabū, wa in yaʾti'l-aḥzābu yawaddū law annahum bādūna fi'l-aʿrābi yasʾalūna ʿan anbāʾikum, wa law kānū fīkum mā qātalū illā qalīlā*

"They suppose the confederated groups have not gone. And if those groups were to come again, they would wish to be out in the desert among the bedouin, asking from afar for news of you. Even if they were among you they would fight but little."

### The verse in which the sūra's name occurs

The word **ٱلْأَحْزَاب** occurs **twice** in this verse and once in the twenty-second. The sūra takes its name from here.

**And that the word comes with the definite article (*al-*) is worth noting:** groups the addressee knows and recognises. The verse is not introducing them, it is recalling them.

### يَحْسَبُونَ … لَمْ يَذْهَبُوا۟ — the persistence of fear

**حَسِبَ — root ح-س-ب.** To count, to reckon; and from this "to suppose" — to make a reckoning in the mind.

**The state the sentence describes is a psychological fact worth recording:** the danger has passed, but the fear has not. The person still behaves as though the threat were there.

**And this may look like a contradiction with the nineteenth verse** — there it was said that the fear had gone and the tongues had sharpened. There is no contradiction; **the two verses speak of two different layers:** the outward behaviour shows that the fear has gone (19), while the inward reckoning still counts the threat (20).

**That is, the verse says this: the courage of the tongue and the reckoning within are not the same thing.** I record it as my own reading.

### يَوَدُّوا۟ لَوْ أَنَّهُم بَادُونَ فِى ٱلْأَعْرَابِ — the position desired

**وَدَّ — root و-د-د.** To love, to wish for. *Wudd* — love; *al-Wadūd* — one of the names of God.

**بَادُون — singular بَادٍ. Root: ب-د-و.** To come into view, to appear; and *bādiya* — the desert, the open country (the place where everything is visible because it is treeless). *Badawī* is from the same root.

**ٱلْأَعْرَاب** — **this word was treated in detail at `049-hucurat.md`, and the finding recorded there holds here too: *aʿrāb* does not mean "the Arabs"; it means *the bedouin*, that is, those who live a nomadic life.** The same note was entered at `048-fetih.md` as well. I rely on that treatment.

**This is a critical note in view of the method (`STYLE.md`):** the word names not an ethnic group but a **way of living.** And the verse does not condemn that way of living; it records **the desire of the person who wants to flee there.**

### يَسْـَٔلُونَ عَنْ أَنۢبَآئِكُمْ — the completing of the picture

**This is the finest point in the verse.**

The position desired is not merely "to be far off". The verse adds a detail: **to be far off and to ask for news.**

**نَبَأ — root ن-ب-أ.** And it was treated as the name of a sūra at `078-nebe.md`. What was recorded there: a *nabaʾ* is not an ordinary piece of news; it is **important news, news with consequences.**

**That is, the person described is following the event.** He is not indifferent. Far off but curious; safe but informed.

**This is a portrait worth recording, and it has a modern counterpart:** standing outside the event and watching the event. The verse depicts this as a desire: **to be safe and at the same time to be counted as inside what is happening.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the joining in one sentence of *bādūna* (far off) with *yasʾalūna ʿan anbāʾikum* (asking for news).

### وَلَوْ كَانُوا۟ فِيكُم مَّا قَٰتَلُوٓا۟ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا — the final measurement

Again a *law* clause (a condition that did not come about) and again an *illā qalīlā*.

**This expression occurs for the third time in this section of the sūra:**

| Verse | The construction | What is little |
|---|---|---|
| **14** | *mā talabbathū bihā illā yasīrā* | The delay would have been little |
| **16** | *lā tumattaʿūna illā qalīlā* | The enjoyment will be little |
| **18** | *lā yaʾtūna'l-baʾsa illā qalīlā* | Their coming to the fighting is little |
| **20** | *mā qātalū illā qalīlā* | Their fighting would have been little |

**Four notes of "little".** And this repetition says something: **the verses describe not an absence but an *insufficiency*.** No one is entirely absent; everyone is present to some degree. **And the sūra's measure is not presence or absence but quantity.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the fourfold repetition of the pattern.

**And this prepares the twenty-first verse that comes immediately after:** *uswa ḥasana*. Against what is insufficient, a measure is set.

---

## 33/21 — لَّقَدْ كَانَ لَكُمْ فِى رَسُولِ ٱللَّهِ أُسْوَةٌ حَسَنَةٌ لِّمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَٱلْيَوْمَ ٱلْءَاخِرَ وَذَكَرَ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا

*Laqad kāna lakum fī rasūli'llāhi uswatun ḥasanatun li-man kāna yarju'llāha wa'l-yawma'l-ākhira wa dhakara'llāha kathīrā*

"There is indeed for you in the Messenger of God a good example — for whoever hopes for God and the Last Day and remembers God much."

### The place of the verse

**This verse stands in the middle of the siege section**, and its place is no accident.

The twentieth verse had drawn a picture of insufficiency: coming little, fighting little, asking for news from afar. **The twenty-first verse sets a measure in the very middle of that picture.**

**And the verse is not a command but a *statement*.** It does not say "take him as an example"; it says "**there is an example**". The verb is *kāna* — the perfect, stating a settled condition.

**This difference of word order must be recorded:** an imperative sets up a demand, a declarative states a fact. **The verse sets the exemplarity down not as a demand but as something that stands there.** Whether it is used or not is left to the second half of the sentence.

### أُسْوَة — root أ-س-و

**This root was treated in detail at `060-mumtehine.md` (60/4 and 60/6). I rely on that treatment and bring what was recorded there over to here, because it is needed for the understanding of this verse.**

What was recorded there:

- **The concrete sense of the root is unexpected: to heal a wound, to treat it.**
- **أَسَا الجُرْحَ** — he treated the wound, he bound it up.
- **الآسِي** — a physician, one who binds wounds.
- **الأُسْوَة / الإِسْوَة** — that by looking at which consolation is found, whose state one aspires to.
- **المُوَاسَاة (*muwāsāt*)** — to share someone's trouble, to give him a portion of what one has. **From the same root.**
- **The bond between the two branches of the root:** in Arabic, the way to console someone is to show him another like himself. To say "you are not alone" is what binds the wound.

**And now let us look at this root in the context of this verse.**

**The verse comes in the middle of a siege.** Those addressed have been afraid, have been shaken, and some of them have wanted to flee. And precisely at that point a word whose root is "to bind a wound" is used.

**This changes the emotional weight of the verse, and it is worth recording:** the verse is not merely offering a model of behaviour. **It is showing someone standing in the same place.** The person inside the siege is looking at someone who stands inside the siege.

**And the finding recorded at `060-mumtehine.md` is completed here:** there Abraham and those with him were set forth as an example, and there too something hard was being asked of those addressed (to put a distance between themselves and their own kin). **In both places the word stands beside something hard.**

**The construct *uswa ḥasana* occurs in three places in the Qurʾān:** this verse, and al-Mumtaḥina 60/4 and 60/6. **All three are in a context of hardship.**

**حَسَنَة** — root ح-س-ن. As was recorded at `060-mumtehine.md`: the adjective is added because *uswa* is in itself neutral; the Qurʾān could have used the word *uswa* for a bad example too.

### فِى رَسُولِ ٱللَّهِ — "in"

**The preposition *fī* — "in".** The verse does not say "the Messenger of God is an example **to** you"; it says "**in** the Messenger of God there is an example".

**This is a distinction at the level of word order and it carries meaning:** the example is not identical with the person; it is something **found in** the person. That is, what is to be looked at is not the whole of the person but that thing in him.

I give this note with caution: the particle *fī* is used in Arabic in functions other than the adverbial, and I draw no forced conclusion from this. **What I record is the picture the wording gives.**

### لِّمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَٱلْيَوْمَ ٱلْءَاخِرَ — the condition

**This is the most often skipped and the most important place in the verse.**

The first half of the sentence made a statement: there is an example. **The second half makes the working of that exemplarity conditional.**

**The particle لِ here conveys restriction:** "for…". The example stands there for everyone, but **its working** depends on a condition.

**And the condition consists of three elements:**

| Element | Root | What |
|---|---|---|
| **يَرْجُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ** | ر-ج-و | To hope for God |
| **وَٱلْيَوْمَ ٱلْءَاخِرَ** | — | To hope for the Last Day |
| **وَذَكَرَ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا** | ذ-ك-ر | To remember God much |

**رَجَا — root ر-ج-و.** To hope, to expect. **And the root has an interesting side:** *arjā* from the same root means "to postpone"; *rajā* also means "the edge, the brink of a thing" (*arjāʾihā* — the edges of the sky, at al-Ḥāqqa 69/17). **In the root of the word, hope is to stand at an *edge*** — on the border of a place not yet reached.

**It is worth pausing on why this condition is set.**

**An example is an example only for someone who looks in the same direction as it.** To look at a person's conduct and draw a measure from it requires sharing **why** that conduct was performed. If it is not shared, the conduct is unintelligible and cannot be repeated.

**In the siege context of the verse this condition is particularly meaningful:** the people in the thirteenth and nineteenth verses were in the same scene. **They too saw the same example.** But it did not work — because the condition was absent.

**That is, the verse explains why an exemplarity works in some and not in others.** The example is not deficient; a condition is deficient in the eye that looks.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the conditional structure of the verse and the fact that this verse comes immediately after the verses describing those who saw the example and did not do the same.

### وَذَكَرَ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا — the third condition

**And the third condition is on a different level from the first two.** The first two are an **orientation** (hoping), the third a **practice** (remembering).

**The tense of the verb changes too:** *yarjū* (imperfect, continuity) → *dhakara* (perfect). The lexicographers record this shift; the perfect here conveys a state that has come about and settled.

**كَثِيرًا — "much".** The measure is quantitative. And this will link directly with the forty-first verse of the sūra: *"O you who believe! Remember God much"* — **ٱذْكُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ذِكْرًا كَثِيرًا** (33/41).

**That is, what the sūra sets down as a condition in the twenty-first verse it repeats as a command in the forty-first.** There are twenty verses between the two, and the sūra's heaviest regulations lie in between.

I record this link as my own reading; its ground is the pairing of *dhikr* + *kathīr* in the two verses.

### What the verse does not say

**A note is needed, because this verse has been much debated.**

The verse does not state the **extent** of the exemplarity. Which behaviours are exemplary and which are particular to a situation is not in the wording of this verse.

**This question has been debated at length in the history of Islamic thought** — the bindingness of the Sunna, its extent, its kinds, the distinction between what is religious and what is human. **This commentary does not enter that debate and issues no ruling.**

**What emerges from the wording of the verse is this:** there is an example, it is good, and its working depends on a condition of orientation. **Its detail is not here.**

### What this says to the present

**One. Being an example is different from giving a rule.**

A rule says what is to be done. An example shows how someone did it. And the difference between the two is this: **a rule must foresee situations; an example gives a direction even in situations that were not foreseen.**

That the verse comes in the middle of a siege supports this: a siege is a situation that was not foreseen, and a list of rules does not suffice there.

**Two. The measure of the root: being an example is a form of consolation.**

In the root of the word *uswa* there is the binding of a wound. **This is an unexpected answer to the question of what exemplarity is for:** an example says, besides "do it this way", also "this is a thing that can be done".

If something hard is being asked, the greatest obstacle is most often that the thing **looks impossible**. An example removes the claim of impossibility.

**Three. The present-day face of the condition.**

The verse says that the example will not work in everyone. **And this shows the limit of exemplarity:** showing a person's conduct produces no change if there is no orientation in the person opposite.

Its practical counterpart is plain: **you cannot change anyone merely by showing an example.** An example guides the person who is already looking in a direction; in the person who is not looking it produces no effect. The verse records this not as a fault but as a way things work.

---

## 33/22 — وَلَمَّا رَءَا ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ٱلْأَحْزَابَ قَالُوا۟ هَٰذَا مَا وَعَدَنَا ٱللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُۥ وَصَدَقَ ٱللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُۥ وَمَا زَادَهُمْ إِلَّآ إِيمَٰنًا وَتَسْلِيمًا

*Wa lammā raʾa'l-muʾminūna'l-aḥzāba qālū hādhā mā waʿadana'llāhu wa rasūluhu wa ṣadaqa'llāhu wa rasūluh, wa mā zādahum illā īmānan wa taslīmā*

"And when the believers saw the confederated groups they said, 'This is what God and His Messenger promised us; God and His Messenger spoke truly.' And it increased them only in faith and in submission."

### The answer to the twelfth verse

**This verse is the direct counterpart of the twelfth verse**, and the correspondence is established at the level of the words. The table was given at the twelfth verse; here I complete it.

| | **33/12** | **33/22** |
|---|---|---|
| **The verb** | *yaqūlu* — they were saying | *qālū* — they said |
| **What was seen** | The same scene | *raʾa'l-muʾminūna'l-aḥzāb* |
| **The key word** | *waʿadanā* | *waʿadanā* |
| **The verdict** | *illā ghurūrā* — nothing but delusion | *ṣadaqa'llāhu wa rasūluh* — they spoke truly |
| **The outcome** | — | *mā zādahum illā īmānan wa taslīmā* |

**The two verses use the same verb (*waʿada*) and the same two subjects (*God and His Messenger*). The only thing that differs is the continuation of the sentence.**

**And this is the strongest datum of the text that the sūra establishes in this section:** the difference lies not in the event but in the reading of the event. This is what I recorded as my own reading, and it is completed here.

### وَلَمَّا رَءَا — "when they saw"

**لَمَّا** — a particle of time; generally in the sense "at the moment when something happened", binding two events together.

**And the verb is *raʾā* — they saw.** In this section of the sūra the matter of seeing occurs for the third time:

| Verse | The seeing |
|---|---|
| **9** | *junūdan **lam tarawhā*** — armies you did not see |
| **10** | *zāghati'l-**abṣār*** — the eyes swerved |
| **22** | *lammā **raʾā** al-muʾminūna'l-aḥzāb* — when the believers saw |

**Three verses, three different states of seeing:** help that cannot be seen, a gaze that loses its focus, and **a steady gaze.** In the twenty-second verse the eye does not swerve; it sees what it sees and frames a sentence.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the distribution of the roots ر-أ-ي and ب-ص-ر within the section.

### هَٰذَا مَا وَعَدَنَا ٱللَّهُ — the demonstrative pronoun

**هَٰذَا** — the near demonstrative: "this here". It points to something that could be indicated by hand.

**And what is being pointed to is the siege itself.** The believers point not to a deliverance but to a **danger** and say "this is what was promised".

**This is the most striking place in the verse and must be read carefully.** It can be understood in two ways:

| Reading | What it says | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **The hardship itself had been promised** | It had been announced beforehand that difficulty would come; when it came, that was confirmed | The logic of al-Baqara 2/214: "without what came upon those before you coming upon you…" |
| **What would come after the hardship had been promised** | This hardship is the sign of the promised outcome | The outcome in verse 25 |

**The two readings do not exclude one another** and the wording of the verse is open to both. **I do not impose a preference.**

**But I record a datum of the text in favour of the first reading:** *hādhā* is the near demonstrative and points to what is present at that moment. What is present at that moment is the siege.

### وَصَدَقَ ٱللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُۥ — the confirming of the word

**The root ص-د-ق** was treated at the seventh verse: truthfulness; and the concrete side of the root, **firmness**.

**And here the root sets up the language of a covenant.** The eighth verse had said *"so that the truthful may be asked about their truthfulness"*. In the twenty-second verse the believers use the same root of the other party: *ṣadaqa'llāhu wa rasūluh*.

**That is, within the sūra *ṣidq* is established as a concept working in both directions:** the one who gives the word keeps it, and so does the one who receives it. I record this as my own reading.

### وَمَا زَادَهُمْ إِلَّآ إِيمَٰنًا وَتَسْلِيمًا — the increase of faith

**This sentence was taken into the list of evidence at `048-fetih.md` in the debate over whether faith increases. I rely on that treatment and do not repeat the debate here.**

What was recorded there: whether faith increases is one of the oldest debates in the history of theology; the two views and their grounds were given in a table, no side was taken, and it was recorded that the question arises from a **difference of definition.**

**There this further finding was made about this verse, and I bring it over here:** *"Some of the things that set off the increase are good news and some are bad news. At al-Aḥzāb 33/22 what causes the increase is a threat outright."*

**A linguistic note on the subjects of the sentence:** *mā zādahum* — the agent of the verb is not named; on the plainest reading the agent is what was seen in the preceding sentence (*al-aḥzāb*, or the scene generally). That is, **what produces the increase is the threat itself.**

**تَسْلِيم — root س-ل-م.** To be sound, to be without flaw; and from this *salām*, *islām*, *taslīm*. **The concrete sense of the root is a *wholeness*** — a thing's being complete, without a crack.

**And that the word is set beside *faith* in this verse is worth noting.** The two words say two different things:

| Word | What |
|---|---|
| **إِيمَان** | Assent — accepting that a thing is true |
| **تَسْلِيم** | Submission — leaving oneself to that acceptance |

**One is in the mind, the other in conduct.** And the verse says that both increase together.

**And the "wholeness" sense in the root of *taslīm* closes a ring with the fourth verse:** the fourth verse had said that there are not two hearts in one body — that is, indivisibility. **Submission is the enacted form of that indivisibility.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the reciprocal position within the sūra of the two roots (the refusal of duality in ق-ل-ب and the wholeness of س-ل-م).

---

## 33/23 — مِّنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ رِجَالٌ صَدَقُوا۟ مَا عَٰهَدُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ عَلَيْهِ فَمِنْهُم مَّن قَضَىٰ نَحْبَهُۥ وَمِنْهُم مَّن يَنتَظِرُ وَمَا بَدَّلُوا۟ تَبْدِيلًا

*Mina'l-muʾminīna rijālun ṣadaqū mā ʿāhadu'llāha ʿalayh, fa-minhum man qaḍā naḥbahu wa minhum man yantaẓir, wa mā baddalū tabdīlā*

"Among the believers are men who were true to the pledge they gave God. Some of them have fulfilled their vow (have given their lives), and some are waiting. They have made no change at all."

### One of the most frequently cited verses of the sūra

**The word order of the verse is striking from its first word.** The sentence begins by bringing the predicate forward: *mina'l-muʾminīna rijālun* — "among the believers are **men**". In Arabic this is a structure required when the subject is indefinite, and it produces **emphasis**.

**مِّنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ — the partitive (indicating a part).** The particle *min* here means "some of". **That is, the verse is not speaking about all the believers; it is setting a part apart.**

**This must also be recorded in view of the method (`STYLE.md`):** the verse does not load an attribute wholesale onto a group. Neither in praise nor in blame.

### رِجَال — the weight of the word

**Root: ر-ج-ل.** And the concrete sense of the root is **the foot**: *rijl* — a foot. *Rājil* — one who goes on foot. *Rajul* — a grown man.

**The word's use here is assessed in two ways in classical exegesis:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **It conveys sex** | The context is war; the word is in its proper sense |
| **It conveys a quality** | *Rajul* is also used in Arabic as a word of praise: "a man in the full sense" |

**The second explanation is supported by the Qurʾān's use of the word elsewhere:** *"And a **man** came running from the far end of the city"* (Yā Sīn 36/20); *"And a believing **man** from Pharaoh's household, who concealed his faith, said…"* (Ghāfir 40/28) — **this use was treated at `040-gafir.md`.** In both places the word emphasises someone standing alone.

**I do not impose a preference.** But this can be recorded: the two explanations do not conflict; the word can carry both a sex and a quality, and this is common in Arabic.

**And there is a link with the fourth verse:** there too *li-rajulin* occurred — "He has not made two hearts for a man". **The sūra uses the word twice: once as the subject of indivisibility, once as the subject of keeping one's word.** The two uses look to the same place: **the one who is not divided, who stands by the word he gave.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that both occurrences of the word in the sūra are in a context of "wholeness".

### صَدَقُوا۟ مَا عَٰهَدُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ عَلَيْهِ — the counterpart of the fifteenth verse

**This sentence is the direct answer to the fifteenth verse.**

| | **33/15** | **33/23** |
|---|---|---|
| **Who** | Those who wanted to flee | *Rijālun* |
| **The verb** | *kānū ʿāhadu'llāha* — they had given a pledge | *ṣadaqū mā ʿāhadu'llāha ʿalayh* — they were true to the pledge |
| **The outcome** | *yuwallūna'l-adbār* (the opposite of the pledge given) | *mā baddalū tabdīlā* — they made no change |

**The same root (ع-ه-د), two different outcomes.** The pledge had been given on both sides; the difference lies in what was done with the pledge.

**And that the verb *ṣadaqū* is constructed with an object is striking:** not "they were truthful in their pledge" but a structure closer to "**they made the pledge they had given come true**". In Arabic the verb *ṣadaqa* can take a direct object. **That is, truthfulness is depicted as a work done upon the word given.**

### قَضَىٰ نَحْبَهُۥ — "he fulfilled his vow"

**This is the expression most often dwelt on in the verse.**

**نَحْب — root ن-ح-ب.** The senses the lexicographers record:

| Sense | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **A vow, a pledge** | An obligation a person takes upon himself |
| **An appointed term, the end of a life** | A determined span |
| **Weeping aloud** | *Naḥīb* — sobbing |
| **Walking fast and resolutely** | *Naḥb fi'l-sayr* — speed on the road |

**قَضَىٰ — root ق-ض-ي.** To complete a matter, to finish it, to settle it by a decision.

**The construction occurs only here in the Qurʾān**, and in classical exegesis these two explanations come to the fore:

| Explanation | What it says | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **He gave his life (he died)** | *Naḥb* here means "the appointed term" or "the pledge of death he took upon himself" | The contrast in the following clause: *wa minhum man yantaẓir* — "and some are waiting" |
| **He fulfilled his vow** | *Naḥb* is "a vow"; the person completed what he had undertaken | The first sense of the root |

**The two explanations do not exclude one another** and in classical exegesis they are mostly understood together: what was undertaken was completed — and in the context of this sūra the completion came by the giving of a life.

**I do not impose a preference.** But the datum the word order gives is this: the sentence sets up a **contrast** — *qaḍā* / *yantaẓir*. And since *intiẓār* (waiting) is a continuing state, the *qaḍā* set against it conveys something finished.

**And the wording of the verse gives no names.** Certain names are mentioned in connection with this verse in the classical sources. **No name is given in this commentary**, because the reports differ at the level of detail, and the verse itself is making a description, not an identification.

### وَمِنْهُم مَّن يَنتَظِرُ — those who wait

**ٱنْتَظَرَ — root ن-ظ-ر.** To look; and *intiẓār* — to look while waiting for a thing to come.

**That the verb comes in the imperfect is worth noting:** the first part is perfect (*qaḍā*), the second imperfect (*yantaẓir*). **One is closed, the other open.**

**And the clause has no object.** The verse gives no answer to the question "waiting for what?" In classical exegesis it is said that this is waiting for what the first group reached (that is, the completion of the word given).

**This aspect of the verse says something worth recording, and I give it as my own reading:** the verse divides those who kept their word into **two groups** and names both in the same sentence, with the same praise. **That is, keeping one's word does not require the word to have been completed.** The one who has not yet completed it is inside the same description.

**This shows where the measure is set:** the measure lies not in the outcome but in **not changing.**

### وَمَا بَدَّلُوا۟ تَبْدِيلًا — the closing

**بَدَّلَ — root ب-د-ل.** To put another thing in the place of a thing. *Badal*, *tabdīl*, *istibdāl* are from the same root.

**And again a cognate accusative:** *mā baddalū **tabdīlā***. Like *zulzilū zilzālan shadīdā* in the eleventh verse, the verb is reinforced by its verbal noun.

**The reciprocal position of the two reinforcements within the sūra is worth recording:**

| Verse | Structure | What is reinforced |
|---|---|---|
| **11** | *zulzilū zilzālan shadīdā* | The violence of the shaking |
| **23** | *mā baddalū tabdīlā* | The absoluteness of not changing |

**The same grammatical instrument, once in an affirmative and once in a negative sentence, twelve verses apart.** The sūra says first how violent the shaking was, then how absolutely those who did not change failed to change in spite of it.

**And when the two verses are read together, the measure of the sūra is completed:** to be shaken is not a fault (11); to change is a fault (23). **The same people were both shaken and did not change.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the use of the same grammatical structure in the two verses.

**And the word *tabdīl* will occur once more in this sūra: in the sixty-second verse, of *sunnat Allāh*:** *"You will find no change (*tabdīl*) in the way of God."* **The same word, once for people and once for a way of working.**

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse turns an outcome-focused view upside down.**
