Two measures can be used in assessing an undertaking: **was it completed**, and **was it changed.** The verse chooses the second.

**Its practical consequence is this:** not having finished yet is not a deficiency. The deficiency is giving up on the road. The verse says this by naming the one who completed and the one who waits **in the same sentence.**

**And the choice of the expression *mā baddalū* matters too:** the verse does not say "they did not give up" or "they did not flee". **It says "they did not change".** That is, the measure is fidelity to the text of the word given. The word remained what it was.

This is the exact opposite of the excuse in the thirteenth verse: there the word stood in place but its content had been altered (on the ground that the circumstances had changed). **Here, with the circumstances at their heaviest, the word stayed the same.**

---

## 33/24 — لِّيَجْزِىَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ بِصِدْقِهِمْ وَيُعَذِّبَ ٱلْمُنَٰفِقِينَ إِن شَآءَ أَوْ يَتُوبَ عَلَيْهِمْ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا

*Li-yajziya'llāhu'l-ṣādiqīna bi-ṣidqihim wa yuʿadhdhiba'l-munāfiqīna in shāʾa aw yatūba ʿalayhim, inna'llāha kāna ghafūran raḥīmā*

"So that God may reward the truthful for their truthfulness, and punish the hypocrites if He wills, or turn to them in forgiveness. God is Forgiving, Merciful."

### A close with two poles — and an asymmetry

**The verse is the sentence that gives the ground of the siege section:** it begins with *li-* (the *lām* of causation), that is, "so that…".

**And the most striking side of the verse is an asymmetry.**

| Side | Outcome | The note |
|---|---|---|
| **The truthful** | *yajziya… bi-ṣidqihim* | A single option; tied to a cause |
| **The hypocrites** | *yuʿadhdhiba* **or** *yatūba ʿalayhim* | Two options; with the note *in shāʾa* |

**On one side one road, on the other side two.** And the side with two roads is the side that has been censured.

**This is a fact of word order that must be recorded.** For twenty verses the sūra criticised a group in detail. And in closing the section it **leaves that group a door.**

The same structure was recorded at `048-fetih.md`: *"What is at issue is not a door closed but a participation deferred."* **In al-Aḥzāb the door is opened wider still, because the possibility of *tawba* is written explicitly.**

**And, per the method (`STYLE.md`), this must be stressed:** the sūra frames **no permanent ruling** about a group. In the nineteenth verse it was said *lam yuʾminū*; in the twenty-fourth a door of repentance is opened to the same group. **What is understood when the two verses are read together is this: the ruling concerns the state at that moment; it is not a file closed on a person.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two verses concern the same group and that the second opens a door.

### بِصِدْقِهِمْ — the *bāʾ* of cause

**The preposition *bāʾ* here conveys causation:** "for their truthfulness".

**And the root ص-د-ق occurs in this sūra for the fourth time, and twice in this verse:**

| Verse | Word | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **8** | *al-ṣādiqīna ʿan ṣidqihim* | The prophets (to be asked) |
| **22** | *ṣadaqa'llāhu wa rasūluh* | God and His Messenger |
| **23** | *ṣadaqū mā ʿāhadu'llāha ʿalayh* | The men who kept their word |
| **24** | *al-ṣādiqīna bi-ṣidqihim* | The same men (the recompense) |

**The root forms a spine in this section of the sūra**, and the question of the eighth verse finds its answer here: the truthful **were to be asked** about their truthfulness (8); the truthful **will be recompensed** for their truthfulness (24).

**And the verbal resemblance between the two verses is almost exact:** *al-ṣādiqīna **ʿan** ṣidqihim* / *al-ṣādiqīna **bi**-ṣidqihim*. **The only thing that changes is the preposition:** one indicates the subject of the question, the other the cause of the recompense.

I record this link as my own reading; its ground is the repetition of the two constructions with a single letter's difference.

### إِن شَآءَ — where the note belongs

**To which part of the sentence does the note *in shāʾa* (if He wills) attach?** In classical exegesis there are two readings:

| Reading | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Attached to the punishment alone** | "If He wills He punishes, or He turns to them in forgiveness" — the note of willing stands before the punishment |
| **Attached to both verbs** | Both the punishment and the acceptance of repentance depend on the willing |

**I do not impose a preference.** But the word order, in that the note comes immediately after the verb *yuʿadhdhiba*, seems to support the first reading a little more. This is an observation, not a ruling.

### إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا — the verse-ending

**The siege section closes with two names of mercy.** For nineteen verses fear, betrayal, excuses and sharp tongues have been described; and the section ends with **forgiveness.**

**This is a repetition of a pattern recorded at `048-fetih.md`:** the Qurʾān often closes its hard sections with a statement of forgiveness. Sūrat al-Fatḥ ended both its verse 14 and its verse 29 with forgiveness.

---

## 33/25 — وَرَدَّ ٱللَّهُ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ بِغَيْظِهِمْ لَمْ يَنَالُوا۟ خَيْرًا وَكَفَى ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ ٱلْقِتَالَ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ قَوِيًّا عَزِيزًا

*Wa radda'llāhu'lladhīna kafarū bi-ghayẓihim lam yanālū khayrā, wa kafa'llāhu'l-muʾminīna'l-qitāl, wa kāna'llāhu qawiyyan ʿazīzā*

"And God turned back those who disbelieved with their rage; they gained no good. And God sufficed the believers in the fighting. God is Strong, Mighty."

### The outcome of the siege

**It is told in a single verse.** Twenty verses were given to the inner world; the outcome fitted into one verse.

**And this shows what the section is about:** the text's interest lay not in the event itself but in the person inside the event.

### رَدَّ — root ر-د-د

To turn back, to return, to send back. *Radd*, *irtidād*, *mardūd* are from the same root.

**And the choice of the verb is worth recording.** The verse does not say "He defeated", "He scattered", "He destroyed". **It says "He turned them back".**

**The verb describes a movement: those who came went back.** And this agrees with the logic of the sūra's name: *al-aḥzāb* — companies that had come together. What came together dispersed; they returned to where they had come from.

**That is, the verse depicts the event not as a defeat but as a *cancellation*.** The siege did not take place; the undertaking was withdrawn.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the choice of the verb.

### بِغَيْظِهِمْ — "with their rage"

**غَيْظ — root غ-ي-ظ.** Rage swallowed down, boiling within. What the lexicographers record about its difference from *ghaḍab*: *ghaḍab* is anger that shows itself outwardly, *ghayẓ* is anger that boils within.

**The preposition *bāʾ* here conveys accompaniment (*muṣāḥaba*):** "together with their rage" — that is, they carried their rage away with them.

**This expression should be compared with *ḥamiyya* (heat of anger) at `048-fetih.md` 48/26**, and the finding established there goes one step further here.

| | **al-Fatḥ 48/26** | **al-Aḥzāb 33/25** |
|---|---|---|
| **The word** | *ḥamiyya* — root ح-م-ي (heating) | *ghayẓ* — root غ-ي-ظ (boiling within) |
| **In whom** | In the hearts of those who disbelieved | In those who disbelieved |
| **What happened** | *Sakīna* was set against it | They were turned back together with it |
| **The outcome** | Hands were withheld without a clash | No good at all was gained |

**Both sūras record the same thing: heat of anger produces no result.**

**And the detail al-Aḥzāb adds is this, and I record it:** the rage is named not at the **beginning** of the event but at its **end**. That is, the verse does not say that those who came came in rage; it says that they **went back in rage.** Rage is not the cause of the undertaking but its **residue.**

**And the finding at `048-fetih.md` is worth repeating:** *"What the verse does is not to criticise the heat of anger in a moral idiom; it is to name it as a state."* So too here: the verse does not condemn the rage, it records it as **the only thing left in hand.**

### لَمْ يَنَالُوا۟ خَيْرًا — "they gained no good"

**نَالَ — root ن-و-ل.** To stretch out one's hand to a thing and take it, to reach, to obtain.

**And the choice of the word *khayr* must be dwelt on.** The verse does not say "they gained no victory" or "they did not reach their aim". **It says "they gained no good".**

**The word speaks with the besiegers' own measure.** What they were seeking was, by their own lights, a "good" — a gain, an outcome. The verse takes that measure and reduces the result to zero.

**This is a rhetorical choice worth recording:** the verse does not pass judgement on the other side's aim; **it says that they did not reach their aim.** I record this as my own reading.

### وَكَفَى ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ ٱلْقِتَالَ — "He sufficed the believers in the fighting"

**كَفَى — root ك-ف-ي.** To suffice, to be enough. This root occurred in the third verse too: *wa kafā bi'llāhi wakīlā*.

**The sentence has two objects:** *kafa'llāhu **al-muʾminīna** **al-qitāl*** — "God sufficed the believers in the fighting / lifted the burden of the fighting from them".

**Two senses are recorded in classical exegesis:**

| Reading | What it says |
|---|---|
| **He lifted the fighting from them** | The matter was resolved without a great clash |
| **He sufficed them in the fighting** | God's help was enough against those facing them |

**Both are possible in the language and they do not exclude one another. I do not impose a preference.**

**And I record a link:** in the third verse it was said *kafā bi'llāhi wakīlā* — "God suffices as a trustee". **In the twenty-fifth verse the same root is repeated inside the event: *kafa'llāh*.** The declaration given at the head of the sūra is named in its middle as having come about.

I record this link as my own reading; its ground is the position of the root in the two verses.

### قَوِيًّا عَزِيزًا — the verse-ending

**قَوِىّ — root ق-و-ي.** Strength. **عَزِيز — root ع-ز-ز.** And the concrete sense of the root is **hardness, resistance to wear**: *arḍun ʿazāz* — hard, impassable ground. From this "mighty, unconquerable, precious".

**The coming of the two names together:** one conveys strength, the other the unwearing quality of that strength. **And this agrees with the content of the verse:** the besiegers gathered a force and that force dispersed; the verse-ending names a strength that does not disperse.

---

## 33/26-27 — وَأَنزَلَ ٱلَّذِينَ ظَٰهَرُوهُم مِّنْ أَهْلِ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ مِن صَيَاصِيهِمْ وَقَذَفَ فِى قُلُوبِهِمُ ٱلرُّعْبَ فَرِيقًا تَقْتُلُونَ وَتَأْسِرُونَ فَرِيقًا · وَأَوْرَثَكُمْ أَرْضَهُمْ وَدِيَٰرَهُمْ وَأَمْوَٰلَهُمْ وَأَرْضًا لَّمْ تَطَـُٔوهَا وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ قَدِيرًا

*Wa anzala'lladhīna ẓāharūhum min ahli'l-kitābi min ṣayāṣīhim wa qadhafa fī qulūbihimu'l-ruʿba, farīqan taqtulūna wa taʾsirūna farīqā · Wa awrathakum arḍahum wa diyārahum wa amwālahum wa arḍan lam taṭaʾūhā, wa kāna'llāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīrā*

"And He brought down from their strongholds those of the People of the Book who had supported them, and cast terror into their hearts. Some you were killing, and some you were taking captive. · And He gave you their land, their dwellings and their possessions as an inheritance, and a land you had not set foot on. God has power over all things."

### First a note, per the method (`STYLE.md`)

**These two verses are the place in this sūra that must be read with the greatest care**, and before beginning to read, four notes must be entered explicitly.

**One. The verse recounts a single event.** The verbs are perfect (*anzala*, *qadhafa*, *awratha*) and they take as their subject a particular group (*alladhīna ẓāharūhum* — "those who supported them"). This is not a statement of a rule but the record of an event.

**Two. The verse frames no general ruling about a religion or a lineage.** The expression *ahl al-kitāb* here is not **a designation** but an **identification**: it states who those who gave support were. Indeed the subject of the sentence is not *ahl al-kitāb* but ***alladhīna ẓāharūhum*** — "those who supported them". **That is, the ruling is tied not to an identity but to an act.**

**Three. The Qurʾān uses the same expression (*ahl al-kitāb*) elsewhere in entirely different contexts** — a call to common ground (Āl ʿImrān 3/64), the note that among them is an upright community (Āl ʿImrān 3/113), the permitting of their food and of marriage (al-Māʾida 5/5). **To draw a general ruling from a single verse runs counter to the Qurʾān's own usage.**

**Four. Al-Mumtaḥina 60/8, as a boundary verse, holds here too**, and it was cited with the same function at `048-fetih.md`: *"God does not forbid you to deal kindly and justly with those who have not fought you over religion and have not driven you from your homes."*

**The note in `STYLE.md` works here exactly: "no wholesale ruling is issued about an ethnic or religious group; what the verse describes are attributes."** This commentary abides by that note.

### Historical background — in the language of "it is transmitted"

In the classical works of biography and exegesis it is transmitted that during the siege a community bound by treaty within the city broke the treaty and gave support to the besiegers; that after the siege dispersed this community was besieged in turn; that after their surrender a ruling was applied through arbitration, within the law of war of that day.

**The details of this account — the name of the community, the identity of the arbiter, the extent of the ruling applied, the numbers — vary in the sources and are not given in this commentary.** The reason is the rule of `STYLE.md` about fabrication and certainty: when the reports differ at the level of detail, the detail cannot be presented as certain knowledge.

**And there is a more important reason, which I record:** the wording of the verse gives none of these details. **The Qurʾān has not written the name of a community.** This is not a deficiency but a choice consistent with the principle recorded at `063-munafikun.md`: **the description lasts, the exposure passes.**

### ظَٰهَرُوهُم — "they gave support"

**Root: ظ-ه-ر (the back).** And this is **the same root** as *tuẓāhirūna* (ẓihār) in the fourth verse.

**This is a word-web within the sūra that must be recorded.**

| Verse | Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *tuẓāhirūna minhunna* | Ẓihār — "you are to me as my mother's back" |
| **26** | *ẓāharūhum* | To back someone, to support |

**The same root, the same form (Form III, *mufāʿala*), two entirely different senses.** And what joins the two is **the back**: in one the back is the subject of a comparison, in the other of a support.

***Ẓahīr*** in Arabic means "a backer, a supporter" — one who stands behind another. **The picture in the word is concrete: giving one's back and lending support.**

I record this link as my own reading; its ground is that the two words are from the same root and in the same form.

### صَيَاصِي — strongholds

**Singular صِيصَة. Root: ص-ي-ص.** The primary sense the lexicographers record is interesting: **صِيصَة**, the spur on a cock's leg, and the horn of an ox — that is, the pointed limb with which an animal defends itself. Also the pointed instrument the weaver uses.

**From this comes the sense "fortress, refuge":** the structure with which a community defends itself.

**The word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

**And the choice of the word is worth recording:** the verse does not use the common words *ḥuṣūn* (fortresses) or *qilāʿ*. It chooses a word whose root is a **defensive organ**. **That is, the place they were brought down from is not merely a building; it is a community's instrument of defence.**

**مِن صَيَاصِيهِمْ — the particle *min* indicates the place of departure:** down from there.

**أَنزَلَ — root ن-ز-ل.** To bring down. **And the choice of the verb is concrete too: a movement from above downwards.** In the tenth verse those who came, came *min fawqikum* (from above you); here those who gave support are *brought down*.

### وَقَذَفَ فِى قُلُوبِهِمُ ٱلرُّعْبَ

**قَذَفَ — root ق-ذ-ف.** To throw, to hurl. The word is used in the Qurʾān for slander too (*qadhf*).

**And the concreteness of the verb is striking:** fear is not "given", it is **thrown.** Like the hurling of an object.

**رُعْب — root ر-ع-ب.** Fear, terror; and the concrete side the lexicographers record: *raʾaba'l-ḥawḍ* — he filled the basin. **That is, *ruʿb* is fear that fills** — that leaves no room inside.

**The construction occurs in several places in the Qurʾān** (Āl ʿImrān 3/151; al-Anfāl 8/12; al-Ḥashr 59/2 — **treated at `059-hasr.md`**). The occurrence in al-Ḥashr is especially close, and there too an evacuation is recounted.

**And the word *heart* once more in the sūra:** the impossibility of two hearts in the fourth verse, hearts reaching the throats in the tenth, diseased hearts in the twelfth, hearts filled with fear here. **All the sūra's distinctions are made at the level of the heart.**

### فَرِيقًا تَقْتُلُونَ وَتَأْسِرُونَ فَرِيقًا — word order

**The word order of this sentence must be dwelt on, because there is a deliberate symmetry.**

| Clause | First element | Second element |
|---|---|---|
| **فَرِيقًا تَقْتُلُونَ** | *farīqan* — **object** | *taqtulūna* — **verb** |
| **وَتَأْسِرُونَ فَرِيقًا** | *taʾsirūna* — **verb** | *farīqā* — **object** |

**In Arabic this kind of symmetry is called *laff wa nashr*, or more aptly *tarṣīʿ / muqābala*:** in the first clause the object comes first, in the second it comes last. **A mirror structure.**

**And the word *farīq* occurs for the second time in this sūra** — its first occurrence was in the thirteenth verse: *yastaʾdhinu **farīqun** minhum* ("a company asks leave"). **The same word, once for a company that wants to flee, once for a community split into two companies.** The root already conveyed "separating".

**That the verbs come in the imperfect is also worth noting:** *taqtulūna*, *taʾsirūna*. While all the verbs around them are perfect (*anzala*, *qadhafa*, *awratha*), these two are imperfect. **The lexicographers explain this as *ḥikāyat al-ḥāl* (the vivid setting of a past event before the eyes).**

### وَأَوْرَثَكُمْ — inheritance

**Root: و-ر-ث.** Inheritance. **And the choice of the verb is worth recording:** the verse does not say "He gave" or "it became spoils for you". **It says "He gave it to you as an inheritance".**

**وَأَرْضًا لَّمْ تَطَـُٔوهَا — "a land you had not set foot on"**

**وَطِئَ — root و-ط-أ.** To tread with the foot, to trample. This root was treated at `048-fetih.md` 48/25 (*an taṭaʾūhum* — "that you should trample them").

**And the contrast between the two verses is worth recording:**

| Place | The construction | What |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Fatḥ 48/25** | *an taṭaʾūhum bi-ghayri ʿilm* | Trampling unknowingly — a ground for halting an action |
| **al-Aḥzāb 33/27** | *arḍan **lam taṭaʾūhā*** | A land not set foot on — a promise |

**The same verb, once a possibility to be avoided, once a future possibility.**

**What "the land you had not set foot on" is has been debated in classical exegesis:**

| View | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Lands not yet reached** | Places to be opened later are meant; a future promise given in the perfect |
| **Places not actually entered at the time** | Within the same event, areas taken over without a clash |

**I do not impose a preference.** The wording of the verse gives no place-name.

**And one further note:** by saying *lam taṭaʾūhā* the verse uses a negative qualification. **That is, the land is described not by being possessed but by not yet having been trodden.** This agrees with the pattern of "the distance between a promise and its coming about" recorded at `048-fetih.md`.

### وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ قَدِيرًا — the close of the section

The siege section ends here. **Nineteen verses.**

**And the verse-ending that closes the section is worth comparing with the one that opened it:**

| Verse | Verse-ending | What |
|---|---|---|
| **9** (opening) | *bimā taʿmalūna baṣīrā* | Seeing |
| **27** (closing) | *ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīrā* | Powerful |

**The section opens with seeing and closes with power.** And what happens in between is exactly this: a help that was not seen (9), a party that sees, and in the end a force turned back.

I record this as my own reading.

### What this says to the present

**This is the place where the greatest care must be taken in speaking of what these verses say to the present, and, per the method (`STYLE.md`), I draw the limit explicitly.**

**These verses cannot be used to fix an attitude today towards any community, religion or people.** The reasons are inside the text:

**One.** The subject of the verse is not an identity but an **act**: *ẓāharūhum* — they gave support. Without the act there is no ruling.

**Two.** The verse recounts a single, historical event and its verbs are perfect. It is not a sentence of rule.

**Three.** The same Qurʾān uses the same expression (*ahl al-kitāb*) in contexts of common ground, of kindness and of justice as well. **To take one place in a text and disregard its other places is not to read that text.**

**And current politics is not entered.** The note in `STYLE.md` is plain and is abided by in this commentary.

**A measure that can be drawn from the text and targets no one is this:** the verse concerns the breaking of a treaty. **And it agrees with the whole logic of the sūra:** the seventh verse spoke of the weight of a word, the fifteenth of the word given being asked about, the twenty-third of not changing the word. **The twenty-sixth verse records a case in which the word was broken.**

From beginning to end the sūra is about **the word**: the word that stays in the mouth (4), the word given and kept (23), the word given and broken (26). I record this as my own reading.

---

## 33/28-29 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ قُل لِّأَزْوَٰجِكَ إِن كُنتُنَّ تُرِدْنَ ٱلْحَيَوٰةَ ٱلدُّنْيَا وَزِينَتَهَا فَتَعَالَيْنَ أُمَتِّعْكُنَّ وَأُسَرِّحْكُنَّ سَرَاحًا جَمِيلًا · وَإِن كُنتُنَّ تُرِدْنَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥ وَٱلدَّارَ ٱلْءَاخِرَةَ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ أَعَدَّ لِلْمُحْسِنَٰتِ مِنكُنَّ أَجْرًا عَظِيمًا

*Yā ayyuha'l-nabiyyu qul li-azwājika in kuntunna turidna'l-ḥayāta'l-dunyā wa zīnatahā fa-taʿālayna umattiʿkunna wa usarriḥkunna sarāḥan jamīlā · Wa in kuntunna turidna'llāha wa rasūlahu wa'l-dāra'l-ākhirata fa-inna'llāha aʿadda li'l-muḥsināti minkunna ajran ʿaẓīmā*

"O Prophet! Say to your wives: If you desire the life of this world and its adornment, then come, I will give you a provision and release you in a fair release. · But if you desire God, His Messenger and the abode of the hereafter, then know that God has prepared a great reward for those among you who do good."

### The opening of a new block

**The siege is over; the sūra enters the house.** And the entry is made with the sūra's second address *yā ayyuha'l-nabiyy*.

**And the first thing the block does is to offer a *choice*.** This is worth recording: in entering the house the sūra begins not with a command but with a question.

### قُل لِّأَزْوَٰجِكَ — address through an intermediary

**The verse addresses the Prophet and asks him to say it to his wives.** That is, the address has two layers.

**And this layering will disappear in the verses that follow:** in the thirtieth and thirty-first the speech continues in the third person, and from the thirty-second onwards directly in the second person feminine plural (*kunna*, not *kum*). **That is, the sūra begins with an intermediary and passes to direct address.**

I record this as a datum of the text.

### Two conditions, two answers

**The structure of the verses is an exact symmetry:**

| | **The first condition (28)** | **The second condition (29)** |
|---|---|---|
| **The particle** | *in kuntunna turidna* | *wa in kuntunna turidna* |
| **The object** | *al-ḥayāta'l-dunyā wa zīnatahā* | *Allāha wa rasūlahu wa'l-dāra'l-ākhira* |
| **How many elements** | Two (life + adornment) | Three (God + Messenger + hereafter) |
| **The answer** | An offer: *taʿālayna* | A statement: *inna'llāha aʿadda* |
| **The outcome** | *sarāḥan jamīlā* | *ajran ʿaẓīmā* |

**There are two points of word order.**

**One. The answer to the first condition is a *command*, the answer to the second a *statement*.** In the first it is said "come, let me do such-and-such" — a procedure is described. In the second there is no procedure; what has been prepared is simply announced.

**And this difference is meaningful:** the first road requires a **decision** and that decision has a procedure. The second road is not a decision but a **continuation**. There is nothing new to be done.

**Two. The second condition has three elements, the first two.** And the third element (*al-dāru'l-ākhira* — the abode of the hereafter) is the counterpart of *al-dunyā* in the first condition. **That is, the list has been deliberately kept longer: on the other side there are both counterparts and more.**

I record these two points as my own reading.

### ٱلْحَيَوٰةَ ٱلدُّنْيَا وَزِينَتَهَا

**دُنْيَا — root د-ن-و.** To be near. *Dunyā* is the feminine of the elative: **"the nearest".** It was treated at `092-leyl.md` and elsewhere.

**The word itself carries no verdict:** *dunyā* does not mean "bad"; it means "**near**". And *ākhira*, opposite it, means "later". **Both are words of time.**

**زِينَة — root ز-ي-ن.** Adornment. And this semantic field will meet us once more in the thirty-third verse, in *tabarruj* — a different root, but the same field.

**And the addition of the word *zīna* is worth noting:** the verse is not content with "the life of this world", it adds "**and its adornment**". That is, what is meant is not staying alive; it is **the surplus of life.**

### فَتَعَالَيْنَ — "come"

**Root: ع-ل-و.** To be high. *Taʿāla* — originally "come up"; in Arabic it has become a common verb of calling.

**That the word comes in the feminine plural imperative (*taʿālayna*)** shows the directness of the address.

### أُمَتِّعْكُنَّ — "let me give you a provision"

**Root: م-ت-ع.** It occurred in the sixteenth verse: to derive benefit temporarily.

**Here the word comes close to a legal term:** *mutʿa*, a provision given to a woman in the case of divorce. **The Qurʾān mentions it in other places too** (al-Baqara 2/236, 2/241; al-Aḥzāb 33/49).

**I do not enter into legal detail**; the schools have adopted different rulings on the amount and the obligatoriness of this provision, and that is not the subject of this commentary.

### وَأُسَرِّحْكُنَّ سَرَاحًا جَمِيلًا — "to release in a fair release"

**سَرَّحَ — root س-ر-ح.** And the concrete sense of the root must be dwelt on: *saraḥa'l-māshiya* — **he let the animals out to pasture.** *Sarḥ* — the herd that goes out to pasture in the morning.

**That is, the picture in the word is a *setting free*** — untying the tether and letting out to pasture.

**This construction (*sarāḥan jamīlā*) was recorded at `073-muzzemmil.md`**, and al-Aḥzāb 33/28 and 33/49 were given there as examples. I rely on that treatment.

**جَمِيل — root ج-م-ل.** Beautiful. And the root has a striking side: *jamal* — a camel; *jumla* — a total. The lexicographers explain *jamāl* as "the harmony of the whole of a thing".

**The construction occurs three times in the Qurʾān, and in all three it qualifies a hard situation:**

| Place | The construction | What |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Ḥijr 15/85** | *fa'ṣfaḥi'l-ṣafḥa'l-jamīl* | Forgiving in a fair way |
| **al-Maʿārij 70/5** | *fa'ṣbir ṣabran jamīlā* | Fair patience — treated at `070-mearic.md` |
| **al-Aḥzāb 33/28, 49** | *sarāḥan jamīlā* | Releasing in a fair way |

**And in all three the adjective *jamīl* states *how* a thing is to be done** — not what is to be done.

**This is a measure worth recording, and I give it as my own reading:** separation is a possibility the verse envisages and it is not forbidden. **What is regulated is not the separation itself but its form.** The ending of a relationship and its ending badly are different things; the verse removes the second.

### What the verse says and what it does not say

**A note is needed.**

**What the verse says:** a choice has been offered and both roads are open. For the one who chooses the first road a procedure has been laid down (a provision + a fair release); for the one who chooses the second, a recompense has been announced.

**What the verse does not say:** what answer the wives gave to this offer. **The Qurʾān does not write it.**

**In the classical sources it is transmitted that all of them chose the second road**, and this is given with the note "it is transmitted". **The text itself does not contain the answer, and this is not a deficiency:** the continuation of the sūra (30-34) is in any case addressed to those who have chosen the second road. **The answer is understood from the way the text goes on.**

I record this as a datum of the text.

### What this says to the present

**One. Setting the choice out openly.**

What the verse does is to make the terms of a relationship explicit. And it is striking that the party making the offer is the party that wishes to continue the relationship.

**Its present-day form is this:** laying expectations out openly in a relationship does not weaken the relationship. **An expectation not laid out openly turns, when it is not met, into a disappointment; an expectation laid out openly turns into a decision.**

**Two. The form of the separation is regulated.**

*Sarāḥan jamīlā* is perhaps one of the constructions in this sūra that looks most directly at the present. The ending of a relationship may be unavoidable; the manner of the ending is always a choice.

**And the picture in the root of the word supports this:** *sarḥ* is letting an animal out to pasture — that is, the one who is released has **an open space before him**. The word conveys not abandoning but **setting free.**

---

## 33/30-31 — يَٰنِسَآءَ ٱلنَّبِىِّ مَن يَأْتِ مِنكُنَّ بِفَٰحِشَةٍ مُّبَيِّنَةٍ يُضَٰعَفْ لَهَا ٱلْعَذَابُ ضِعْفَيْنِ وَكَانَ ذَٰلِكَ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ يَسِيرًا · وَمَن يَقْنُتْ مِنكُنَّ لِلَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِۦ وَتَعْمَلْ صَٰلِحًا نُّؤْتِهَآ أَجْرَهَا مَرَّتَيْنِ وَأَعْتَدْنَا لَهَا رِزْقًا كَرِيمًا

*Yā nisāʾa'l-nabiyyi man yaʾti minkunna bi-fāḥishatin mubayyinatin yuḍāʿaf laha'l-ʿadhābu ḍiʿfayn, wa kāna dhālika ʿala'llāhi yasīrā · Wa man yaqnut minkunna li'llāhi wa rasūlihī wa taʿmal ṣāliḥan nuʾtihā ajrahā marratayni wa aʿtadnā lahā rizqan karīmā*

"O women of the Prophet! Whoever among you commits a manifest indecency, the punishment will be doubled for her twofold. That is easy for God. · And whoever among you is devoutly obedient to God and His Messenger and does righteous work, We shall give her her reward twice over; and We have prepared for her a noble provision."

### The two faces of a standing

**These two verses together say one thing: a standing grows heavier in both directions at once.**

| | **Verse 30** | **Verse 31** |
|---|---|---|
| **The act** | *yaʾti bi-fāḥishatin mubayyina* | *yaqnut… wa taʿmal ṣāliḥā* |
| **The recompense** | *yuḍāʿaf… ḍiʿfayn* — doubled | *ajrahā marratayn* — twice over |
| **The addition** | — | *rizqan karīmā* |

**And the symmetry is deliberate:** the same multiplier is applied in both directions.

**This describes what a standing means, and I record it:** a standing is neither pure privilege nor pure burden. **It is the increase of both at once.** I give this as my own reading; its ground is that the two verses use the same multiplier.

### يَٰنِسَآءَ ٱلنَّبِىِّ — the change of address

In the twenty-eighth verse the address was to the Prophet (*qul li-azwājika*). **In the thirtieth the address turns directly to them:** *yā nisāʾa'l-nabiyy.*

**And the construction changes:** in 28 *azwājika* (your wives), in 30 *nisāʾa'l-nabiyy* (the women of the Prophet).

**This is a datum of the text and I record it.** The word *azwāj* brings forward the bond of spousehood and pairing, *nisāʾ* the being a woman. **And *al-nabiyy* (the name of the office) is used in the construct** — that is, the address is framed by ascription not to a personal bond but to an office.

**The construction will occur three times in this block: 30, 32, and in 59 it will return as *azwājika*.**

### بِفَٰحِشَةٍ مُّبَيِّنَةٍ — a note of qualification

**فَاحِشَة — root ف-ح-ش.** An indecency that exceeds all measure. The primary sense of the root is **excess**: *fahusha'l-amr* — the matter passed all bounds.

**مُبَيِّنَة — root ب-ي-ن.** Open, manifest.

**And the presence of the qualification *mubayyina* is important:** the verse takes as its subject not any fault whatever but the **manifest** one. The note narrows the ruling.

**The verse is a *conditional* sentence, and conditional sentences do not report an event that has occurred.** In Arabic the structure *man … yaʾti* sets up a supposition. **I enter this note because reading a conditional sentence as though it were an imputation is a common error.**

### يَقْنُتْ — root ق-ن-ت

**Its sense must be dwelt on, because no single English word carries it.**

*Qunūt*, by the definition the lexicographers record: **a continuous and silent state of obedience; standing constantly at a task.** The word means obedience, humility, and standing for a long time.

**The Qurʾān uses the word in striking places:** *"All that is in the heavens and the earth is *qānit* to Him"* (al-Baqara 2/116); *"And Mary was *qānita* to her Lord"* (al-Taḥrīm 66/12).

**And that the word was chosen here instead of *ṭāʿa* (ط-و-ع) is worth noting.** In the first verse of the sūra *lā tuṭiʿ* (do not obey) was used. Here there is *qunūt*.

**The difference is this:** *ṭāʿa* is compliance with a command and may be a single act. *Qunūt* is a **state** — it conveys continuity.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the "continuation" sense of the root.

### يُضَٰعَفْ … ضِعْفَيْنِ — the reckoning of the doubling

**ضَاعَفَ — root ض-ع-ف.** And the root has an interesting side: the same root means both **weakness** (*ḍaʿf*) and **doubling** (*ḍiʿf*).

**The lexicographers explain this duality thus:** *ḍiʿf* is the **like** of a thing added to itself — that is, one more measure of a thing. Its link with weakness comes from the picture of "a thing being doubled over / folded" in the word.

**How many times over has been debated in classical exegesis:**

| View | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Twofold** | *Ḍiʿfayn* — one more measure, that is, two in all |
| **Threefold** | Since *ḍiʿf* already means "one more measure", *ḍiʿfayn* means two more measures, three in all |

**I do not impose a preference;** the lexicographers differ on this. **What the verse intends is less a numerical reckoning than a *heightening***, and the expression *marratayn* (twice) in the thirty-first verse supports this.

### رِزْقًا كَرِيمًا — an added recompense

**كَرِيم — root ك-ر-م.** Precious, generous, noble. The lexicographers dwell on the concrete sense of the root: *karuma'l-arḍ* — the soil became fertile, abundant.

**And there is an asymmetry in the thirty-first verse:** in the thirtieth the recompense is merely doubled; in the thirty-first something further is added **on top of** the doubling (*wa aʿtadnā lahā rizqan karīmā*).

**That is, the symmetry is broken — and it is broken in favour of the good.** This is a pattern that recurs in the Qurʾān and is worth recording: the penalty is equal, the recompense is more.

### What this says to the present

**A standing is not a privilege.**

This is the clearest thing these two verses say. If the place a person occupies increases his visibility, it increases his responsibility too. **And the verse frames this not as a threat but as an equation:** the same multiplier works in both directions.

**Its present-day form holds for any visible position:** if what a person does reaches more people because of the place he occupies, then his mistake reaches more people too. **This is not an injustice; it is the logic of visibility itself.**

---

## 33/32 — يَٰنِسَآءَ ٱلنَّبِىِّ لَسْتُنَّ كَأَحَدٍ مِّنَ ٱلنِّسَآءِ إِنِ ٱتَّقَيْتُنَّ فَلَا تَخْضَعْنَ بِٱلْقَوْلِ فَيَطْمَعَ ٱلَّذِى فِى قَلْبِهِۦ مَرَضٌ وَقُلْنَ قَوْلًا مَّعْرُوفًا

*Yā nisāʾa'l-nabiyyi lastunna ka-aḥadin mina'l-nisāʾi ini'ttaqaytunna fa-lā takhḍaʿna bi'l-qawli fa-yaṭmaʿa'lladhī fī qalbihī maraḍun wa qulna qawlan maʿrūfā*

"O women of the Prophet! You are not like any one of the women — if you guard yourselves. So do not be soft and coaxing in your speech; the one in whose heart is sickness will conceive a desire. Speak a fitting word."

### لَسْتُنَّ كَأَحَدٍ مِّنَ ٱلنِّسَآءِ — and a condition

**The first sentence of the verse looks like a declaration of privilege. What follows corrects that.**

**إِنِ ٱتَّقَيْتُنَّ — "if you guard yourselves."** The sentence is made conditional.

**And this is the most important fact of word order in the verse:** the distinction comes not from the standing but from the **conduct.** The standing has been given, but what produces the difference is taqwā.

**When the word *aḥad* was treated at `112-ihlas.md`, this very verse was given as the example:** *"Where it occurs in an affirmative sentence it is almost always inside a construct: 'you are not like any one of the women' (al-Aḥzāb 33/32)."* I rely on that treatment.

**أَحَد — "any one".** The word carries an indefiniteness: the sentence does not say "you are not like such-and-such women"; it says **"like any one"**. That is, the comparison is made not with a person but with a **category**.

**And this shows why the sentence is not a sentence of comparison in the belittling sense:** no one is being disparaged, a standing is being described.

### فَلَا تَخْضَعْنَ بِٱلْقَوْلِ — the form of speech

**خَضَعَ — root خ-ض-ع.** And the primary sense of the root is **to bend, to bow the neck**: *khaḍaʿa* — he lowered himself, he bent. The Qurʾān uses the root of necks: *"their necks would bend to it"* (al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/4 — *khāḍiʿīn*).

**And when it joins with the note *bi'l-qawl* the picture that emerges is: the bending of the voice.** That is, something speech does not by its content but by its **tone**.

**In classical exegesis the expression is generally explained thus:** speaking with a softened, thinned, coaxing voice.

**And the striking side of the verse is this:** what is forbidden is not speaking. **Indeed the sentence does not end with a prohibition; it ends with a command: *wa qulna qawlan maʿrūfā* — "speak a fitting word."**

**That is, the verse does not cut off speech; it regulates its form.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the positive command that follows the prohibition. **This is the structure seen in the second verse of the sūra as well:** when one direction is closed, another direction is put in its place.

### فَيَطْمَعَ ٱلَّذِى فِى قَلْبِهِۦ مَرَضٌ — the ground

**طَمِعَ — root ط-م-ع.** The desire to obtain a thing; and it is generally used of something to which one has **no right**. *Ṭamaʿ* — greed.

**فِى قَلْبِهِۦ مَرَضٌ** — this expression occurs in the sūra for the second time. Its first occurrence was in the twelfth verse (in the context of the siege).

**And the difference between the two occurrences is worth recording:**

| Verse | Context | The symptom of the sickness |
|---|---|---|
| **12** | The siege | Counting the promise a delusion |
| **32** | The house | Drawing a desire out of speech |

**The same expression, in two entirely different fields.** And what joins the two is this: **in both, the person draws from something that is there a conclusion that suits his own condition.** From the promise, delusion; from speech, an invitation.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the repetition of the expression in two different contexts in the same sūra.

**And one further note, per the method (`STYLE.md`):** the verse does not lay **the whole** of the responsibility on the one who speaks. The sentence speaks of a sickness, and the sickness is on the other side. **What the verse does is to propose a precaution — not to impute an offence.**

### قَوْلًا مَّعْرُوفًا — the measure

**مَعْرُوف — root ع-ر-ف.** It was treated at the sixth verse: what is known, recognised, what everyone agrees to be good.

**And its use as a qualification of "speech" is worth noting:** *qawlan maʿrūfā* — "a recognised word". That is, a community's normal, customary, ordinary way of speaking.

**The Qurʾān uses this construction elsewhere too** (al-Baqara 2/235, 2/263; al-Nisāʾ 4/5, 4/8).

**The sūra's axis of "speech" reaches its third link here:**

| Verse | The speech | Its quality |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* | What stays in the mouth |
| **32** | *lā takhḍaʿna bi'l-qawl* / *qawlan maʿrūfā* | Bending / recognised |
| **70** | *qawlan sadīdā* | Firm, hitting its mark |

**Three verses, three qualities of speech.** And at the very end (70) the sūra will give the positive measure of speech.

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse records the difference between the *content* of speech and its *tone*.**

This is one of the most practical things that can be said about communication: the same sentence, in different tones, says different things. **And tone is read faster than content.**

**And the measure the verse brings is not a restriction but a call to *clarity*:** *qawlan maʿrūfā* — let what is said be understood as what it is. Let the tone not load a second message on top of the content.

**And the ground the verse gives is also worth noting:** the ground is not moral but **anticipatory.** "The one in whose heart is sickness will conceive a desire" — that is, the verse takes into account a misunderstanding that may form on the other side. **This is a model in which responsibility in communication lies not only with the speaker, but in which the speaker too takes the other side into account.**

---

## 33/33 — وَقَرْنَ فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ وَلَا تَبَرَّجْنَ تَبَرُّجَ ٱلْجَٰهِلِيَّةِ ٱلْأُولَىٰ وَأَقِمْنَ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَءَاتِينَ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَأَطِعْنَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥٓ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ لِيُذْهِبَ عَنكُمُ ٱلرِّجْسَ أَهْلَ ٱلْبَيْتِ وَيُطَهِّرَكُمْ تَطْهِيرًا

*Wa qarna fī buyūtikunna wa lā tabarrajna tabarruja'l-jāhiliyyati'l-ūlā, wa aqimna'l-ṣalāta wa ātīna'l-zakāta wa aṭiʿna'llāha wa rasūlah, innamā yurīdu'llāhu li-yudhhiba ʿankumu'l-rijsa ahla'l-bayti wa yuṭahhirakum taṭhīrā*

"Stay with dignity in your houses; do not flaunt your finery as in the first age of ignorance. Perform the prayer, give the alms, obey God and His Messenger. God wishes only, O people of the house, to remove the defilement from you and to purify you utterly."

### The structure of the verse

**The verse consists of two parts, and the two parts speak different languages.**

| Part | Content | Mood | Address |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The first** (5 commands) | *qarna*, *lā tabarrajna*, *aqimna*, *ātīna*, *aṭiʿna* | Imperative, **feminine plural** | *kunna* |
| **The second** | *innamā yurīdu'llāh…* | A declarative sentence | *kum* — **masculine plural** |

**This change of grammatical gender stands at the centre of the whole debate about the verse, and it will be treated separately below.**

### وَقَرْنَ فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ — the root of the word and a difference of reading

**Which root this word comes from depends on a difference of variant reading, and it changes the meaning.**

| Reading | Root | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **قَرْنَ** (with *fatḥa*) | **ق-ر-ر** — to be fixed, to settle, to come to rest | "Settle in your houses / stay there with steadiness" |
| **قِرْنَ** (with *kasra*) | **ق-و-ر**, or another conjugation of ق-ر-ر; on one view **و-ق-ر** (*waqār*, dignity) | "Be dignified / stay with dignity in your houses" |

**Both readings are found among the readers, and both are well known.**

**The concrete sense of the root ق-ر-ر: *qarra* — to remain fixed in a place, to settle.** From the same root *qarār* (settledness, fixity), *mustaqarr* (a place of settlement), *qurratu ʿayn* (the settling of the eye — joy).

**The sense of the root و-ق-ر: to be heavy.** *Waqār* — dignity. And at `041-fussilet.md` this root occurred as *waqr* (a heaviness in the ear).

**The two readings complete one another:** one conveys fixity in a place, the other weight in bearing. **Both are accepted in classical exegesis, and no preference is adopted in this commentary.**

**And a note is needed:** this command stands within a block addressed to the Prophet's wives (28-34). **The extent of the command — whether it is for them alone or a general measure — has been debated in classical exegesis, and the schools have reached different conclusions.** No legal ruling is issued in this commentary; the existence of the debate is recorded.

**I record two data that emerge from the text:**

**One.** The addressee of the block is stated explicitly: *yā nisāʾa'l-nabiyy* (30, 32). The commands stand inside that address.

**Two.** In verse 59 of the same sūra the address will be widened: *"to your wives, your daughters **and the believing women**"*. **That is, when the sūra wishes to generalise, it does so explicitly.** This is an observation; it is not a ruling and does not by itself settle the debate.

### وَلَا تَبَرَّجْنَ تَبَرُّجَ ٱلْجَٰهِلِيَّةِ ٱلْأُولَىٰ

**تَبَرُّج — root ب-ر-ج.** And the concrete sense of the root is **visibility**: *burj* — a tower, a bastion; a structure seen from afar because it is high. *Tabarrajat* — the woman displayed her adornment.

**This root was treated as the name of a sūra at `085-buruc.md`**, and this verse was already given there as an example: *"tabarruj — a woman's displaying her adornment and her beauty, bringing it out into the open."* I rely on that treatment.

**ٱلْجَٰهِلِيَّة — root ج-ه-ل.** Ignorance; and in the Qurʾān it is used as the name of a period.

**At `048-fetih.md` the four uses of this word in the Qurʾān were given in a table** and this verse was included there. What was recorded there: the word is used for **surmise** (Āl ʿImrān 3/154), **ḥamiyya** (al-Fatḥ 48/26), **judgement** (al-Māʾida 5/50) and **tabarruj** (al-Aḥzāb 33/33).

**And what the four uses have in common is worth recording:** in the Qurʾān *jāhiliyya* is used not as a **stretch of history** but as an **attitude**. All four name a form of behaviour.

**ٱلْأُولَىٰ — "the first" / "the former".** Classical exegesis has dwelt on this adjective:

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **The period before the Qurʾān** | "First" here means "former" |
| **A yet older period** | By "the first ignorance" a more ancient age is meant; the addressee's own period is a separate one |
| **An allusion to a second ignorance** | The adjective implies an ignorance that will come later |

**I do not impose a preference.** And I do not enter into applying the third explanation to contemporary events — the note in `STYLE.md` that "no side is taken in current politics" holds here.

### وَأَقِمْنَ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَءَاتِينَ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ — two basic commands

**The placing of these two commands here is worth recording from the point of view of word order.**

The first two commands of the verse (*qarna*, *lā tabarrajna*) are particular to this block. **The third and fourth commands, by contrast, are the Qurʾān's most frequent pair of commands** and are required of every believer.

**That is, the verse places a general obligation inside a particular address.** And this has a function: **it says once again that the standing does not produce privilege.** In the thirtieth and thirty-first verses responsibility was doubled; here it is stated that the basic obligations continue unchanged.

I record this as my own reading.

**The root ص-ل-و** will be treated in detail at the fifty-sixth verse; I leave it to there. **The root ز-ك-و**: to be purified, to grow, to be blessed with increase — in the root of the word there is both purification and growth.

### إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ لِيُذْهِبَ عَنكُمُ ٱلرِّجْسَ أَهْلَ ٱلْبَيْتِ وَيُطَهِّرَكُمْ تَطْهِيرًا

**This sentence is one of the most debated sentences of this sūra and perhaps of the Qurʾān. Per the method (`STYLE.md`), a particular care is needed here, and I state the method in advance:**

**One.** The words will be analysed one by one.
**Two.** The data the text gives (grammatical gender, context, construction) will be recorded.
**Three.** The disagreement will be given in a table, with its grounds and impartially.
**Four.** **No preference will be imposed, no insinuation will be made against any group, no polemic will be entered.**
**Five.** It will be recorded explicitly that a datum of the text does not by itself settle the debate.

### The words

**إِنَّمَا** — the particle of restriction (*ḥaṣr*): "only, nothing but". It adds a certainty and an exclusivity to the sentence.

**يُرِيدُ … لِيُذْهِبَ** — the verb *irāda* is attached by a *lām* to a clause of purpose. The lexicographers count the *lām* in this structure either as emphatic or as causal (giving the ground).

**ٱلرِّجْس — root ر-ج-س.** And the semantic field of the root is wide: filth, uncleanness; and in the Qurʾān it is used for material dirt, for moral uncleanness and for punishment alike.

| The uses in the Qurʾān | For what |
|---|---|
| al-Anʿām 6/125 | The state laid upon those who do not believe |
| al-Māʾida 5/90 | Wine, gambling, the set-up stones and the divining arrows |
| al-Tawba 9/125 | The increase of the filth in hearts |
| al-Aḥzāb 33/33 | The thing that is to be removed |

**In this verse the word comes with the definite article (*al-rijs*)** — that is, something definite and known. **What is meant is not explained in the verse.**

**طَهَّرَ — root ط-ه-ر.** Purity. **This root was treated at `058-mucadele.md`, `076-insan.md` and `074-muddessir.md`.** The concrete sense of the root is material cleanliness; the Qurʾān uses it for moral purification as well.

**تَطْهِيرًا — a cognate accusative.** Again a reinforcement (as in verses 11 and 23). **This is the third cognate accusative in the sūra:**

| Verse | Structure |
|---|---|
| **11** | *zulzilū zilzālan shadīdā* |
| **23** | *mā baddalū tabdīlā* |
| **33** | *yuṭahhirakum taṭhīrā* |

And the same pattern will come again in verse 56 (*sallimū taslīmā*).

### أَهْلَ ٱلْبَيْت — the construction

**أَهْل — root أ-ه-ل.** Those who live in a place, the possessors of a thing, a person's close kin. It occurred at `048-fetih.md` 48/11 as *ahlūnā* (our families).

**بَيْت — root ب-ي-ت.** A house; and the primary sense of the root is **to pass the night** (*bāta* — he passed the night). **That is, in the root of the word a house is "the place where one passes the night".**

**The construction occurs in two places in the Qurʾān:**

| Place | Context |
|---|---|
| **Hūd 11/73** | The angels' word to Abraham's wife: *raḥmatu'llāhi wa barakātuhu ʿalaykum **ahla'l-bayt*** |
| **al-Aḥzāb 33/33** | This verse |

**Hūd 11/73 is a parallel worth recording and is a neutral datum of the text:** there the address is directed to Abraham's wife (in the preceding verse *imraʾatuhu* — his wife — is named) and the construction is used as *ahla'l-bayt*, **with a masculine plural pronoun** (*ʿalaykum*).

**I record this datum but frame no ruling upon it.**

### The change of grammatical gender — a datum of the text

**There is a change of grammatical gender in the verse, and this stands at the centre of the debate.**

| Part | Pronoun | Gender |
|---|---|---|
| *qarna fī buyūti**kunna*** | *kunna* | Feminine plural |
| *lā tabarraj**na*** | *-na* | Feminine plural |
| *aqim**na***, *ātī**na***, *aṭiʿ**na*** | *-na* | Feminine plural |
| *li-yudhhiba ʿan**kum** | *kum* | **Masculine plural** |
| *wa yuṭahhira**kum** | *kum* | **Masculine plural** |
| *wa'dhkur**na*** (verse 34) | *-na* | Feminine plural |

**This is an undisputed fact of the text and both sides accept it. The divergence is over how the fact is to be interpreted.**

### The disagreement — with its grounds

**The table below conveys impartially the principal views found in the classical exegetical literature and their grounds. No view is preferred, and no insinuation is made against any group.**

| View | Extent | Its grounds | The objection of the other side |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. The Prophet's wives alone** | The *ahl al-bayt* in the verse are the wives who are the addressees of verses 28-34 | (1) Context: what precedes and what follows address the wives throughout; (2) in Arabic the masculine plural is used when a man is present within a group, or in agreement with a masculine word such as *ahl*; (3) at Hūd 11/73 the same construction occurs with a masculine pronoun in an address to Abraham's wife | That the change of gender falls exactly at this sentence is striking; and it is stated that there are reports to the contrary |
| **B. The close members of the Prophet's family** | *Ahl al-bayt* are particular persons among the Prophet's close kin | (1) The change of gender; (2) the reports transmitted to this effect; (3) that the particle *innamā* conveys exclusivity | The context before and after addresses the wives; the placing of a ruling detached from the context in the middle of the sentence requires explanation |
| **C. Both together** | The construction covers both the wives and the close family | (1) The wide sense of the word *ahl*; (2) a reading in which the context indicates the wives and the change of gender indicates a widening | It is said that this fully meets neither side's grounds |

**After this table I enter five notes:**

**One. No view is preferred in this commentary.** Per the principle of `STYLE.md` that "if a preference is adopted its ground is written and the note that it is not binding is entered": no preference has been adopted here, because the question stands at the centre of a disagreement between the schools, and to prefer would be to insinuate something against one side.

**Two. That the context concerns the wives is a datum of the text.** The whole of verses 28-34 addresses the wives; so does what precedes and what follows verse 33. **I record this as a datum.**

**Three. And this datum does not by itself settle the debate.** The reasons: (a) the lexicographers have recorded that there are rulings in the Qurʾān which extend beyond the context of a sentence; (b) the change of gender is a fact that itself requires explanation; (c) the question rests not on language alone but on transmitted material too.

**Four. The material of the reports is not entered into in this commentary.** Reports are transmitted in both directions; the evaluation of those reports is the business of the science of ḥadīth and cannot be done here. **Per the note in `STYLE.md` that "you are not to ascribe a saying of which you are not sure", no name, wording or source is given.**

**Five. This verse cannot be used to exalt one group or to exclude another** — on any reading. The wording of the verse states a **will**: *yurīdu'llāhu li-yudhhiba*. **A statement of will is not a claim of superiority.**

### What the sentence says on its own

**Setting the debate aside, I record three things that emerge from the wording of the sentence. These are offered as evidence for no view.**

**One. The sentence is a sentence of *purification*, not a sentence of *privilege*.** There are two verbs: to remove the defilement and to purify. **Both convey an operation**, not a status.

**Two. The sentence stands in the middle of the commands around it.** Before it there are five commands, after it one more (34: *wa'dhkurna*). **That is, the statement of purification has been placed in the middle of the obligations — not after them.**

**This is a fact of word order worth recording, and I give it as my own reading:** purification has been positioned not as something that takes the place of the obligations but as something standing **inside** them.

**Three. What *al-rijs* is is not explained in the verse.** In classical exegesis explanations such as sin, doubt and filth are given; the verse gives no definition.

### What this says to the present

**In speaking about this verse the place where the greatest care must be taken is what the verse can be used for.**

**Because it stands at the centre of a dispute, the verse is often read less for its own content than as a *position*.** And this is the most harmful thing that can be done to any text: the text becomes unable to say what it says.

**What the sentence says on its own is plain:** a will to purify is stated, and this statement is placed in the middle of a series of obligations.

**And a measure emerges that agrees with the sūra as a whole:** from the beginning the sūra says that **a name does not change reality.** The name of a standing — being a wife, being of the people of the house — does not of itself bring with it what that standing requires. **Indeed the thirty-second verse had made this explicitly conditional:** *lastunna ka-aḥadin mina'l-nisāʾi **ini'ttaqaytunna***.

I record this as my own reading, and I draw no conclusion for or against any side.

---

## 33/34 — وَٱذْكُرْنَ مَا يُتْلَىٰ فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ مِنْ ءَايَٰتِ ٱللَّهِ وَٱلْحِكْمَةِ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ لَطِيفًا خَبِيرًا

*Wa'dhkurna mā yutlā fī buyūtikunna min āyāti'llāhi wa'l-ḥikmah, inna'llāha kāna laṭīfan khabīrā*

"And remember what is recited in your houses of the verses of God and the wisdom. God is Subtle, Aware."

### The close of the block

**The block 28-34 ends here, and the close is made with a command: *wa'dhkurna*.**

**The root ذ-ك-ر** occupies an important place in this sūra: 21 (*dhakara'llāha kathīrā*), 34 (here), 35 (*al-dhākirīna'llāha kathīran wa'l-dhākirāt*), 41 (*udhkuru'llāha dhikran kathīrā*).

**And all four are gathered in the same group of blocks.** From the condition of exemplarity (21) to the general command (41) the sūra repeats remembrance.

### مَا يُتْلَىٰ فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ — "what is recited in your houses"

**تَلَا — root ت-ل-و.** To go behind, to follow; and from this "to recite" — the letters following one another. *Tilāwa* — recitation.

**The verb is passive and imperfect:** *yutlā* — "what is being recited". **That is, an ongoing recitation is described.**

**فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ — "in your houses".** The word *bayt* occurs for the second time in this passage (in 33 as *ahla'l-bayt*, here as *buyūtikunna*) and is a link in the sūra's axis of "the house".

**And the striking side of this verse is this:** the house is named as the place where the revelation comes down. **That is, in the sūra the house is at once a boundary to be protected (53), a place to stay in with dignity (33), and the place where the revelation is recited.**

I record this as my own reading.

### وَٱلْحِكْمَة — "and the wisdom"

**Root: ح-ك-م.** And the concrete sense of the root is **to restrain, to rein in**: *ḥakama'l-dābba* — he put the bit into the animal's mouth. *Ḥakama* — a bit. From this *ḥukm*, *ḥikma*, *ḥakīm*.

**This concrete sense of the root is worth recording:** wisdom is not the heaping up of knowledge; it is **holding a thing in its place.** A bit does not stop the animal — it steers it.

**What the distinction of "the verses and the wisdom" is has been debated in classical exegesis:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **The Qurʾān and the Sunna** | *Āyāt* the Qurʾān, *ḥikma* the Prophet's explanations |
| **The Qurʾān and the meaning within it** | *Āyāt* the wording, *ḥikma* the depth the wording carries |
| **Both the Qurʾān** | The construction expresses a single whole from two sides |

**I do not impose a preference.** The pair "the Book and the wisdom" occurs in several places in the Qurʾān (al-Baqara 2/129, 2/151; Āl ʿImrān 3/164; al-Jumuʿa 62/2 — **treated at `062-cuma.md`**).

### لَطِيفًا خَبِيرًا — the verse-ending

**لَطِيف — root ل-ط-ف.** And the semantic field of the root goes in two directions: **fineness** (being too fine to be seen with the eye) and **graciousness** (acting gently).

**The lexicographers join the two thus:** *laṭīf* is the one who can reach what is fine; and the one who acts finely. **That is, it has both a side of *knowing* and a side of *dealing*.**

**خَبِير — root خ-ب-ر.** To know the inner truth of a thing. And the lexicographers dwell on the concrete sense of the root: *khabara'l-arḍ* — he ploughed the earth and saw what was beneath it. **That is, *khubr* is knowledge of what lies below the surface.**

**The coming of the two names together is worth noting and agrees with the verse:** the verse speaks of something that takes place inside houses — the verses recited. **And the verse-ending closes with two names that penetrate what is hidden.**

**And the course of the verse-endings across the block is worth recording:**

| Verse | The verse-ending | What |
|---|---|---|
| **29** | *ajran ʿaẓīmā* | Recompense |
| **30** | *ʿala'llāhi yasīrā* | Ease |
| **31** | *rizqan karīmā* | Recompense |
| **34** | *laṭīfan khabīrā* | **Knowing** |

**The block begins with recompense and ends with knowing.** And this agrees with the subject of the block: the house is the place not seen from outside.

I record this as my own reading.

---

## 33/35 — إِنَّ ٱلْمُسْلِمِينَ وَٱلْمُسْلِمَٰتِ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ وَٱلْقَٰنِتِينَ وَٱلْقَٰنِتَٰتِ وَٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ وَٱلصَّٰدِقَٰتِ وَٱلصَّٰبِرِينَ وَٱلصَّٰبِرَٰتِ وَٱلْخَٰشِعِينَ وَٱلْخَٰشِعَٰتِ وَٱلْمُتَصَدِّقِينَ وَٱلْمُتَصَدِّقَٰتِ وَٱلصَّٰٓئِمِينَ وَٱلصَّٰٓئِمَٰتِ وَٱلْحَٰفِظِينَ فُرُوجَهُمْ وَٱلْحَٰفِظَٰتِ وَٱلذَّٰكِرِينَ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا وَٱلذَّٰكِرَٰتِ أَعَدَّ ٱللَّهُ لَهُم مَّغْفِرَةً وَأَجْرًا عَظِيمًا

*Inna'l-muslimīna wa'l-muslimāti wa'l-muʾminīna wa'l-muʾmināti wa'l-qānitīna wa'l-qānitāti wa'l-ṣādiqīna wa'l-ṣādiqāti wa'l-ṣābirīna wa'l-ṣābirāti wa'l-khāshiʿīna wa'l-khāshiʿāti wa'l-mutaṣaddiqīna wa'l-mutaṣaddiqāti wa'l-ṣāʾimīna wa'l-ṣāʾimāti wa'l-ḥāfiẓīna furūjahum wa'l-ḥāfiẓāti wa'l-dhākirīna'llāha kathīran wa'l-dhākirāt — aʿadda'llāhu lahum maghfiratan wa ajran ʿaẓīmā*

"Indeed the Muslim men and the Muslim women, the believing men and the believing women, the devoutly obedient men and the devoutly obedient women, the truthful men and the truthful women, the patient men and the patient women, the humble men and the humble women, the men who give alms and the women who give alms, the fasting men and the fasting women, the men who guard their chastity and the women who guard, the men who remember God much and the women who remember — God has prepared for them forgiveness and a great reward."

### The longest parallel enumeration in the Qurʾān

**This verse is the longest parallel enumeration of man and woman in the Qurʾān: ten pairs, twenty words.**

It was mentioned as "the widest example" at `085-buruc.md` and at `071-nuh.md`; here it is opened up.

### The list

| # | Masculine form | Feminine form | Root | What |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | **ٱلْمُسْلِمِين** | **ٱلْمُسْلِمَٰت** | س-ل-م | The one who submits — who leaves himself entire |
| **2** | **ٱلْمُؤْمِنِين** | **ٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰت** | أ-م-ن | The one who trusts, gives security, assents |
| **3** | **ٱلْقَٰنِتِين** | **ٱلْقَٰنِتَٰت** | ق-ن-ت | The one who stands in continual obedience |
| **4** | **ٱلصَّٰدِقِين** | **ٱلصَّٰدِقَٰت** | ص-د-ق | The truthful; whose word is firm |
| **5** | **ٱلصَّٰبِرِين** | **ٱلصَّٰبِرَٰت** | ص-ب-ر | The one who holds himself |
| **6** | **ٱلْخَٰشِعِين** | **ٱلْخَٰشِعَٰت** | خ-ش-ع | The one who lowers himself, who bows |
| **7** | **ٱلْمُتَصَدِّقِين** | **ٱلْمُتَصَدِّقَٰت** | ص-د-ق | The one who gives alms |
| **8** | **ٱلصَّٰٓئِمِين** | **ٱلصَّٰٓئِمَٰت** | ص-و-م | The one who keeps himself from a thing — who fasts |
| **9** | **ٱلْحَٰفِظِين فُرُوجَهُمْ** | **ٱلْحَٰفِظَٰت** | ح-ف-ظ | The one who guards his chastity |
| **10** | **ٱلذَّٰكِرِين ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا** | **ٱلذَّٰكِرَٰت** | ذ-ك-ر | The one who remembers God much |

### A note on some of the roots

**Most of the roots have been analysed earlier in this commentary; I rely on those treatments and do not repeat them.**

- **س-ل-م** — treated at verse 22 (*taslīm*): the concrete sense of the root is **wholeness**.
- **أ-م-ن** — treated at `002-bakara.md` and `049-hucurat.md`; and it will return in verse 72 of this sūra as *amāna*. **The same root occurs in this verse of the sūra as an attribute and at its close as a burden.**
- **ق-ن-ت** — treated at verse 31: obedience that conveys continuity.
- **ص-د-ق** — treated at verses 8, 22, 23 and 24: truthfulness; and the concrete side of the root, **firmness**.
- **ص-ب-ر** — treated at `049-hucurat.md` 49/5: the primary sense of the root is **to hold, to confine**.
- **ذ-ك-ر** — occurred at verses 21 and 34; it will return as a command in verse 41.

**Three roots need to be opened up here for the first time:**

**خ-ش-ع** — To be lowered, to bend, the sinking of the voice. For the concrete sense of the root the lexicographers give the earth as an example: *khashaʿati'l-arḍ* — the ground sank, it was lowered. **The Qurʾān uses this root for the sinking of voices too:** *"And voices are hushed before the All-Merciful"* (Ṭāhā 20/108 — *khashaʿati'l-aṣwāt*).

**ص-و-م** — And the primary sense of the root is interesting: **to hold oneself back from a thing, to stop.** In Arabic *ṣāma'l-nahār* — the day reached its midpoint and the sun seemed to stand still; *ṣāmati'l-khayl* — the horses stopped eating their fodder. **That is, *ṣawm* in its root is not "abstaining from food" but "standing still".** The Qurʾān uses the root for not speaking too: *"I have vowed a *ṣawm* to the All-Merciful"* (Maryam 19/26) — there the fast is **silence**.

**ح-ف-ظ** — To guard, to watch over, to preserve. *Ḥifẓ* — memorisation (taking into safekeeping). *Ḥāfiẓ* — the one who guards.

### A point of word order — one of the most striking structures in the sūra

**Now the real matter: the *form* of this list.**

**In this verse the Qurʾān has chosen one thing: to repeat every attribute twice, in two gender forms.**

**And an alternative was possible.** In Arabic the masculine plural can be used for a mixed group, and this is the normal working of the language. Had the verse gone on *inna'l-muslimīna wa'l-muʾminīna wa'l-qānitīna…*, it would grammatically have covered women too, and **the verse would have been half as long.**

**The Qurʾān does not do this. It raises ten words to twenty.**

**And this repetition itself carries information. I record it as my own reading and set out its grounds.**

**One. An inclusive masculine form does not *show* what it includes.** At the level of grammar the inclusion is there; but the person reading the list hears one gender. **The verse takes the inclusion out of grammar and carries it into the wording.** The inclusion is no longer a piece of information about a rule but **something heard.**

**Two. The repetition changes the rhythm of the list.** When the verse is recited every attribute comes twice, and the only difference between them is a gender ending. **This sets the sound-structure of the verse into the rhythm of a *pair*** — and this rhythm is repeated ten times. The verse says what it says **with its sound as well.**

**Three. The length is itself an emphasis.** There is no other such long dual enumeration in the Qurʾān. **The length itself shows that the verse is not an exception but a deliberate choice.**

**Four. And the kind of attributes in the list is worth noting.** Ten attributes are enumerated and not one of them is particular to a sex. Submission, faith, obedience, truthfulness, patience, humility, almsgiving, fasting, chastity, remembrance — **all of them are things both sides can do.** That is, the verse describes a shared field and names every item of that field twice.

### The two places where the symmetry breaks

**And at the end of the list the symmetry breaks twice. This is a fact of the text that must be recorded.**

| Pair | Masculine | Feminine | The difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| **9** | *al-ḥāfiẓīna **furūjahum*** | *wa'l-ḥāfiẓāt* | The object only in the first |
| **10** | *al-dhākirīna'**llāha kathīran*** | *wa'l-dhākirāt* | The object and the adverb only in the first |

**In the first eight pairs both sides are named in exactly the same form. In the last two pairs the object is said only once.**

**The lexicographers explain this thus:** in Arabic a complement mentioned in the first element need not be repeated in the second; in the second it is counted as **elided** (*maḥdhūf*) and the sense is not diminished. This is a widespread rule of economy in the language.

**That is, in point of meaning there is no difference:** the women who guard their chastity and the women who remember God much have been named in the same way.

**But there is a fact of *rhythm*, and I record it:** the verse keeps up the full repetition through eight pairs and shortens the repetition in the last two. **And this produces a close that accelerates towards the end of the list** — the sentence gathers itself after a long repetition and passes at once to the closing clause: *aʿadda'llāhu lahum…*

I record this as an observation about style; I draw no ruling from it.

### أَعَدَّ ٱللَّهُ لَهُم — the close

**And in the close the pronoun is masculine plural: *lahum*.**

**This is the normal working of the language:** a pronoun returning to a mixed list of twenty items is masculine plural in Arabic.

**And one further thing becomes visible here:** having divided the list in two, the verse gives the recompense with **a single pronoun.** **That is, the dividing is in the enumeration, the joining in the recompense.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the list is separated and the verse-ending joined.

**مَّغْفِرَةً وَأَجْرًا عَظِيمًا** — two recompenses: forgiveness and a great reward. **The same verse-ending occurred in verse 29 as well** (*ajran ʿaẓīmā*), and as was recorded at `048-fetih.md` this is the pattern that states the recompense of an undertaking.

### Context — why the verse is here

**The verse's place in the sūra is no accident.**

The preceding seven verses (28-34) addressed **a particular group**: the Prophet's wives. And in that block a particular standing, a particular responsibility and a particular measure were described.

**The thirty-fifth verse, immediately afterwards, opens the extent all the way.**

**And this transition is worth recording:** having recounted the particular situation of one group, the sūra gives, on the same page, a list that **takes in everyone.** **That is, the particular does not annul the general.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the succession of the two blocks.

**And the thirty-sixth verse that follows will be of the same width:** *mā kāna li-**muʾminin wa lā muʾmina***. **That is, in verses 35 and 36 the sūra uses the man-woman pair twice in succession** — once as a list of attributes, once as a ruling.

### What this says to the present

**What this verse says to the present comes less from its content than from its form.**

A text can say what it includes in two ways: by a rule, or by repetition. **A rule is economical but invisible; repetition is expensive but visible.**

**This verse chooses the second and pays the price:** twenty words instead of ten.

**And its counterpart is this:** a person whose name does not appear in a list does not find himself in that list, even if he knows the rule includes him. **Naming is not inclusion itself — but it is the feeling of inclusion.**

I record this as my own reading. And I record too that it carries an interesting tension with the founding principle of the sūra: **from the beginning the sūra has been saying "a name does not change reality". This verse, by contrast, shows that naming does a piece of work — not in changing reality but in *showing* it.** The two do not conflict: in the fourth verse a name was trying to make present something that was not there; here a name makes visible something that is.

---

## 33/36 — وَمَا كَانَ لِمُؤْمِنٍ وَلَا مُؤْمِنَةٍ إِذَا قَضَى ٱللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُۥٓ أَمْرًا أَن يَكُونَ لَهُمُ ٱلْخِيَرَةُ مِنْ أَمْرِهِمْ وَمَن يَعْصِ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥ فَقَدْ ضَلَّ ضَلَٰلًا مُّبِينًا

*Wa mā kāna li-muʾminin wa lā muʾminatin idhā qaḍa'llāhu wa rasūluhu amran an yakūna lahumu'l-khiyaratu min amrihim, wa man yaʿṣi'llāha wa rasūlahu fa-qad ḍalla ḍalālan mubīnā*

"When God and His Messenger have decided a matter, it is not for a believing man or a believing woman to have a choice in that matter of theirs. And whoever disobeys God and His Messenger has strayed into a manifest straying."

### The entry to the section on Zayd

**This verse is a principle placed before the account in the thirty-seventh verse.** The method of the sūra is visible once more here: **first the principle, then the application.**

The fourth and fifth verses had abolished the ascription of the adopted son; the thirty-sixth verse says that the right of choice falls away in the face of a decision; and the thirty-seventh will recount the concrete event where these two intersect.

### وَمَا كَانَ لِمُؤْمِنٍ وَلَا مُؤْمِنَةٍ — the repetition of the pair

**Immediately after the thirty-fifth verse, the man-woman pair is used once more.**

**And here the function of the repetition is different:** in verse 35 it was a list of **recompense**, here it is an **obligation.** The Qurʾān says both in the same dual form.

I record this as a datum of the text: on the same page the sūra names both the right and the obligation separately for the two sexes.

**Since it comes indefinite and in a negative context it conveys generality:** "for no believing man and no believing woman".

### ٱلْخِيَرَة — root خ-ي-ر

**The right of choice, preference. From the same root as *khayr* (good).**

**This duality of the root is worth noting:** in Arabic *khayr* means "what is good"; and *ikhtiyār*, "to choose what is good". **That is, the word for choice carries a *value judgement* in its root.**

**And the word *al-khiyara* occurs in two places in the Qurʾān:** here and at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/68 (*mā kāna lahumu'l-khiyara* — there too it is said that the authority of choice is not with people).

### مِنْ أَمْرِهِمْ — the limit of the note

**This note is important and is generally skipped.** The verse does not say "they have no right of choice in anything". **It speaks of a field bounded by a conditional clause:** *idhā qaḍa'llāhu wa rasūluhu **amran*** — "when they have decided a matter."

**That is, the right of choice is not abolished in general;** it is said that in a matter about which a decision has been made, one cannot set oneself against that decision.

**And the word *amr* occurs twice in the verse:** *qaḍa'llāhu… amran* and *min amrihim*. **The same word, once the subject of the decision, once the affair of the people.** The word order shows the place where the two coincide.

### قَضَى — root ق-ض-ي

To complete a matter, to settle it by a decision, to finish it. **It occurred in the twenty-third verse as *qaḍā naḥbah*.**

**In the root of the word there is a *finishedness*** and it agrees with the logic of the verse: if a decision has been made, the matter is closed.

### فَقَدْ ضَلَّ ضَلَٰلًا مُّبِينًا — the verse-ending

**ضَلَّ — root ض-ل-ل.** To lose the way, to stray; and the concrete sense of the root is **to be lost inside something**: *ḍalla'l-māʾu fi'l-laban* — the water was lost in the milk.

**Again a cognate accusative:** *ḍalla **ḍalālan** mubīnā*. **The fourth reinforcement in the sūra.**

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse regulates the order between decision and objection.**

**And what is striking is this: the verse takes as its subject not objection itself but its *timing*.** The discussion before a decision is made is not the subject of the verse. The subject is the claim of choice that begins **after** the decision has been made.

**Its present-day form holds for any process of shared decision:** to object before a decision is taken is a part of the process; to say "I do not go along" after it has been taken annuls not the decision but the process.

I record this as my own reading, and I do not confuse the religious context of the verse with this general observation.

---

## 33/37 — وَإِذْ تَقُولُ لِلَّذِىٓ أَنْعَمَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَأَنْعَمْتَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْسِكْ عَلَيْكَ زَوْجَكَ وَٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ وَتُخْفِى فِى نَفْسِكَ مَا ٱللَّهُ مُبْدِيهِ وَتَخْشَى ٱلنَّاسَ وَٱللَّهُ أَحَقُّ أَن تَخْشَىٰهُ فَلَمَّا قَضَىٰ زَيْدٌ مِّنْهَا وَطَرًا زَوَّجْنَٰكَهَا لِكَىْ لَا يَكُونَ عَلَى ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ حَرَجٌ فِىٓ أَزْوَٰجِ أَدْعِيَآئِهِمْ إِذَا قَضَوْا۟ مِنْهُنَّ وَطَرًا وَكَانَ أَمْرُ ٱللَّهِ مَفْعُولًا

*Wa idh taqūlu li'lladhī anʿama'llāhu ʿalayhi wa anʿamta ʿalayhi amsik ʿalayka zawjaka wa'ttaqi'llāha wa tukhfī fī nafsika ma'llāhu mubdīhi wa takhsha'l-nāsa wa'llāhu aḥaqqu an takhshāh, fa-lammā qaḍā Zaydun minhā waṭaran zawwajnākahā li-kay lā yakūna ʿala'l-muʾminīna ḥarajun fī azwāji adʿiyāʾihim idhā qaḍaw minhunna waṭarā, wa kāna amru'llāhi mafʿūlā*

"And when you were saying to the one whom God had favoured and whom you had favoured, 'Keep your wife with you, and guard yourself against God.' You were hiding within yourself what God was to bring out, and you were wary of people; whereas God was the one you should have been wary of. Then, when Zayd had ended his tie with her, We married her to you, so that there should be no difficulty for the believers regarding the wives of their adopted sons, once they have ended their tie with them. And the command of God is carried out."

### How I shall read this section

**This verse is one of the most disputed and most abused places in the Qurʾān. I state the method in advance:**

**One.** The account's link with the ruling in verses 4-5 will be shown — the sūra lays down a principle and recounts its application.
**Two.** **The imputations circulating in the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* and story-telling literature about the expression *tukhfī fī nafsika* will not be entered into.** That these imputations were rejected in classical exegesis will be recorded in the language of "it is transmitted", and **I shall keep within what the wording of the verse says.**
**Three.** The woman's name will not be given; the details of the reports about how the marriage was made and how it ended will not be entered into. **The only name the Qurʾān gives is Zayd.**

### The only Companion named in the Qurʾān

**As was recorded at `111-tebbet.md`: Zayd is named by name at al-Aḥzāb 33/37 — the only place where a Companion's name occurs in the Qurʾān.** I rely on that treatment.

**And this is a fact worth recording.** The Qurʾān scarcely ever names the people of its own period — neither friend nor enemy. The reason was recorded at `063-munafikun.md`: *"the description lasts, the exposure passes."*

**And precisely for that reason, where the single exception is made matters.**

**In the verse Zayd's name occurs at the very middle of the sentence: *fa-lammā qaḍā Zaydun minhā waṭarā*.** And up to that moment the same person had been named not by his name but by a description: *"the one whom God had favoured and whom you had favoured."*

**I record this as my own reading:** the place where the verse gives the name is the place where the ruling is established. **A name is written into the Qurʾān in the very context in which the ascription that name carried (Zayd b. Muḥammad) is abolished.** That is, what is abolished is not a name but a corrected ascription; and in doing this the Qurʾān enters the person's **own name** into the record.

**This is an inference and not a transmitted report.** Its ground is the position of the name within the verse.

### أَنْعَمَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَأَنْعَمْتَ عَلَيْهِ — two favours
