**The same verb (*anʿama*), with two different subjects, one after the other.**

**The verse does not say what they were.** In classical exegesis it is said that the first was guidance and the second a kindness the Prophet had done him. **I do not enter into detail.**

**But the word order does a piece of work and I record it:** the verse introduces the person by way of a **relationship.** And both directions of the relationship are named: what came from God and what came from the Prophet.

**And this introduction weighs down the situation that follows in the verse:** a word said to a person to whom kindness has been done is not an ordinary piece of advice.

### أَمْسِكْ عَلَيْكَ زَوْجَكَ وَٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ — the word that was said

**أَمْسِكْ — root م-س-ك.** To hold, to keep in hand, not to let go. *Mask* — holding; *imsāk* — holding, keeping back (the *imsāk* of fasting is from the same root).

**عَلَيْكَ — "with you".** The preposition shows that the holding is within a field belonging to the person.

**The construction occurs in the Qurʾān in the context of divorce too:** *"Either holding on in a fitting way (*imsāk bi-maʿrūf*) or releasing in a fair way"* (al-Baqara 2/229). **That is, *imsāk* is the opposite of *tasrīḥ* (which occurred in verse 28).**

**And this link is worth recording within the sūra:** in the twenty-eighth verse a *sarāḥan jamīlā* was offered to the Prophet's wives; in the thirty-seventh the Prophet advises *imsāk* to someone else. **The same pair of words, in two different places.**

**وَٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ** — the very same command as in the first verse of the sūra. **There it was said to the Prophet; here the Prophet says it to someone else.**

### وَتُخْفِى فِى نَفْسِكَ مَا ٱللَّهُ مُبْدِيهِ

**This is the most disputed place in the verse, and here, per the method (`STYLE.md`), I draw a firm limit.**

**What the wording of the verse says:**

1. The Prophet was **hiding** something within himself.
2. God was going to **bring it out**.
3. The Prophet was **wary** of people.
4. The one to be wary of was God.

**What the wording of the verse does not say:** **what** the thing hidden was. The verse does not give it.

**In the classical exegetical literature there are two kinds of material on this, and the two must be kept apart.**

**The first kind — imputations of story-telling and *Isrāʾīliyyāt* origin.** In this literature detailed and tendentious narratives circulate about what the hidden thing was.

**This commentary does not enter into these narratives**, and I record this: **it is widely transmitted that in the classical exegetical tradition these imputations were rejected.** A considerable number of exegetes characterised these narratives as material with no source value, without a chain of transmission, or inconsistent under textual criticism, and rejected them.

**I give this in the language of "it is transmitted" and mention no name or wording,** because the note in `STYLE.md` is plain: a saying of which one is not sure is not ascribed to an exegete.

**The second kind — explanations resting on the wording of the verse.** In classical exegesis there are also explanations of what the hidden thing may have been that keep within the verse's own sentences:

| Explanation | What it says | Its ground in the verse |
|---|---|---|
| **Knowledge of a coming ruling** | The Prophet knew that he had been informed this marriage would take place, and did not disclose it because he was wary of people's reaction | *ma'llāhu mubdīh* — "what God was to bring out"; and the clause of ground at the end of the verse |
| **That the marriage would end** | The thing hidden was knowledge that that marriage would not continue | The same sentence |

**I do not impose a preference.** But I record this: **the first explanation agrees with the verse's own clause of ground.** The verse writes the **reason** for the event explicitly: *li-kay lā yakūna ʿala'l-muʾminīna ḥarajun fī azwāji adʿiyāʾihim.* That is, the ground the verse gives is legal and links directly with verses 4-5.

**This is an observation, not a ruling.**

### وَتَخْشَى ٱلنَّاسَ وَٱللَّهُ أَحَقُّ أَن تَخْشَىٰهُ — a correction

**خَشِىَ — root خ-ش-ي.** To fear; and what the lexicographers record about its difference from *khawf*: *khashya* is fear resting on knowledge and on reverence. *Khawf* is more general.

**And this sentence is one of the examples of a fact treated at `080-abese.md`.** What was recorded there and what I bring over here:

- **There are places in the Qurʾān where a choice of the Prophet's is corrected**, and they were given as a list: al-Tawba 9/43, al-Anfāl 8/67-68, al-Aḥzāb 33/37, al-Taḥrīm 66/1.
- **This was presented not as a proof but as an observation.**
- It was also recorded there that in a report stated to be found in al-Bukhārī and Muslim it is transmitted that ʿĀʾisha said of this verse something to the effect that had Muhammad been going to conceal anything of the revelation, he would have concealed this verse — **its wording not quoted verbatim, its sense given.**

**I rely on that treatment and do not repeat that section.**

**I add one thing here and record it as my own reading:** what the verse corrects is not a **sin** but an **order of priority.** In the sentence the elative *aḥaqqu* (having more right) is used — that is, being wary of people is not forbidden, it is **put in order.**

**And this links with the first verse of the sūra:** *"do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites."* There too the matter was aligning oneself with people's expectations. **The sūra repeats the same matter thirty-six verses later for the Prophet himself.**

I record this link as my own reading; its ground is that both verses fix an attitude in the face of "people's reaction".

### فَلَمَّا قَضَىٰ زَيْدٌ مِّنْهَا وَطَرًا — the delicacy of the expression

**وَطَر — root و-ط-ر.** The need, want or business a person looks for from a thing. *Qaḍā waṭarahu* — he finished his business, he met his need.

**The construction occurs only in this verse in the Qurʾān — and twice in this verse.**

**And the choice of the expression is worth recording.** The verse does not say "he divorced her" (*ṭallaqa*). **The expression it uses is a veiled way of stating that the relationship had in fact ended.**

**This is the level of language the Qurʾān prefers in such matters** and it agrees with the measure recorded at `073-muzzemmil.md`: in matters that enter the field of privacy the Qurʾān chooses indirect expression.

**And the expression occurs twice in the verse so that at the second occurrence it may be tied to the general ruling:** *idhā qaḍaw minhunna waṭarā*. **The same expression, once a single event and once a general rule.** The word order makes the passage from event to ruling by repeating the word.

### زَوَّجْنَٰكَهَا — the naming of the agent

**The verb is first person plural: "We married her to you."**

**This is the most striking grammatical fact of the verse and must be recorded.** The sentence does not say "he married her" or "you married her". **The agent of the verb is stated directly.**

**And this has a function:** the event is presented not as a personal choice but as **the application of a ruling.** Indeed the verse gives the ground immediately afterwards.

I record this as a datum of the text.

### لِكَىْ لَا يَكُونَ عَلَى ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ حَرَجٌ — the clause of ground

**This is the most important sentence in the verse, because the verse itself says *why* the event took place.**

**حَرَج — root ح-ر-ج.** And the concrete sense of the root is **narrowness, constriction**: *ḥaraj* — a place hard to get into, cramped; ground thick with trees that gives no passage.

**The Qurʾān uses the root in several places:** *"He has laid upon you no difficulty (*ḥaraj*) in religion"* (al-Ḥajj 22/78); *"his breast becomes tight and constricted (*ḥarajan*)"* (al-Anʿām 6/125).

**And the clause of ground links directly with the fourth and fifth verses:**

| Verse | What it did |
|---|---|
| **33/4** | *mā jaʿala adʿiyāʾakum abnāʾakum* — adopted sons are not sons by birth |
| **33/5** | *udʿūhum li-ābāʾihim* — call them by their fathers |
| **33/37** | *li-kay lā yakūna ʿala'l-muʾminīna ḥarajun **fī azwāji adʿiyāʾihim*** |

**The same word — أَدْعِيَآء — occurs in all three verses.** And this completes the structure of the sūra: **the principle was laid down (4-5), its application recounted (37).**

**And why the application was needed can be understood:** the legal abolition of an institution does not of itself abolish that institution's social effect. It had been said that an adopted son is not counted a son; but in people's minds the ascription went on. **The verse puts an end to that continuation by an application.**

**And this is the hardest test of the sūra's founding principle, and I record it:** the fourth verse had said "a name does not change reality". But there is something a name establishes **socially**, and that is not undone by a sentence. **The sūra is aware of this and therefore follows the principle with an application.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the clause of ground is given explicitly inside the verse.

### وَكَانَ أَمْرُ ٱللَّهِ مَفْعُولًا — the verse-ending

**مَفْعُول — a passive participle:** "that which has been done". **That is, the command is named not as something still to be done but as something done.**

**The construction occurs in several places in the Qurʾān** and always conveys a certainty (al-Nisāʾ 4/47; al-Anfāl 8/42).

### A note on the details of the reports

**A great many details circulate about this verse in the classical and modern literature:** how the marriage was made, what the parties thought, the cause of the separation, the date of the event.

**These details are not entered into in this commentary.** The reasons:

**One.** The reports differ from one another at the level of detail, and per the method (`STYLE.md`) a detail of which one is not sure cannot be presented as certain knowledge.

**Two.** **The wording of the verse gives none of these details.** To make a piece of information the Qurʾān does not give into a condition of a verse's meaning is a breach of the three-layered frame set up at `048-fetih.md`.

**Three.** The verse gives its ground **itself.** When the reason for an event is written in the text, looking outside for another reason is unnecessary.

### What this says to the present

**One. The abolition of an institution and the abolition of its effect are different things.**

This is exactly the ground the verse gives. The legal regulation has been made (4-5); but people's behaviour continues. **And to change the behaviour the verse does not write another legal text — it shows an application.**

This can be recorded as an observation about social change: **for a habit to fall away it is not enough for the rule to change; it must be seen that the rule works.**

**Two. Being wary of people's reaction.**

The most human sentence in the verse is *wa takhsha'l-nās*. And the verse does not stamp this as a weakness; **it corrects it as a matter of order.**

Its present-day form is plain: **the most common obstacle to doing a thing one knows to be right is not believing that the thing is wrong; it is being wary of how it will be received.** The verse does not pretend this wariness away — it only says which wariness has priority.

---

## 33/38-39 — مَّا كَانَ عَلَى ٱلنَّبِىِّ مِنْ حَرَجٍ فِيمَا فَرَضَ ٱللَّهُ لَهُۥ سُنَّةَ ٱللَّهِ فِى ٱلَّذِينَ خَلَوْا۟ مِن قَبْلُ وَكَانَ أَمْرُ ٱللَّهِ قَدَرًا مَّقْدُورًا · ٱلَّذِينَ يُبَلِّغُونَ رِسَٰلَٰتِ ٱللَّهِ وَيَخْشَوْنَهُۥ وَلَا يَخْشَوْنَ أَحَدًا إِلَّا ٱللَّهَ وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ حَسِيبًا

*Mā kāna ʿala'l-nabiyyi min ḥarajin fīmā faraḍa'llāhu lah, sunnata'llāhi fi'lladhīna khalaw min qabl, wa kāna amru'llāhi qadaran maqdūrā · Alladhīna yuballighūna risālāti'llāhi wa yakhshawnahu wa lā yakhshawna aḥadan illa'llāh, wa kafā bi'llāhi ḥasībā*

"There is no difficulty for the Prophet in what God has laid down for him. This is God's way with those who passed away before. And God's command is a decree determined. · They are those who deliver the messages of God and are in awe of Him and are in awe of no one but God. And God suffices as a reckoner."

### The repetition of the word حَرَج

**In the thirty-seventh verse the ground was the removal of a *ḥaraj* — for the believers. In the thirty-eighth the same word is used of the Prophet.**

| Verse | Who | What |
|---|---|---|
| **37** | *ʿala'l-muʾminīna* | That there be no difficulty regarding the wives of adopted sons |
| **38** | *ʿala'l-nabiyyi* | No difficulty in what has been laid down |

**The same word, on both sides within two verses.** The word order shows that the ruling is applied to both sides in the same way.

I record this as my own reading.

### فَرَضَ — root ف-ر-ض

**The concrete sense of the root must be dwelt on: *faraḍa* — to cut a thing and make it distinct, to make a notch.** *Furḍa* — a notch, a nick. From this comes the sense "to determine, to appoint, to make obligatory".

**That is, *farḍ* is in its root an act of *cutting* and *marking out*** — the marking of a thing's limit.

**And this agrees with the continuation of the verse:** *qadaran maqdūrā* — a decree determined. **Both words carry the sense of "determining".**

### سُنَّةَ ٱللَّهِ — sunnat Allāh

**Root: س-ن-ن.** And the concrete sense of the root is **to make a thing flow or run smoothly over a surface**: *sanna'l-māʾ* — he made the water flow; *sinān* — a spearhead (sharpened); *misann* — a whetstone.

**From this comes the sense "a way, a custom, a course that runs on":** a road walked over again and again and thereby made distinct.

**This concept was treated in detail at `048-fetih.md` (48/23), and verse 62 of this sūra was taken into the table there. I rely on that treatment.**

**The note recorded there and needing repetition here:** *"The application of the concept of sunnat Allāh to contemporary events was not entered into; it was recorded that the concept is a measure for understanding the past, not an authority for judging the present."*

**In this sūra *sunnat Allāh* occurs twice:**

| Verse | Context |
|---|---|
| **38** | That there be no difficulty in what is laid down for the prophets |
| **62** | The ruling about those who spread baseless news |

**And what the two occurrences have in common is worth recording:** both come **after a regulation.** The sūra presents the rulings it brings not as "new" but as the continuation of a course that has always run.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that both occurrences stand in the same position.

**فِى ٱلَّذِينَ خَلَوْا۟ مِن قَبْلُ** — **خَلَا, root خ-ل-و**: to be empty, to pass away. **And the same expression occurred at `002-bakara.md` 2/214:** *alladhīna khalaw min qablikum* — "those who passed away before you."

**In both verses the same expression with the same function:** a reference to a pattern in the past.

### قَدَرًا مَّقْدُورًا — a measure

**Root: ق-د-ر.** And the primary sense of the root is **to measure, to determine the quantity of**. It was treated as the name of a sūra at `097-kadr.md`.

**And the construction is a reinforcement of the cognate-accusative kind:** *qadaran maqdūrā* — "a determined determination". Two words from the same root.

**The Qurʾān uses such repetitions of a root for emphasis** and it has already been seen five times in this sūra (11, 23, 33, 36, and here).

### ٱلَّذِينَ يُبَلِّغُونَ رِسَٰلَٰتِ ٱللَّهِ — the description of the prophets

**بَلَّغَ — root ب-ل-غ.** To reach, to arrive; in Form II, "to make reach". **And this root occurred in the tenth verse too:** *balaghati'l-qulūbu'l-ḥanājir* — "the hearts **reached** the throats".

**The same root, once the place fear reaches, once the delivering of a message.** I record it.

**رِسَٰلَٰت — plural.** The plural of *risāla*. **That it comes in the plural is worth noting:** not a single message but messages.

### وَلَا يَخْشَوْنَ أَحَدًا إِلَّا ٱللَّهَ — the answer to the thirty-seventh verse

**This sentence is the direct counterpart of the expression *takhsha'l-nās* in the thirty-seventh verse.**

| Verse | Expression | What |
|---|---|---|
| **37** | *wa takhsha'l-nāsa* | Being wary of people (corrected) |
| **37** | *wa'llāhu aḥaqqu an takhshāh* | The one who should be feared |
| **39** | *wa lā yakhshawna aḥadan illa'llāh* | The description of the prophets |

**Three expressions, within two verses, from the same root (خ-ش-ي).** And the order describes a movement: a correction, a measure, a description.

**That is, the thirty-ninth verse sets down what is corrected in the thirty-seventh as the general attribute of the prophets.** I record it as my own reading.

**And the word *aḥad* occurs in the sūra for the second time after the thirty-second verse** — there *ka-aḥadin mina'l-nisāʾ*, here *aḥadan illa'llāh*. **As was recorded at `112-ihlas.md`, in a negative context the word means "no one at all", and that is exactly its sense here.**

### وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ حَسِيبًا — the verse-ending

**The pattern كَفَىٰ بِـ** for the third time in this sūra (3, 25, 39).

**حَسِيب — root ح-س-ب.** To count, to reckon. **It occurred in the twentieth verse as *yaḥsabūna*** — there a mistaken reckoning, here the one who takes the reckoning.

**The same root, once a person's erring count, once the one who sees the reckoning rightly.** I record it.

---

## 33/40 — مَّا كَانَ مُحَمَّدٌ أَبَآ أَحَدٍ مِّن رِّجَالِكُمْ وَلَٰكِن رَّسُولَ ٱللَّهِ وَخَاتَمَ ٱلنَّبِيِّـۧنَ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ بِكُلِّ شَىْءٍ عَلِيمًا

*Mā kāna Muḥammadun abā aḥadin min rijālikum wa lākin rasūla'llāhi wa khātama'l-nabiyyīn, wa kāna'llāhu bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīmā*

"Muhammad is not the father of any of your men; but he is the Messenger of God and the seal of the prophets. God is Knowing of all things."

### One of the densest verses of the sūra

**This verse does two separate things in the same sentence**, and that the two are in the same sentence is no accident:

1. It abolishes a claim of **lineage**.
2. It states a **standing**.

**And the two are joined by *wa lākin* (but).** That is, the sentence begins with a negation and ends with an affirmation.

### مَّا كَانَ مُحَمَّدٌ أَبَآ أَحَدٍ مِّن رِّجَالِكُمْ

**The sentence is the direct complement of the fourth verse.**

| Verse | The sentence | What |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *mā jaʿala adʿiyāʾakum abnāʾakum* | The general ruling: adopted sons are not sons |
| **40** | *mā kāna Muḥammadun abā aḥadin min rijālikum* | The same ruling, applied concretely |

**The fourth verse said "they are not your sons" — from the side of the son. The fortieth says "he is not the father" — from the side of the father.** The principle is completed from both ends.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two sentences take up the same relationship from two directions.

**مِّن رِّجَالِكُمْ — "of your men".** The word *rajul* occurs in this sūra for the third time (4, 23, 40).

**And that the word is *rijāl* (grown men) is recorded in classical exegesis:** the expression is taken to indicate that the Prophet had no male child who reached adulthood. **This is a reading drawn from the wording of the verse and is widespread in classical exegesis; I do not present it as a settled ruling.**

**The name "Muhammad" occurs in four places in the Qurʾān:** Āl ʿImrān 3/144, al-Aḥzāb 33/40, Muḥammad 47/2, al-Fatḥ 48/29. **This note was given at `111-tebbet.md` and at `048-fetih.md`.**

### وَخَاتَمَ ٱلنَّبِيِّـۧنَ — analysis of the root

**Root: خ-ت-م.** **This root was treated in detail at `002-bakara.md` (2/7, *khatama'llāhu ʿalā qulūbihim*) and this construction was mentioned there too. I rely on that treatment and bring what was recorded there over to here.**

What was recorded there:

- **The root: to come to the end of a thing, and to set a seal.**
- **From the same root comes *khātam* — a signet ring; and this is the *khātam al-nabiyyīn* used of the Prophet.**
- **A seal does two things: it closes and it authenticates.** When an envelope is sealed it both becomes impossible to enter and is declared to have a settled content.

**And now these two functions must be applied to this construction.**

**The concrete picture of the root:** a *khātam* is the seal pressed on the mouth of a letter. And a seal does two things to a letter: it states that **the writing has ended**, and it authenticates that **the writing is genuine.**

| What the seal does | Its counterpart in the construction |
|---|---|
| **It closes** | The end has been reached |
| **It authenticates** | Those before have been confirmed |

**Both of these aspects are recorded in classical exegesis** and they do not exclude one another.

**And one detail about the picture of the seal is worth recording:** the seal is pressed on the **outside** of the letter but is not the letter **itself**. **That is, the seal is not a part of the text; it is the sign that the text is complete.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the concrete sense of the root.

### The difference of reading: خَاتَم / خَاتِم

**There is a difference of variant reading in this word and it must be recorded.**

| Reading | Pattern | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **خَاتَم** (*khātam*, with *fatḥa* on the *tāʾ*) | A noun — in the pattern of an instrument noun | **Seal** — the instrument that seals, a signet ring |
| **خَاتِم** (*khātim*, with *kasra* on the *tāʾ*) | An active participle | **Sealer** — the one who does the sealing |

**Both readings are found among the readers.** The widespread reading is *khātam*; the reading *khātim* is also transmitted.

**The difference of meaning is fine and is recorded in classical exegesis thus:** in the reading *khātam* the emphasis falls on the **instrument** (being a seal), in the reading *khātim* on the **act** (the sealing).

**Both readings come out at the same place** and no preference is adopted in this commentary.

### ٱلنَّبِيِّـۧن — the prophets

**Root: ن-ب-أ.** News. *Nabī* — the one who brings news; or, on one view, from the root ن-ب-و, "the one who is high".

**And that the construction uses the word *nabī* and not *rasūl* is worth noting.** The verse uses both words in the same sentence: *rasūla'llāhi* and *khātama'l-nabiyyīn*.

**The difference between the two words has been debated in classical exegesis** and no conclusion has been reached. One of the most widespread explanations is this: *nubuwwa* is a wider and *risāla* a narrower concept — every messenger is a prophet, not every prophet a messenger. **This explanation is disputed and no ruling is issued in this commentary.**

**But the datum the word order gives is this:** the verse ties the note of the seal to the concept **said to be the wider one.** This is an observation.

### On the extent of the construction

**What this construction means is a matter of theology in Islamic thought and goes beyond the limits of this commentary.**

**What emerges from the wording of the text is this:** Muhammad is the seal of the prophets. **And in its root the seal carries both the sense of an end and the sense of authentication.**

**Different interpretations have been put forward on this through history, and they have been a subject of division among schools and movements. This commentary does not enter that debate and makes no insinuation against any group.**

Per the note in `STYLE.md`: **the disagreement is not concealed, but no polemic is entered.** The existence of the disagreement is recorded here; its content and its parties are not the subject of this commentary.

### The place of the verse in the sūra — the closing of the ring

**And now the most striking structural fact of the sūra.**

In the fourth verse the sūra laid down a principle: **a name does not change reality.** And it gave three examples: two hearts, ẓihār, the adopted son.

**The fortieth verse closes the third of those three examples** — and in closing it uses **the same structure**:

| | **33/4** | **33/40** |
|---|---|---|
| **The pattern** | *mā jaʿala…* | *mā kāna…* |
| **What is denied** | That an adopted son is a son by birth | That a particular person is a father |
| **What follows** | *dhālikum qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* | *wa lākin rasūla'llāhi wa khātama'l-nabiyyīn* |

**And the difference is worth recording:** in the fourth verse a **diagnosis** follows the denial (this is your saying). In the fortieth a **standing** follows the denial.

**That is, the sūra leaves no gap in place of the ascription it removes.** A father's ascription is removed and two standings are put in its place: messengership and the seal.

**And this is what the sūra has been doing from the beginning:** in the fifth verse lineage was removed and brotherhood and being a *mawlā* were put in its place; in the sixth precedence in inheritance was regulated and the door of kindness was opened in its place; in the fortieth fatherhood is removed and messengership put in its place.

**In removing one bond the sūra always establishes another.** I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the same structure in the three verses.

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse is what determines who a person is.**

The sentence proposes two definitions and rejects one: **fatherhood** (definition through lineage) is rejected, **messengership** (definition through an office) is set in its place.

**And it agrees with the sūra as a whole:** since the fourth verse the sūra has been dealing with false identities built on lineage and ascription. **The fortieth verse applies the same measure to its own prophet.**

I record this as my own reading. **And I enter one further note:** that a text applies its own measure to its own founder may be counted a datum about the seriousness of that measure. This is not a proof but an observation — it was given at `080-abese.md` with the same note of caution.

---

## 33/41-44 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ ٱذْكُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ذِكْرًا كَثِيرًا · وَسَبِّحُوهُ بُكْرَةً وَأَصِيلًا · هُوَ ٱلَّذِى يُصَلِّى عَلَيْكُمْ وَمَلَٰٓئِكَتُهُۥ لِيُخْرِجَكُم مِّنَ ٱلظُّلُمَٰتِ إِلَى ٱلنُّورِ وَكَانَ بِٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ رَحِيمًا · تَحِيَّتُهُمْ يَوْمَ يَلْقَوْنَهُۥ سَلَٰمٌ وَأَعَدَّ لَهُمْ أَجْرًا كَرِيمًا

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanu'dhkuru'llāha dhikran kathīrā · Wa sabbiḥūhu bukratan wa aṣīlā · Huwa'lladhī yuṣallī ʿalaykum wa malāʾikatuhu li-yukhrijakum mina'l-ẓulumāti ila'l-nūr, wa kāna bi'l-muʾminīna raḥīmā · Taḥiyyatuhum yawma yalqawnahu salām, wa aʿadda lahum ajran karīmā*

"O you who believe! Remember God much. · And glorify Him morning and evening. · It is He who sends *ṣalāt* upon you — and His angels — to bring you out of the darknesses into the light. He is Merciful to the believers. · Their greeting on the day they meet Him is 'peace'; and He has prepared for them a noble reward."

### The function of the block

**The Zayd section is over. Here the sūra takes a *breath*.**

The verses from forty-one to forty-eight are the gentlest part of the sūra, and they give not a regulation but a **direction**.

**And this is a fact in the structure of the sūra that must be recorded:** between and after the two heaviest sections (the siege and Zayd) a verse of remembrance or of exemplarity has always been placed.

| Block | What follows |
|---|---|
| The siege (9-20) | *Uswa ḥasana* (21) |
| The household and the general principle (28-35) | — |
| Zayd (36-40) | The block of remembrance (41-48) |

I record this as my own reading.

### ٱذْكُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ذِكْرًا كَثِيرًا

**The condition of the twenty-first verse turns here into a command.**

| Verse | Expression | Mood |
|---|---|---|
| **21** | *wa dhakara'llāha kathīrā* | A condition — for the exemplarity to work |
| **41** | *udhkuru'llāha dhikran kathīrā* | A command — reinforced with a cognate accusative |

**And it occurred in the thirty-fifth verse too as an attribute:** *al-dhākirīna'llāha kathīran wa'l-dhākirāt.*

**That is, the sūra says the same thing three times in three different moods:** condition, attribute, command. I record it.

**ذِكْرًا كَثِيرًا — again a cognate accusative.** The sixth reinforcement in the sūra.

**And that the Qurʾān enters a note of "much" for remembrance is striking.** The Qurʾān generally sets no quantity to acts of worship; here there is a note of quantity. **And the measure is "much" — not a particular number.**

**This is worth recording:** the note is quantitative but not numerical. That is, it conveys not a lower limit but an **orientation**.

### وَسَبِّحُوهُ بُكْرَةً وَأَصِيلًا

**سَبَّحَ — root س-ب-ح.** And the concrete sense of the root is **to swim**: *sabaḥa fi'l-māʾ* — he swam in the water. The Qurʾān uses the root of the heavenly bodies too: *"Each swims in an orbit"* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/33 — *yasbaḥūn*).

**The lexicographers dwell on how the root passes from "to swim" to "to declare free of imperfection":** *tasbīḥ* is to **remove** a thing from what does not befit it — as the swimmer passes through the water and moves away.

**بُكْرَة وَأَصِيل** — morning and evening. **This pair was recorded at `048-fetih.md`** and this verse was given there as an example: *"The Qurʾān uses the same pair in several places: 'Glorify Him morning and evening' (al-Aḥzāb 33/42)."* I rely on that treatment.

**أَصِيل — root أ-ص-ل.** *Aṣl* — a root, a foundation. *Aṣīl* — the end of the day, the time from the afternoon to the evening. **The lexicographers do not establish with certainty the link between the "root" sense of the word and the sense "end of day"; I record it but build no meaning on it.**

### هُوَ ٱلَّذِى يُصَلِّى عَلَيْكُمْ وَمَلَٰٓئِكَتُهُۥ

**This sentence is the herald of the fifty-sixth verse, and the analysis of the root ص-ل-و will be made there.**

**But a fact of word order must be recorded here.**

| Verse | Who | To whom |
|---|---|---|
| **33/43** | God and His angels | **To the believers** |
| **33/56** | God and His angels | **To the Prophet** |

**The same structure, in the same sūra, with two different recipients.** And the forty-third verse comes **before** the fifty-sixth.

**This is an order worth recording:** the sūra first states the *ṣalāt* made upon the believers, then the one made upon the Prophet. I record this as my own reading.

### لِيُخْرِجَكُم مِّنَ ٱلظُّلُمَٰتِ إِلَى ٱلنُّورِ — the ground

**The construction is a recurring pattern in the Qurʾān** (al-Baqara 2/257; Ibrāhīm 14/1; al-Ḥadīd 57/9 — **treated at `057-hadid.md`**; al-Ṭalāq 65/11).

**And the pattern has an invariable feature, worth recording:** *ẓulumāt* is always **plural** and *nūr* always **singular**.

**In classical exegesis this is tied to the manyness of darkness and the oneness of light:** there are many ways of leaving the road, and the right road has a single direction.

**I record this explanation; I do not present it as a settled ruling** — but the fact (the plural/singular contrast) is consistent in the Qurʾān and is a countable datum.

### تَحِيَّتُهُمْ يَوْمَ يَلْقَوْنَهُۥ سَلَٰمٌ

**تَحِيَّة — root ح-ي-ي.** And the primary sense of the root is **life**. *Taḥiyya* is originally a greeting meaning "I wish you life, long life".

**That is, in the root of the word there is a *wish*** — the good wish said on meeting.

**سَلَٰم — root س-ل-م.** For the third time in this sūra (in 22 as *taslīm*, in 35 as *muslimīn*, here as *salām*).

**And the concrete sense of the root — wholeness, completeness — is at work in all three occurrences:**

| Verse | Word | What |
|---|---|---|
| **22** | *taslīmā* | Leaving oneself wholly |
| **35** | *al-muslimīna wa'l-muslimāt* | Those who submit |
| **44** | *salām* | The greeting |

**The sūra opens the root as a state and closes it as a recompense.** I record this as my own reading.

**أَجْرًا كَرِيمًا** — in the thirty-first verse *rizqan karīmā* occurred; here *ajran karīmā*. **The same adjective with two different nouns.**

---

## 33/45-48 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ إِنَّآ أَرْسَلْنَٰكَ شَٰهِدًا وَمُبَشِّرًا وَنَذِيرًا · وَدَاعِيًا إِلَى ٱللَّهِ بِإِذْنِهِۦ وَسِرَاجًا مُّنِيرًا · وَبَشِّرِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ بِأَنَّ لَهُم مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ فَضْلًا كَبِيرًا · وَلَا تُطِعِ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ وَٱلْمُنَٰفِقِينَ وَدَعْ أَذَىٰهُمْ وَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ وَكِيلًا

*Yā ayyuha'l-nabiyyu innā arsalnāka shāhidan wa mubashshiran wa nadhīrā · Wa dāʿiyan ila'llāhi bi-idhnihī wa sirājan munīrā · Wa bashshiri'l-muʾminīna bi-anna lahum mina'llāhi faḍlan kabīrā · Wa lā tuṭiʿi'l-kāfirīna wa'l-munāfiqīna wa daʿ adhāhum wa tawakkal ʿala'llāh, wa kafā bi'llāhi wakīlā*

"O Prophet! We have sent you as a witness, a bringer of good news and a warner; · and as one calling to God by His leave, and as an illuminating lamp. · Give the believers the good news that they have a great bounty from God. · And do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites; pay no heed to their injury; and rely on God. God suffices as a trustee."

### The closing of the ring

**These four verses close a ring with the first three verses of the sūra**, and the ring is almost exact.

| | **33/1-3** | **33/45-48** |
|---|---|---|
| **The address** | *yā ayyuha'l-nabiyy* | *yā ayyuha'l-nabiyy* |
| **The prohibition** | *lā tuṭiʿi'l-kāfirīna wa'l-munāfiqīn* | *lā tuṭiʿi'l-kāfirīna wa'l-munāfiqīn* |
| **The command** | *tawakkal ʿala'llāh* | *tawakkal ʿala'llāh* |
| **The verse-ending** | *wa kafā bi'llāhi wakīlā* | *wa kafā bi'llāhi wakīlā* |

**The same verse-ending, the same prohibition, the same command.**

**And there are two differences, both worth recording:**

**One. In the forty-eighth verse there is an addition: *wa daʿ adhāhum*** — "pay no heed to their injury". **It was not there at the head of the sūra.**

**Two. In the forty-fifth and forty-sixth verses five attributes are enumerated.** They were not enumerated at the head of the sūra.

**That is, the sūra returns to the same frame but has added two things inside it:** a definition and a note of endurance. **And what happened in the forty verses between required exactly this:** a siege, an internal separation, a heavy regulation.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the verbal overlap of the two blocks and their differences.

### Five attributes

**This verse was already mentioned at `048-fetih.md`:** *"O Prophet! We have sent you as a witness, a bringer of good news and a warner… (al-Aḥzāb 33/45-46). The same three words, in the same order; there two further attributes are added."* I rely on that treatment.

| # | Attribute | Root | What |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | **شَاهِد** | ش-ه-د | A witness — one who sees and states what he has seen |
| **2** | **مُبَشِّر** | ب-ش-ر | One who brings good news |
| **3** | **نَذِير** | ن-ذ-ر | One who warns |
| **4** | **دَاعِيًا إِلَى ٱللَّهِ** | د-ع-و | One who calls |
| **5** | **سِرَاجًا مُّنِيرًا** | س-ر-ج | An illuminating lamp |

**The root ب-ش-ر:** *bashara* — skin, the surface of the body. *Bushrā* — good news; and the link is this: the **skin of the face** of a person who receives good news changes. **That is, good news is in the root of the word a change of countenance.**

**The root ن-ذ-ر:** *nadhr* — a vow; and *indhār* — warning. The lexicographers join the two on the common ground of "binding oneself to a thing / making someone aware of a thing".

**The root د-ع-و — for the fourth time in this sūra.** It occurred in the fourth and fifth verses as *adʿiyāʾ* and *udʿūhum*.

**And this repetition is worth recording:** the same root was, at the head of the sūra, the name of a **false ascription** (*adʿiyāʾ* — adopted sons), and here it is the name of a **true call** (*dāʿiyan ila'llāh*).

**The root is "to call"; and the sūra corrects the direction of the call twice:** call the child by his father (5), call people to God (46).

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the four occurrences of the root in the sūra.

### سِرَاجًا مُّنِيرًا — a lamp

**Root: س-ر-ج.** A lamp, a thing that gives light.

**This word was treated at `071-nuh.md`** and the finding recorded there is worth bringing over: *"the Qurʾān uses the word *sirāj* of the Prophet as well — 'an illuminating lamp' (*sirājan munīrā*, al-Aḥzāb 33/46). There, of course, no burning body is meant. The word conveys not a physical mechanism but the function of being a source."*

**The Qurʾān uses the word of the sun too** (Nūḥ 71/16; al-Furqān 25/61; al-Nabaʾ 78/13 — there *sirājan wahhājā*).

**And a difference is worth recording:** for the sun the adjective *wahhāj* (scorching, fierce) is used, for the Prophet *munīr* (illuminating).

**I give this difference as an observation;** I build no forced meaning upon it.

### وَدَعْ أَذَىٰهُمْ — "pay no heed to their injury"

**The sūra's axis of injury begins here.**

**The root أ-ذ-ي:** to disturb, to hurt. **The distinction the lexicographers record is important:** *adhā* is not a great harm; it is **a light but continual annoyance.** Less than *ḍarar*, but more wearing.

**The Qurʾān uses the root in this sense:** *"They can do you no harm beyond an injury"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/111 — *lan yaḍurrūkum illā adhā*). **The verse plainly distinguishes *adhā* from *ḍarar*.**

**And the root occurs seven times in this sūra** (48, 53 twice, 57, 58, 59, 69). **This is one of the densest uses within a single sūra in the Qurʾān.**

**دَعْ — root و-د-ع.** To leave, to abandon. *Daʿ* — leave it.

**And the form of the command is worth recording:** the verse does not say "do not answer them" or "be patient". **It says "leave it".**

**The difference is this:** patience is carrying something. **To leave is not to carry it.** The verse commands the second.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the choice of the verb.

### What this says to the present

**The two commands of the forty-eighth verse together describe an attitude:** not falling into line, and paying no heed.

**And the relationship between the two is worth recording.** The most common price a person pays for not falling into line with a group is the continual small annoyances that come from that group — *adhā*. And these annoyances, taken one by one, are trivial; **their sum is a pressure.**

**The verse's solution is not to answer back:** *daʿ* — leave it. **That is, the verse asks in the same sentence both for not falling into line and for not reacting.**

**And for this to be possible a third command comes:** *tawakkal ʿala'llāh*. As at the head of the sūra, the note of leaving the outcome is what makes the attitude bearable.

---

## 33/49 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوٓا۟ إِذَا نَكَحْتُمُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ ثُمَّ طَلَّقْتُمُوهُنَّ مِن قَبْلِ أَن تَمَسُّوهُنَّ فَمَا لَكُمْ عَلَيْهِنَّ مِنْ عِدَّةٍ تَعْتَدُّونَهَا فَمَتِّعُوهُنَّ وَسَرِّحُوهُنَّ سَرَاحًا جَمِيلًا

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū idhā nakaḥtumu'l-muʾmināti thumma ṭallaqtumūhunna min qabli an tamassūhunna fa-mā lakum ʿalayhinna min ʿiddatin taʿtaddūnahā, fa-mattiʿūhunna wa sarriḥūhunna sarāḥan jamīlā*

"O you who believe! If you marry believing women and then divorce them before you have touched them, you have upon them no waiting period to count. So give them a provision and release them in a fair release."

### A regulation and a repetition

**The verse states a ruling and closes the ruling with a command.**

**And the closing command is word for word the same as in the twenty-eighth verse:** *sarāḥan jamīlā*.

| Verse | Who | The construction |
|---|---|---|
| **28** | The choice offered to the Prophet's wives | *usarriḥkunna sarāḥan jamīlā* |
| **49** | A command to all the believers | *sarriḥūhunna sarāḥan jamīlā* |

**The same construction, once an offer and once a command.** And there are twenty-one verses between them.

**This is a method of the sūra and I record it:** a measure applied in a particular case is afterwards repeated as a general command. **The sūra opens to everyone the measure established in the Prophet's house.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the exact repetition of the construction.

### عِدَّة — root ع-د-د

**To count. *ʿAdad* — a number; *ʿidda* — a counted period.**

**And in the root of the word there is an act of *counting*** — indeed the verse adds the verb as well: *taʿtaddūnahā* — "which you would count". Two words from the same root.

**What the institution of the *ʿidda* is, and how long it lasts in which cases, is the business of jurisprudence and is not taken up in this commentary.** The subject was touched on at `065-talak.md`; I leave it there.

**What emerges from the wording of the verse is this:** in a particular case (a marriage contracted and ended before it has actually begun) there is no waiting period.

**And the ground of the ruling is visible in the logic of the institution:** one of the functions of the *ʿidda* is that lineage should not be confused; where that possibility does not exist, no period is needed either. **This is an observation, not a legal ruling.**

### فَمَتِّعُوهُنَّ — a provision

**The root م-ت-ع** for the third time in this sūra (16, 28, 49).

**And the structure of the ruling is worth recording:** the verse first **removes** an obligation (there is no *ʿidda*), then **lays down** an obligation (give a provision and release them in a fair release).

**That is, in bringing an easing the verse does not dump the cost of the easing on the other side.** The period is lifted, but the provision and the manner remain.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the order of the verse's two clauses.

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse is that short relationships too have an ending.**

However short a bond may be, its ending has a form. **And the verse does not count the shortness of the period as a ground:** it does not say "it had not begun anyway".

**On the contrary, it asks for two things:** a provision and a form. **That is, the duration of a relationship and the responsibility in its ending are not the same thing.**

---

## 33/50 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ إِنَّآ أَحْلَلْنَا لَكَ أَزْوَٰجَكَ ٱلَّٰتِىٓ ءَاتَيْتَ أُجُورَهُنَّ … خَالِصَةً لَّكَ مِن دُونِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ قَدْ عَلِمْنَا مَا فَرَضْنَا عَلَيْهِمْ فِىٓ أَزْوَٰجِهِمْ وَمَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَٰنُهُمْ لِكَيْلَا يَكُونَ عَلَيْكَ حَرَجٌ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا

*Yā ayyuha'l-nabiyyu innā aḥlalnā laka azwājaka'llātī ātayta ujūrahunna… khāliṣatan laka min dūni'l-muʾminīn, qad ʿalimnā mā faraḍnā ʿalayhim fī azwājihim wa mā malakat aymānuhum li-kaylā yakūna ʿalayka ḥaraj, wa kāna'llāhu ghafūran raḥīmā*

"O Prophet! We have made lawful for you your wives whose bridal gifts you have given … This is yours alone, apart from the believers. We know what We have laid down for them concerning their wives and those their right hands possess — so that there should be no difficulty for you. God is Forgiving, Merciful."

**Note:** the full text of the verse is long; its beginning and its end are given here, and the enumeration in the middle is treated below.

### The structure of the verse

**The verse states a regulation particular to the Prophet** and says so explicitly: *khāliṣatan laka min dūni'l-muʾminīn* — "as yours alone, apart from the believers."

**This note is the most important sentence of the verse and must be recorded at the outset:** the regulation the verse brings **is not a general ruling**, and the verse itself states this.

**خَالِصَة — root خ-ل-ص.** Pure, unmixed, belonging to one thing alone. *Ikhlāṣ* is from the same root (treated as the name of a sūra at `112-ihlas.md`). **In the root of the word there is a *sifting out*:** what has been cleared of any admixture.

### The categories enumerated

In its middle part the verse makes an enumeration. The categories in brief:

| # | Category |
|---|---|
| **1** | Wives whose bridal gifts have been given |
| **2** | Those in his possession |
| **3** | Of the daughters of paternal and maternal uncles and aunts, those who emigrated with him |
| **4** | A believing woman who gives herself to the Prophet — if the Prophet accepts |

**For the fourth category the verse enters a further note:** *khāliṣatan laka min dūni'l-muʾminīn.*

**To which category this note belongs has been debated in classical exegesis:**

| View | What it says |
|---|---|
| **It belongs to the fourth category alone** | The note attaches to the clause immediately before it |
| **It belongs to the whole enumeration** | The note qualifies the entire regulation |

**I do not impose a preference.** The word order, in that the note comes immediately after the fourth category, seems to support the first view; this is an observation.

### A note on وَمَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَٰنُهُمْ

**This expression names an institution that existed in the social order of that period (slavery).**

**No legal ruling is issued on this in this commentary** and how the subject has been debated through history is not entered into. But for the text to be readable two notes must be entered:

**One.** The Qurʾān names this institution **as an existing reality** and brings regulations. The same Qurʾān, in many places, sets the freeing of slaves forward as a **good deed and an expiation**: *"the freeing of a neck from its yoke"* (al-Balad 90/13 — **treated at `090-beled.md`**); the expiation of an oath (al-Māʾida 5/89); the expiation of ẓihār (al-Mujādala 58/3 — **treated at `058-mucadele.md`**); and, as one of the categories to which alms are directed, *fi'l-riqāb* (al-Tawba 9/60).

**Two.** What conclusion is to be drawn as a whole from the verses in which this expression occurs is a question long debated in the history of Islamic thought and **goes beyond the limits of this commentary.** What is done here is to record, **without concealment**, the existence of the expression and the Qurʾān's other notes on the same subject.

**Per the method (`STYLE.md`): no legal ruling is issued, no preference is imposed, and current debates are not entered.**

### لِكَيْلَا يَكُونَ عَلَيْكَ حَرَجٌ — the ground

**And the clause of ground in the verse has the same structure as that of the thirty-seventh.**

| Verse | The ground | For whom |
|---|---|---|
| **37** | *li-kay lā yakūna **ʿala'l-muʾminīna** ḥaraj* | The believers |
| **38** | *mā kāna **ʿala'l-nabiyyi** min ḥaraj* | The Prophet |
| **50** | *li-kaylā yakūna **ʿalayka** ḥaraj* | The Prophet |

**In all three verses the same word (*ḥaraj*) and the same function: the removal of a constriction.**

**And the picture in the root of the word — a dense thicket that gives no passage — is the same in all three: the opening of a way.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the repetition of the word in the three verses.

---

## 33/51 — تُرْجِى مَن تَشَآءُ مِنْهُنَّ وَتُـْٔوِىٓ إِلَيْكَ مَن تَشَآءُ وَمَنِ ٱبْتَغَيْتَ مِمَّنْ عَزَلْتَ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكَ ذَٰلِكَ أَدْنَىٰٓ أَن تَقَرَّ أَعْيُنُهُنَّ وَلَا يَحْزَنَّ وَيَرْضَيْنَ بِمَآ ءَاتَيْتَهُنَّ كُلُّهُنَّ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مَا فِى قُلُوبِكُمْ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ عَلِيمًا حَلِيمًا

*Turjī man tashāʾu minhunna wa tuʾwī ilayka man tashāʾ, wa mani'btaghayta mimman ʿazalta fa-lā junāḥa ʿalayk, dhālika adnā an taqarra aʿyunuhunna wa lā yaḥzanna wa yarḍayna bimā ātaytahunna kulluhunna, wa'llāhu yaʿlamu mā fī qulūbikum, wa kāna'llāhu ʿalīman ḥalīmā*

"You may put off whom you will of them and take to yourself whom you will. And if you seek one of those you have set aside, there is no blame on you. That is more likely to bring comfort to their eyes, that they should not grieve, and that all of them should be content with what you give them. God knows what is in your hearts. God is Knowing, Forbearing."

### The words

**تُرْجِى — root ر-ج-و or ر-ج-أ.** To postpone, to put off. **The same root occurred in the twenty-first verse as *yarjū* (to hope)**, and it was recorded there: under the root lie both "to hope" and "to postpone".

**And the comparison of the two occurrences is worth noting:** in 21 hope, here postponement. **The same root, once looking forward in time, once casting a thing forward in time.**

**تُـْٔوِى — root أ-و-ي.** To shelter, to give refuge. *Maʾwā* — a place of refuge.

**عَزَلْتَ — root ع-ز-ل.** To separate, to set aside. *ʿUzla* is from the same root.

### ذَٰلِكَ أَدْنَىٰٓ — the pattern of the ground

**أَدْنَىٰ — root د-ن-و.** To be near; the elative: "nearer, more fitting".

**And this pattern occurs twice in the sūra:**

| Verse | The construction | The ground |
|---|---|---|
| **51** | *dhālika adnā an taqarra aʿyunuhunna wa lā yaḥzanna wa yarḍayna* | Ease of heart |
| **59** | *dhālika adnā an yuʿrafna fa-lā yuʾdhayna* | Being recognised and not injured |

**Both verses bring a regulation and give its ground with the pattern *adnā*.**

**And this is a fact that must be recorded:** in these two places the Qurʾān writes explicitly **why** the ruling has been laid down. **And both grounds look to people's inward condition:** in one, ease of heart; in the other, not being injured.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the use of the pattern with the same function in both verses.

**And another feature of the *adnā* pattern:** it is an elative, that is, it says "more" and not "most". **It conveys not a definite best but an orientation** — like *aqsaṭ* in the fifth verse.

### أَن تَقَرَّ أَعْيُنُهُنَّ — an idiom

**The root ق-ر-ر.** It occurred in the thirty-third verse in the reading *qarna*: to be fixed, to come to rest.

***Qurratu ʿayn* — "the settling of the eye".** It is an idiom in Arabic and means joy, calm, contentment.

**And the picture in the idiom comes from the root:** the eye, when it is uneasy, roams and cannot stay still — this was described exactly in the tenth and nineteenth verses (*zāghati'l-abṣār*, *tadūru aʿyunuhum*). **The eye's "settling" is the sign of calm.**

**And this sets up a web within the sūra:**

| Verse | The eye | The state |
|---|---|---|
| **10** | *zāghati'l-abṣār* | Fear — a swerving gaze |
| **19** | *tadūru aʿyunuhum* | Fear — a rolling gaze |
| **51** | *taqarra aʿyunuhunna* | Calm — a settled gaze |

**The sūra records three states of the eye, and all three are indicators of the inward condition.** I record this as my own reading.

### وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مَا فِى قُلُوبِكُمْ

**And again the *heart*.** The places where the word occurs in the sūra: 4, 10, 12, 26, 32, 51, 53.

**And this occurrence is worth noting:** the pronoun is **masculine plural** (*qulūbikum*) — that is, the address is not to the women alone but to everyone.

**The function of the sentence:** a regulation is being made, and whether the regulation works is measured not from outside but from the heart. **The measure of the fifth verse (*mā taʿammadat qulūbukum*) is repeated here.**

### حَلِيم — the verse-ending

**Root: ح-ل-م.** Not to be hasty, not to be angered, to act with weight. *Ḥilm* — patient maturity.

**And the agreement of the verse-ending with the verse:** the verse takes as its subject a regulation and the feelings that regulation may arouse in people. **And the name *ḥalīm* carries the sense "He knows but does not hasten".**

---

## 33/52 — لَّا يَحِلُّ لَكَ ٱلنِّسَآءُ مِنۢ بَعْدُ وَلَآ أَن تَبَدَّلَ بِهِنَّ مِنْ أَزْوَٰجٍ وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَكَ حُسْنُهُنَّ إِلَّا مَا مَلَكَتْ يَمِينُكَ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ رَّقِيبًا

*Lā yaḥillu laka'l-nisāʾu min baʿdu wa lā an tabaddala bihinna min azwājin wa law aʿjabaka ḥusnuhunna illā mā malakat yamīnuk, wa kāna'llāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin raqībā*

"Women are not lawful for you after this, nor that you should exchange your wives for others — even though their beauty please you. Except those your right hand possesses. God is Watchful over all things."

### A verse of limit

**The fiftieth verse had stated a widening; the fifty-second sets a limit.**

**And that the two verses come one after the other is worth recording:** two verses after bringing a regulation, the sūra draws a limit around that regulation.

I record this as a datum of the text.

### تَبَدَّلَ — root ب-د-ل

**To change, to put another in the place of a thing.** **And this root occurred in the twenty-third verse:** *mā baddalū tabdīlā* — "they made no change at all."

**The same root, once the not changing of a word, once the not changing of a spouse.** I record it.

### وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَكَ حُسْنُهُنَّ — the plainness of the note

**أَعْجَبَ — root ع-ج-ب.** To astonish, to please. *ʿAjīb* — astonishing; *iʿjāb* — admiration, being taken with something.

**And the placing of this note in the verse is striking.**

The verse could have laid down the prohibition and passed on. Instead it also writes **in what circumstance the prohibition will become hard**: *wa law aʿjabaka ḥusnuhunna.*

**This is a recurring feature in the Qurʾān's way of framing a prohibition, and I record it:** the prohibition is laid down while showing awareness of the human inclination. **That is, the verse does not tell the one addressed "this is easy for you".**

I give this as my own reading; its ground is the presence of the *law* clause.

### رَقِيب — the verse-ending

**Root: ر-ق-ب.** And the concrete sense of the root is **the neck**: *raqaba* — a neck. *Raqīb* — the watcher; and the link is this: **the one who watches stretches out his neck to look.**

**From the same root come *murāqaba* (observation) and *irtiqāb*.**

**And the agreement of the verse-ending with the verse:** the verse sets a limit, and the verse-ending states that the limit is watched. **The picture in the root of the word supports this too: one who looks from a distance, stretching out his neck.**

---

## 33/53 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَا تَدْخُلُوا۟ بُيُوتَ ٱلنَّبِىِّ إِلَّآ أَن يُؤْذَنَ لَكُمْ إِلَىٰ طَعَامٍ غَيْرَ نَٰظِرِينَ إِنَىٰهُ وَلَٰكِنْ إِذَا دُعِيتُمْ فَٱدْخُلُوا۟ فَإِذَا طَعِمْتُمْ فَٱنتَشِرُوا۟ وَلَا مُسْتَـْٔنِسِينَ لِحَدِيثٍ إِنَّ ذَٰلِكُمْ كَانَ يُؤْذِى ٱلنَّبِىَّ فَيَسْتَحْىِۦ مِنكُمْ وَٱللَّهُ لَا يَسْتَحْىِۦ مِنَ ٱلْحَقِّ وَإِذَا سَأَلْتُمُوهُنَّ مَتَٰعًا فَسْـَٔلُوهُنَّ مِن وَرَآءِ حِجَابٍ ذَٰلِكُمْ أَطْهَرُ لِقُلُوبِكُمْ وَقُلُوبِهِنَّ

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū lā tadkhulū buyūta'l-nabiyyi illā an yuʾdhana lakum ilā ṭaʿāmin ghayra nāẓirīna ināhu wa lākin idhā duʿītum fa'dkhulū fa-idhā ṭaʿimtum fa'ntashirū wa lā mustaʾnisīna li-ḥadīth, inna dhālikum kāna yuʾdhi'l-nabiyya fa-yastaḥyī minkum wa'llāhu lā yastaḥyī mina'l-ḥaqq, wa idhā saʾaltumūhunna matāʿan fa's'alūhunna min warāʾi ḥijāb, dhālikum aṭharu li-qulūbikum wa qulūbihinn…*

"O you who believe! Do not enter the houses of the Prophet unless you are invited to a meal, and without watching for its time. But when you are invited, enter; and when you have eaten, disperse — do not linger over talk. That was disturbing the Prophet, and he was too shy to tell you; but God is not shy of the truth. And when you ask them for something, ask them from behind a screen. That is purer for your hearts and for theirs…"

**The continuation of the verse:** *"And it is not for you to injure the Messenger of God, nor ever to marry his wives after him. That would be a grave thing in God's sight."*

### The most detailed verse of conduct in the sūra

**This verse is one of the longest in the Qurʾān and contains more than one regulation.** In order:

1. Not entering without leave.
2. Not watching for the time of the meal.
3. Entering when invited.
4. Dispersing after the meal.
5. Not lingering over talk.
6. Asking for a thing from behind a screen.
7. Not injuring the Prophet.
8. Not marrying his wives after him.

### The link with al-Ḥujurāt

**At `049-hucurat.md` 49/4-5 the *ḥujurāt* and the physical boundary of privacy were treated. It is essential to rely on that treatment, because the two verses are two stages of the same matter.**

What was recorded there:

- **What is meant are the rooms that made up the Prophet's house**, and the word's coming in the **plural** describes a structure: a series of enclosed spaces adjoining the mosque but separate from it, beside the public area but not part of it.
- **What was censured was not entering but calling out.** *"A physical boundary can be crossed by voice. A door may be shut and the person may still disturb the one inside. Privacy is not kept safe by a wall alone; it is kept safe by the wall being respected."*
- ***Ḥujra* and *ḥijr* (reason) are from the same root** — both are a boundary.

**And now the two verses must be compared.**

| | **al-Ḥujurāt 49/4** | **al-Aḥzāb 33/53** |
|---|---|---|
| **The act** | *yunādūnaka* — calling out | *lā tadkhulū* — entering |
| **The boundary** | *min warāʾi'l-**ḥujurāt*** — from behind the rooms | *min warāʾi **ḥijāb*** — from behind the screen |
| **What is censured** | Crossing the boundary by voice | Crossing the boundary bodily, and staying too long |
| **The diagnosis** | *aktharuhum lā yaʿqilūn* | *kāna yuʾdhi'l-nabiyy* |
| **The remedy** | To wait | Leave, invitation, and dispersal |

**In both verses the same construction is used: *min warāʾi* — "from behind".** In one, behind the rooms; in the other, behind the screen.

**And the difference is worth recording, and I give it as my own reading:** in al-Ḥujurāt *min warāʾi* indicated the place of the violation (those "who call out from behind the rooms" were censured). **In al-Aḥzāb the same construction indicates the place of right conduct** ("ask from behind the screen").

**That is, the same particle in two verses with two opposite functions:** in one the place where the boundary is crossed, in the other the place where it is kept.

### غَيْرَ نَٰظِرِينَ إِنَىٰهُ — watching for the time

**إِنًى — root أ-ن-ي.** The time of a thing's ripening, its coming to readiness. *Ānī* — come to its point. The Qurʾān uses the root of hot water: *"from a boiling spring"* (al-Ghāshiya 88/5 — *āniya*).

**The construction occurs only here in the Qurʾān.**

**And the behaviour described is extremely concrete:** sitting there waiting for the food to be cooked. **That is, the verse depicts a violation of a boundary as a matter of *timing*.**

**This is an observation worth recording:** the violation is not in entering; it is in **fixing the moment of entry oneself.** The same measure was laid down at al-Ḥujurāt 49/5: *"Had they been patient until you came out…"* — there too the matter was who fixes the length of the waiting.

**And the finding recorded at `049-hucurat.md` works here exactly:** *"The boundary is set not where the person's patience runs out but where the other party is ready."*

### فَإِذَا طَعِمْتُمْ فَٱنتَشِرُوا۟ — the command to disperse

**ٱنتَشَرَ — root ن-ش-ر.** To spread, to scatter, to open out. *Nashr* — spreading; *muntashir* — scattered.

**And the choice of the verb is worth recording:** the verse does not say "go out" (*ukhrujū*), it says **"disperse"**. **The verb describes the breaking up of a gathering** — not individual departure.

**The same verb occurs in Sūrat al-Jumuʿa too:** *"When the prayer has been performed, disperse through the land"* (al-Jumuʿa 62/10 — **treated at `062-cuma.md`**). **In both places the same structure: a command to disperse after a gathering.**

### وَلَا مُسْتَـْٔنِسِينَ لِحَدِيثٍ — a fine social observation

**This is the most striking expression in the verse and deserves to be dwelt on.**

**Root: أ-ن-س.** And the semantic field of the root is warm: *uns* — closeness, cohesion, the ease felt in being together. *Insān* is counted from the same root (according to some lexicographers). *Anīs* — a friend, one who relieves loneliness. *Istiʾnās* — to take pleasure in someone, to warm to him, to enjoy spending time with him.

**The Qurʾān uses the root in the account of Moses:** *"I saw / I sensed a fire"* (Ṭāhā 20/10 — *ānastu nāran*) — there *ānasa* means noticing something from afar and turning towards it.

**حَدِيث — root ح-د-ث.** What is new; and from this "speech, conversation" — the new thing that is said.

**Now let us look at what the construction describes:** *mustaʾnisīna li-ḥadīth* — **taking pleasure in the conversation and staying on there.**

**And this is a fine social observation. For three reasons:**

**One. What is censured is not a bad intention.** The person conversing wishes to harm no one; **on the contrary, he is enjoying being there.** The root of the word (*uns* — cohesion) says this plainly.

**Two. What is censured is an *excess*.** Entering is not forbidden, eating is not forbidden, talking is not forbidden. **What is forbidden is going on after the business is over.** That is, the boundary is not in the behaviour but in the **duration**.

**Three. And precisely for that reason it is hard to notice.** The person knows he is doing nothing bad; he does not see that the host is uncomfortable, because the discomfort is not voiced. **The verse says this too: *fa-yastaḥyī minkum* — "he is too shy to tell you."**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the joining of the "taking pleasure" sense of the root with the note of *ḥayāʾ* in the continuation of the verse.

### فَيَسْتَحْىِۦ مِنكُمْ وَٱللَّهُ لَا يَسْتَحْىِۦ مِنَ ٱلْحَقِّ

**The most elegant sentence in the verse.**

**ٱسْتَحْيَا — root ح-ي-ي.** It occurred in the forty-fourth verse as *taḥiyya*: the primary sense of the root is **life**. *Ḥayāʾ* — shame, reticence; and the lexicographers make the link thus: blood rushes to the face of the one who is ashamed, that is, **a sign of life** appears.

**And what the sentence does is this:** a vicious circle is described and broken.

**The vicious circle:** the host is uncomfortable → he is too shy to say so → the guest does not understand → he goes on.

**The break:** *wa'llāhu lā yastaḥyī mina'l-ḥaqq* — what needs to be said is said.

**This is a structure that must be recorded, and I give it as my own reading:** to resolve a social bind the verse brings in not one of the parties but **a third voice.** The host cannot say it; the text says it.

**And this explains why the rule exists:** some boundaries cannot be defended by the one they belong to. **Because the defence itself damages the relationship it is trying to protect.** The function of a rule begins exactly here: not leaving a person to defend his own boundary case by case.

**The same expression occurs once more in the Qurʾān:** *"God is not ashamed to give the likeness of a gnat"* (al-Baqara 2/26 — *inna'llāha lā yastaḥyī an yaḍriba mathalan*). **In both places the same structure: the removal of a reticence about speaking.**

### مِن وَرَآءِ حِجَابٍ

**حِجَاب — root ح-ج-ب.** To cover, to veil, to be an obstacle between. *Ḥājib* — a doorkeeper, a chamberlain; and the eyebrow (because it covers the eye).

**And the sense of the word in this verse is concrete:** a physical divider set between when a request is conveyed.

**On the extent of this verse and on its present-day applications there are differing interpretations in Islamic thought.** **No legal ruling is issued in this commentary.**

**What emerges from the wording of the verse:** the regulation takes as its subject a particular situation (something being asked for from the Prophet's house) and its ground is written explicitly in the verse.

### ذَٰلِكُمْ أَطْهَرُ لِقُلُوبِكُمْ وَقُلُوبِهِنَّ — the ground

**The root ط-ه-ر** was treated at the thirty-third verse.

**And the structure of the ground is worth recording — for three reasons:**

**One. The ground is given for *both parties*:** *li-qulūbikum **wa qulūbihinn***. It is not a one-sided protection.

**Two. The ground is tied to the heart**, not to outward appearance. The measure the sūra has maintained from the beginning.

**Three. Again an elative: *aṭhar*** — "purer". **The *aqsaṭ* of the fifth verse, the *adnā* of the fifty-first, and the *aṭhar* here.**

**The sūra always gives its grounds in the comparative pattern** — "more", not "most". I record this as my own reading; its ground is the unity of the pattern in the three verses.

### وَمَا كَانَ لَكُمْ أَن تُؤْذُوا۟ رَسُولَ ٱللَّهِ — the axis of injury

**The root أ-ذ-ي** occurs **twice** in this verse (*yuʾdhi'l-nabiyy* and *an tuʾdhū rasūla'llāh*).

**And the two occurrences are on different levels:**

| Occurrence | What | The agent |
|---|---|---|
| *kāna yuʾdhi'l-nabiyy* | Discomfort given unawares | Those who linger over talk |
| *an tuʾdhū rasūla'llāh* | A general prohibition | Everyone |

**That is, the verse names with the same word a discomfort given unawares as well.**

**This is worth recording:** *adhā* does not depend on intention. No one may wish to hurt; the verse still names the act *adhā*.

I give this as my own reading; its ground is that the two occurrences are framed with the same word.

### On the final ruling of the verse

**The ruling at the end of the verse** — that his wives are not to be married after the Prophet — **links directly with the statement *wa azwājuhu ummahātuhum* in the sixth verse.**

**And the link must be recorded:** the sixth verse had stated a **standing**; the fifty-third writes one consequence of that standing explicitly.

**This confirms what was recorded at the sixth verse:** there is debate about the extent of that standing, but this is the consequence most plainly written into the text.

### What this says to the present

**This verse says the most concrete things that can be said about privacy, and it gives three measures for today.**

**One. An invitation is not a permission of unlimited access.**

The verse counts being invited, entering and eating all as legitimate. **The only thing it limits is what comes after the business is over.** That is, a door's being opened does not mean that door stays open indefinitely.

**Two. Some boundaries cannot be defended by the one they belong to.**

The verse's note *fa-yastaḥyī minkum* is the clearest expression of this. **That a person does not say he is uncomfortable does not mean that he is not uncomfortable.** And what prevents the discomfort from being voiced is most often not ill will but courtesy.

**Its practical consequence:** waiting for the other party's objection in order to measure whether a behaviour is acceptable is not a reliable method. **The objection may not come — because objecting carries a cost of its own.**

**Three. *Mustaʾnisīna li-ḥadīth* — the expression most easily carried into today's language.**

Taking pleasure in a conversation and not getting up, keeping a message exchange going, stretching out a meeting, prolonging a visit beyond what is needed. **What all of them have in common is that the one who stays is enjoying it.**

**And the measure of the verse is worth noting:** no one is accused. The verse points to the moment when the business is over and commands dispersal. **The problem is not the behaviour itself; it is not seeing where it ends.**

---

## 33/54-55 — إِن تُبْدُوا۟ شَيْـًٔا أَوْ تُخْفُوهُ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ بِكُلِّ شَىْءٍ عَلِيمًا · لَّا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْهِنَّ فِىٓ ءَابَآئِهِنَّ وَلَآ أَبْنَآئِهِنَّ وَلَآ إِخْوَٰنِهِنَّ وَلَآ أَبْنَآءِ إِخْوَٰنِهِنَّ وَلَآ أَبْنَآءِ أَخَوَٰتِهِنَّ وَلَا نِسَآئِهِنَّ وَلَا مَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَٰنُهُنَّ وَٱتَّقِينَ ٱللَّهَ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ كَانَ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ شَهِيدًا

*In tubdū shayʾan aw tukhfūhu fa-inna'llāha kāna bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīmā · Lā junāḥa ʿalayhinna fī ābāʾihinna wa lā abnāʾihinna wa lā ikhwānihinna wa lā abnāʾi ikhwānihinna wa lā abnāʾi akhawātihinna wa lā nisāʾihinna wa lā mā malakat aymānuhunn, wa'ttaqīna'llāh, inna'llāha kāna ʿalā kulli shayʾin shahīdā*

"Whether you disclose a thing or conceal it, God knows all things. · There is no blame on them concerning their fathers, their sons, their brothers, their brothers' sons, their sisters' sons, their women, or those their right hands possess. And guard yourselves against God. God is Witness over all things."

### The fifty-fourth verse: disclosing and concealing

**A short verse, but it gathers up one of the sūra's axes.**

**أَبْدَىٰ / أَخْفَىٰ** — to disclose / to conceal. **And this pair occurred in the thirty-seventh verse:** *wa **tukhfī** fī nafsika ma'llāhu **mubdīh***.

| Verse | Who | What |
|---|---|---|
| **37** | The Prophet | He was concealing it within himself; God was to bring it out |
| **54** | Everyone | Whether you disclose or conceal |

**The same two roots, in the same sūra, once in a single event and once as a general rule.**

**And what was said of the Prophet in the thirty-seventh verse is repeated for everyone in the fifty-fourth.** The method of the sūra: from the particular to the general.

I record this as my own reading.

**And the placing of the verse immediately after the fifty-third is meaningful too:** the fifty-third verse took as its subject a discomfort left unsaid (*fa-yastaḥyī minkum*). **The fifty-fourth states that what is left unsaid is known too.**

### The fifty-fifth verse: the list of those within the degrees of kinship

**The verse brings an exception to the regulation of the screen in the fifty-third verse** and gives a list.

| # | Category |
|---|---|
| **1** | Their fathers |
| **2** | Their sons |
| **3** | Their brothers |
| **4** | Their brothers' sons |
| **5** | Their sisters' sons |
| **6** | Their women |
| **7** | Those their right hands possess |

**The structure of the list is worth recording:** the first five categories are established **by the tie of blood.**

**And this links with the fourth and fifth verses of the sūra.** There it was said that the tie of lineage cannot be established by speech; here a **consequence** of the tie of lineage becomes visible: the list of kinship is drawn according to blood.

**That is, the sūra shows a second application of the principle in the fourth verse** — the first was in the thirty-seventh (the wife of an adopted son), the second is here (the degrees of kinship).

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the list rests on lineage.

**In Sūrat al-Nūr (24/31) there is a wider list, and the differences between the two lists have been taken up in classical exegesis. This commentary does not enter into legal detail.**

**وَلَا مَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَٰنُهُنَّ** — the note entered at the fiftieth verse holds here too; I do not repeat it.

### وَٱتَّقِينَ ٱللَّهَ — after the list

**And after the list a command comes: *wa'ttaqīna'llāh*.**

**This is a fact of word order worth recording:** immediately after a list of permission a command of taqwā is placed.

**And its function is this:** the list shows where a boundary does not end. **But the existence of the list does not mean that no measure at all remains with those inside it.**

**The link with the first verse of the sūra:** the command *ittaqi'llāh* was given to the Prophet at the head of the sūra. In the fifty-fifth verse the same command is repeated in the feminine plural form.

### شَهِيد — the verse-ending

**Root: ش-ه-د.** To be present, to see, to bear witness. **In the forty-fifth verse *shāhid* was used of the Prophet; here *shahīd* is used of God.**

**The same root, with two different subjects, ten verses apart.** I record it.

**And the agreement of the verse-ending with the verse:** the verse regulates a field not seen from outside (the inside of the house) and closes with the name that states **the widest form of seeing.**

---

## 33/56 — إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ وَمَلَٰٓئِكَتَهُۥ يُصَلُّونَ عَلَى ٱلنَّبِىِّ يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ صَلُّوا۟ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلِّمُوا۟ تَسْلِيمًا

*Inna'llāha wa malāʾikatahu yuṣallūna ʿala'l-nabiyy, yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū ṣallū ʿalayhi wa sallimū taslīmā*

"Indeed God and His angels send *ṣalāt* upon the Prophet. O you who believe! Send *ṣalāt* upon him too, and greet him with a full greeting of peace."

### The root ص-ل-و

**This root was treated at `001-fatiha.md` and in many other places; here I focus on the word's sense in this verse.**

**On the concrete sense of the root the lexicographers record several things, none of them certain:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **صَلَوَان (*ṣalawān*)** | The two movable bones on either side of the waist; a link is made with the bending in prayer |
| **صَلَّى (*ṣallā*)** | In horse racing the horse that comes second — the one following the *ṣalā* of the first. From this "coming behind, following" |
| **اِصْطَلَى** | To come near a fire, to warm oneself (the root ص-ل-ي — counted as separate) |

**These derivations are not certain and I build no meaning upon them.** What must be recorded is that the word carries in Arabic a sense of **turning towards and attaching oneself.**

### The ṣalāt of three subjects — a table

**The most striking side of the verse is that the same verb is ascribed to three different subjects.** And though the verb is the same, its counterpart is not.

**In classical exegesis these three counterparts are distinguished thus:**

| Subject | What the ṣalāt corresponds to | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **God** | Mercy, praise, exaltation; mention in the presence of the angels | The state of the verb's agent; the use of the same verb of the believers at al-Aḥzāb 33/43 with the ground given as *li-yukhrijakum mina'l-ẓulumāti ila'l-nūr* |
| **The angels** | Supplication, seeking forgiveness | The statement in the Qurʾān that the angels seek forgiveness for the believers (al-Muʾmin 40/7; al-Shūrā 42/5) |
| **People** | Supplication, the sending of blessings and peace | The command of the verse: *ṣallū ʿalayhi wa sallimū* |

**This distinction is widely recorded in classical exegesis.** And it has a counterpart in the language: **the same verb takes a different content according to the state of the agent.**

**Its parallel in Arabic is common:** the same verb says one thing when done from above downwards and another when done from below upwards.

**No preference is imposed in this commentary; the classical distinction is conveyed.**

### The word order of the verse

**And the word order of the verse is worth recording.**

**The sentence begins with a *statement*:** *inna'llāha wa malāʾikatahu yuṣallūna ʿala'l-nabiyy.* **Then a *command* comes:** *ṣallū ʿalayhi.*

**That is, the command comes after a statement** — and the ground of the command becomes the statement itself. **The verse does not say "do this because it is beneficial"; it says "this is already happening, join in."**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the order of the two sentences.

**And the tense of the verb:** *yuṣallūna* — imperfect, conveying continuity. **That is, what is described is not a single occurrence but something ongoing.**

### وَسَلِّمُوا۟ تَسْلِيمًا

**Again a cognate accusative.** The seventh reinforcement in the sūra.

**And the root س-ل-م for the fourth time in this sūra** (22, 35, 44, 56).

**And the link with the twenty-second verse is worth recording:** there too the word *taslīm* was not in the same cognate-accusative pattern, but it came from the same root and named the believers' response in the face of the siege (*mā zādahum illā īmānan wa taslīmā*).

**There are thirty-four verses between the two, and both use the same root.** I record it.

### A comparison of the two verses

**I recorded at the forty-third verse that the same structure is used twice in the sūra; here I complete it.**

| | **33/43** | **33/56** |
|---|---|---|
| **Subject** | *huwa… wa malāʾikatuhu* | *inna'llāha wa malāʾikatahu* |
| **Recipient** | *ʿalaykum* — the believers | *ʿala'l-nabiyy* — the Prophet |
| **The ground** | *li-yukhrijakum mina'l-ẓulumāti ila'l-nūr* | — |
| **A command** | None (a statement) | Yes: *ṣallū ʿalayhi* |

**And the difference is worth recording:** in the forty-third verse there is a ground, in the fifty-sixth there is a command. **The first says what it is for, the second what is to be done.**

I record this as my own reading.

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse says something about how a command is framed.**

The command arises not from an obligation but from **an invitation to join something already going on.** The sentence first states what is, then asks for participation.

**And this changes the weight of the command:** the person is not beginning something; **he is being added to something that is already happening.**

---

## 33/57-58 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْذُونَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥ لَعَنَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ وَأَعَدَّ لَهُمْ عَذَابًا مُّهِينًا · وَٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْذُونَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ بِغَيْرِ مَا ٱكْتَسَبُوا۟ فَقَدِ ٱحْتَمَلُوا۟ بُهْتَٰنًا وَإِثْمًا مُّبِينًا

*Inna'lladhīna yuʾdhūna'llāha wa rasūlahu laʿanahumu'llāhu fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhirati wa aʿadda lahum ʿadhāban muhīnā · Wa'lladhīna yuʾdhūna'l-muʾminīna wa'l-muʾmināti bi-ghayri ma'ktasabū fa-qadi'ḥtamalū buhtānan wa ithman mubīnā*

"Those who injure God and His Messenger — God has cursed them in this world and the hereafter, and has prepared for them a humiliating punishment. · And those who injure believing men and believing women for something they have not done have taken upon themselves a slander and a manifest sin."

### Two steps of the axis of injury

**The two verses use the same verb (*yuʾdhūna*) with two different objects** and state two different outcomes.

| | **Verse 57** | **Verse 58** |
|---|---|---|
| **Who is injured** | God and His Messenger | Believing men and women |
| **The note** | — | *bi-ghayri ma'ktasabū* — for something they have not done |
| **The outcome** | Curse and punishment | Taking on *buhtān* and *ithm* |

**And that the two verses come one after the other is one of the most striking facts of word order in the sūra.**

**Immediately after recounting the injury done to the Prophet, the Qurʾān recounts the injury done to ordinary believers.** And it uses the same verb for both.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the succession of the two verses and the shared verb.

### يُؤْذُونَ ٱللَّهَ — the sense of the expression

**Classical exegesis has dwelt on this expression, because the injuring of God cannot be thought of in its literal sense.**

**The classical explanations:**

| Explanation | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Figurative** | The expression conveys acts with which God is not pleased; "injury" here is figurative |
| **Ascribing to God what is done to His Messenger** | Since the two subjects are named together, the act belongs properly to the second |
| **Denial and slander** | What is meant is saying about God what does not befit Him |

**I do not impose a preference.** And the note of **tanzīh** entered at `057-hadid.md` (57/4 and 57/29) and at `002-bakara.md` (2/115) holds here too: in speaking about God human qualities are not fastened onto Him, and al-Shūrā 42/11 (*laysa ka-mithlihī shayʾ*) stands as the limit.

### بِغَيْرِ مَا ٱكْتَسَبُوا۟ — the function of the note

**ٱكْتَسَبَ — root ك-س-ب.** To earn; in Form VIII (*iktisāb*) it generally conveys an **effort** and an **intent**.

**And this distinction (*kasaba* / *iktasaba*) was recorded at `002-bakara.md`:** at al-Baqara 2/286 the two forms come side by side and the lexicographers record that the first is used of good and the second of evil.

**The function of the note is this:** the verse takes as its subject **undeserved** injury. That is, a response to something a person has actually done is not the subject of this verse.

**And this explains why the verse ends with the word *buhtān*.**

### بُهْتَٰن — root ب-ه-ت

**And the concrete sense of the root must be dwelt on: *bahata* — to leave someone dumbfounded, to astonish him to the point of freezing.** The Qurʾān uses the root in this sense: *"Abraham… so the one who disbelieved **was left dumbfounded**"* (al-Baqara 2/258 — *fa-buhita'lladhī kafar*).

**From this comes the sense *buhtān* — slander.** **And the link is this: slander freezes the one it is aimed at.** The person does not know what to say, because he has no connection at all with what is being levelled at him.

**This is the most elegant side of the word and I record it:** Arabic has named slander by **the victim's reaction.** **The name of the offence comes not from the offender's act but from the state of the one wronged.**
