**And the link with `049-hucurat.md`:** that sūra was from beginning to end about wounding with speech — mockery, nicknames, surmise, backbiting. **Al-Aḥzāb 33/58 closes the same matter with a sentence of ruling.**

### ٱحْتَمَلُوا۟ — "they have taken upon themselves"

**Root: ح-م-ل.** To carry, to take on.

**And this root will return in the seventy-second verse of the sūra:** *wa **ḥamalahā**'l-insān* — "and man took it up."

**The comparison of the two occurrences is worth recording:**

| Verse | What is taken up | How |
|---|---|---|
| **58** | *buhtānan wa ithman mubīnā* | The slanderer takes it on unawares |
| **72** | *al-amāna* | Man takes it on by accepting it |

**The same root, once a burden taken without wanting it, once a burden undertaken.** I record it and shall return to it at the seventy-second verse.

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the fifty-eighth verse is one of those most needed today.**

**The verse ties injury to a matter of *desert*:** *bi-ghayri ma'ktasabū.* **That is, the question is not "is this person bad" but "did this person do it".**

**And the verse names injury done over something not done *buhtān*** — with a word whose root is "to leave dumbfounded".

**Its present-day form is concrete:** to speak about a person over something he has not done leaves him defenceless. Because a defence is possible only about something that actually happened. **Against something that did not happen there is no defence — only bewilderment.**

The root of the word says exactly this.

---

## 33/59 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ قُل لِّأَزْوَٰجِكَ وَبَنَاتِكَ وَنِسَآءِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ يُدْنِينَ عَلَيْهِنَّ مِن جَلَٰبِيبِهِنَّ ذَٰلِكَ أَدْنَىٰٓ أَن يُعْرَفْنَ فَلَا يُؤْذَيْنَ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا

*Yā ayyuha'l-nabiyyu qul li-azwājika wa banātika wa nisāʾi'l-muʾminīna yudnīna ʿalayhinna min jalābībihinn, dhālika adnā an yuʿrafna fa-lā yuʾdhayn, wa kāna'llāhu ghafūran raḥīmā*

"O Prophet! Say to your wives, your daughters and the believing women: let them draw their *jilbāb*s over themselves. That is more likely that they be recognised and not injured. God is Forgiving, Merciful."

### A note at the outset

**Per the method (`STYLE.md`), no legal ruling is issued on this verse.** What will be done is this:

1. The lexical sense of the word and what the lexicographers give as its counterpart in that period will be conveyed.
2. **The ground the verse itself gives will be brought forward.**
3. It will be recorded that the rulings the schools have drawn from this verse differ, and **the note "it is not binding" will be entered.**
4. **Current debates and politics will not be entered.**

### The widening of the address

**This is the sūra's fifth and last address *yā ayyuha'l-nabiyy*.**

**And those addressed are listed in three circles:**

| Circle | Who |
|---|---|
| **1** | *azwājika* — your wives |
| **2** | *banātika* — your daughters |
| **3** | *nisāʾi'l-muʾminīn* — the believing women |

**This widening must be recorded and was touched on at the thirty-third verse:** when the sūra wishes to generalise a ruling it does so **explicitly**.

**In the twenty-eighth verse the address was *azwājika* alone; in verses thirty to thirty-four it was *yā nisāʾa'l-nabiyy*. In the fifty-ninth a third circle is added.**

I record this as a datum of the text.

### يُدْنِينَ — root د-ن-و

**To be near. And in Form IV (*adnā*) "to bring near, to let hang down, to draw over".**

**And this root occurs in the sūra for the third time:** *adnā* (51), *adnā* (in the second half of 59), and here *yudnīna*. **The word *dunyā* is also from the same root (28).**

**And the root occurs twice within the verse:** *yudnīna* (the verb) and *adnā* (the elative). **Two words from the same root, one carrying the command and the other the ground.**

This is a fact of word order and I record it.

**The sense of the verb:** to **bring near, to let fall, to draw down** a thing towards oneself or towards a place. The verse describes how the garment is to be used as a **movement**.

### جَلَٰبِيب — the sense of the word

**Singular جِلْبَاب. Root: ج-ل-ب.** And the primary sense of the root is **to bring a thing from one place to another, to draw it along**: *jalaba* — he brought, he conveyed. *Jalb* — drawing, bringing.

**From this *jilbāb* — a garment worn over, brought over the body, covering.**

**What the lexicographers give as the word's counterpart in that period is this, and it carries no full certainty:**

| The descriptions found in the sources | What it says |
|---|---|
| **A wide covering that covers from head to foot** | An outer garment taken over the body |
| **A garment larger than a head-covering and smaller than a *ridāʾ*** | A size between two garments |
| **The outer covering a woman puts on over her dress** | Taken over the indoor garment |

**The lexicographers do not agree on the word's exact counterpart.** The common point that can be recorded is this: **a *jilbāb* is not an inner garment but a covering taken over when going out.**

**And the particle *min* must be dwelt on:** *min jalābībihinn* — "of their *jilbāb*s". **Whether this *min* is partitive or explicative (indicating the class) has been debated in classical exegesis.**

| Reading | What it says |
|---|---|
| **Partitive** | "Let them draw a part of their *jilbāb*s over themselves" — a portion of the covering |
| **Explicative** | "Let them draw something of the *jilbāb* kind over themselves" |

**I do not impose a preference.** The same discussion was recorded for the particle *minhum* at `048-fetih.md` 48/29.

**And I record this in addition:** the verse **does not give** how the covering is to be drawn, what area it is to cover, its measure or its form. The only thing it gives is a verb: *yudnīna ʿalayhinna.*

### ذَٰلِكَ أَدْنَىٰٓ أَن يُعْرَفْنَ فَلَا يُؤْذَيْنَ — the verse's own ground

**This is the most important place in the verse and must be brought forward.**

**Because the verse writes its own ground.** And the ground has two steps:

1. **أَن يُعْرَفْنَ** — that they be recognised.
2. **فَلَا يُؤْذَيْنَ** — and thereby not injured.

**عَرَفَ — root ع-ر-ف.** To know, to recognise. **And this root for the third time in the sūra** (in 6 and 32 as *maʿrūf*, here as *yuʿrafna*).

**The particle فَ** here conveys a consequence: the **result** of being recognised is not being injured.

**And the verb *yuʾdhayna* — the root أ-ذ-ي — occurs for the sixth time in this sūra.**

**That is, the sūra's axis of injury is tied here to a precaution**, and the close comes with a precaution.

**I enter three notes about the ground of the verse:**

**One. The ground is tied not to a fault in the woman but to a threat outside.** The verse does not say "this is more decorous" or "this is more fitting". **It states a consequence: they will not be injured.**

**Two. The ground is in the *passive*:** *yuʿrafna* (they are recognised), *yuʾdhayna* (they are injured). **The agent of neither verb is named.** That is, the verse does not name the party that recognises or the party that injures; it states only the outcome.

**Three. And the same pattern as in the fifty-first verse:** *dhālika adnā*. **Both verses bring a regulation and give its ground with this pattern**, and both grounds look to people's inward condition and to what they are exposed to.

I draw these three notes from the wording of the verse.

### Historical background — in the language of "it is transmitted"

In the classical exegetical sources it is transmitted that at that time some women in the city were harassed outside, and that this regulation aimed to prevent the harassment by making them recognisable. **In the sixtieth verse "those who spread baseless news in the city" will be named in the same context.**

**I give this account with the note "it is transmitted", do not enter into its detail, and do not make it a condition of the verse's meaning.**

**What emerges from the text itself runs in the same direction anyway:** the verse's clause of ground speaks of a **possibility of being injured.** That is, even with no information brought in from outside, the text points to an atmosphere of threat.

### On the differing rulings of the schools

**There are differences among the schools and the exegetes on the rulings drawn from this verse and from al-Nūr 24/31.** The difference gathers at these points:

| The point at issue | The situation |
|---|---|
| **The extent of the covering** | There are differing views on which areas are to be covered |
| **The function of the particle *min*** | Partitive or explicative — recorded above |
| **Whether the ruling depends on the ground** | It has been debated what the ruling would be if the ground (being recognised) were removed |
| **Its relation to al-Nūr 24/31** | How the two verses complete one another |

**No side is taken in any of these debates in this commentary and no ruling is issued.**

**And I enter the note of `STYLE.md` explicitly: what is given here is a conveying of information, and it is not binding.** A ruling about anyone's practice is not the subject of this text.

**Current debates and politics are not entered.** This verse has been the material of a great many political disputes over the last two centuries; **this commentary is not, and cannot be, a party to any of them.**

### وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا — the verse-ending

**And that the verse ends with a statement of forgiveness is worth recording.**

**This is the fourth time in the sūra** (5, 24, 50, 59). **And all four come after a regulation.**

| Verse | The regulation | The verse-ending |
|---|---|---|
| **5** | The ascription of lineage | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |
| **24** | The close of the siege section | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |
| **50** | A ruling particular to the Prophet | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |
| **59** | The *jilbāb* | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |

**Wherever it lays down a ruling the sūra enters a note of forgiveness.** I record this as my own reading; its ground is the position of the four verse-endings.

**And this has a function:** a statement of forgiveness coming after a regulation means that it is reckoned with from the outset that the regulation **cannot be carried out flawlessly.**

### What this says to the present

**In speaking about what this verse says to the present it is necessary to keep to the verse's own ground.**

**And that ground is this:** *an yuʿrafna fa-lā yuʾdhayna* — that they be recognised and not injured.

**This ground puts a fact on record:** in a society people may be under the risk of being injured outside. **And the verse accepts this as a datum and proposes a precaution.**

**But there is something the verse does *not* say, and it must be recorded:** the verse does not legitimise injury. **On the contrary, the preceding verse (58) had named injury explicitly as *buhtān* and *ithm*.**

**That the two verses come one after the other is important for this reason, and I record it as my own reading:**

| Verse | To whom | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **58** | To the one who injures | *buhtānan wa ithman mubīnā* — this is what you have taken on |
| **59** | To the one injured | A precaution |

**That is, the sūra does not resolve the matter one-sidedly.** The ruling about the one who injures was given in the preceding verse; the precaution is proposed in the following one.

**And this order must be recorded:** the ruling comes first, the precaution after. **The sūra first names the act, then states the precaution.**

This is a datum of the text and I draw no legal conclusion from it.

---

## 33/60-62 — لَّئِن لَّمْ يَنتَهِ ٱلْمُنَٰفِقُونَ وَٱلَّذِينَ فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ وَٱلْمُرْجِفُونَ فِى ٱلْمَدِينَةِ لَنُغْرِيَنَّكَ بِهِمْ ثُمَّ لَا يُجَاوِرُونَكَ فِيهَآ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا · مَّلْعُونِينَ أَيْنَمَا ثُقِفُوٓا۟ أُخِذُوا۟ وَقُتِّلُوا۟ تَقْتِيلًا · سُنَّةَ ٱللَّهِ فِى ٱلَّذِينَ خَلَوْا۟ مِن قَبْلُ وَلَن تَجِدَ لِسُنَّةِ ٱللَّهِ تَبْدِيلًا

*La-in lam yantahi'l-munāfiqūna wa'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun wa'l-murjifūna fi'l-madīnati la-nughriyannaka bihim thumma lā yujāwirūnaka fīhā illā qalīlā · Malʿūnīna aynamā thuqifū ukhidhū wa quttilū taqtīlā · Sunnata'llāhi fi'lladhīna khalaw min qabl, wa lan tajida li-sunnati'llāhi tabdīlā*

"If the hypocrites, those in whose hearts is sickness, and those who spread baseless news in the city do not desist, We shall surely set you against them; then they will be your neighbours there only a little while. · Accursed; wherever they are found they are seized and killed. · This is God's way with those who passed away before. And you will find no change in the way of God."

### Three groups

**In the twelfth verse two groups were named; here a third is added.**

| Group | The word | What |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *al-munāfiqūn* | Those with two doors |
| **2** | *alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍ* | Those with sickness in their hearts |
| **3** | *al-murjifūn* | Those who spread baseless news |

**And the listing of three separate expressions supports the observation I recorded at the twelfth verse:** the distinction appears to be deliberate.

### ٱلْمُرْجِفُون — root ر-ج-ف

**The root of this word is one of the most striking choices of word in the sūra.**

**ر-ج-ف: to shake, to make tremble, to rock violently.** *Rajfa* — a quaking; and it was treated in the context of the sūra at `079-naziat.md` (*yawma tarjufu'l-rājifa*).

**And the definition recorded at `073-muzzemmil.md`:** *"إِرْجَاف (*irjāf*) — unsettling people by spreading baseless news."* I rely on that treatment.

**This verse was already mentioned at `079-naziat.md` too.**

**Now the real question: how do the "shaking" sense of the root and the "spreading baseless news" sense come together?**

**The link the lexicographers record is this:** *irjāf* is the spreading of reports that shake a community. **That is, the word names the act not by itself but by its *effect*** — just as *buhtān* (58) is named by the state of the one wronged.

**And this sets up a word-web within the sūra, and I record it:**

| Verse | Word | Root | What is shaken |
|---|---|---|---|
| **11** | *zulzilū zilzālan shadīdā* | ز-ل-ز-ل | The believers — under siege |
| **60** | *al-murjifūn* | ر-ج-ف | The city — by a report |

**The sūra records two kinds of shaking:** one by a threat coming from outside, the other by a report spread from within. **And the word used for the second is derived from the shaking itself.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the concrete senses of the two roots are the same.

**فِى ٱلْمَدِينَةِ — the name of the city.** In the thirteenth verse *Yathrib* occurred in the mouths of those calling for flight; here *al-Madīna*. **In the same sūra, the same city, two different names** — this was recorded at the thirteenth verse as a datum of the text.

### لَنُغْرِيَنَّكَ بِهِمْ — the conditional clause

**أَغْرَىٰ — root غ-ر-و / غ-ر-ي.** To make a thing stick to another; *ghirāʾ* — glue. From this "to set someone upon a matter, to set him against".

**The Qurʾān uses the root once more:** *"So We **stirred up** enmity and hatred among them"* (al-Māʾida 5/14, 5/64 — *aghraynā*).

**And that the sentence is *conditional* must be recorded:** *la-in lam yantahi* — "if they do not desist".

**يَنتَهِ — root ن-ه-ي.** To desist, to give up; *nahy* — prohibition.

**That is, the verse does not state a ruling, it lays down a *condition*.** And whether the condition came about is not said in the text.

**This is a fact of word order that must be recorded and is important in view of the method (`STYLE.md`):** the verse is a sentence of warning and **the way out is in the first clause** — *if they do not desist.*

### مَّلْعُونِينَ أَيْنَمَا ثُقِفُوٓا۟ — on the sixty-first verse

**This verse describes a sanction to be applied in the order of a city under the conditions of that day, and it stands in a single, historical context.**

**Per the method (`STYLE.md`) I record explicitly:**

**One.** The verse is **dependent on the condition** in the sixtieth verse. It is not a sentence of ruling on its own.

**Two.** The verse concerns not an identity but an **act** that is described: shaking the city by spreading baseless news.

**Three.** No conclusion about any person or group today can be drawn from this verse. The measure recorded at `063-munafikun.md` holds here too.

**Four.** **No legal ruling at all is drawn from this verse in this commentary.**

**The words:**

**ثُقِفُوٓا۟ — root ث-ق-ف.** And the sense of the root is interesting: *thaqifa* — to find and seize a thing; and also **to straighten what is crooked** (the straightening of a spear: *tathqīf*). *Thaqīf* — quick-witted, sharp of understanding.

**The Qurʾān uses the root in several places:** *"Wherever you come upon them…"* (al-Baqara 2/191).

**قُتِّلُوا۟ تَقْتِيلًا — a cognate accusative.** The eighth reinforcement in the sūra.

### سُنَّةَ ٱللَّهِ — the second occurrence in the sūra

**It was treated at the thirty-eighth verse; I rely on that treatment.**

**And the comparison of the two occurrences is worth recording:**

| Verse | Context | For what |
|---|---|---|
| **38** | What is laid down for the Prophet | That a regulation is a continuing one |
| **62** | The order of the city | That a sanction is a continuing one |

**Both come after a regulation** — this was recorded at the thirty-eighth verse.

**وَلَن تَجِدَ لِسُنَّةِ ٱللَّهِ تَبْدِيلًا** — **the root ب-د-ل,** for the third time in this sūra (23, 52, 62).

| Verse | What does not change / is not changed |
|---|---|
| **23** | Those who kept their word did not change their word |
| **52** | That wives are not to be exchanged |
| **62** | No change is found in the way of God |

**The same root on three separate levels: a human word, a human relationship, a divine working.** I record it.

**And the note entered at `048-fetih.md` must be repeated here:** the application of the concept of *sunnat Allāh* to contemporary events is not entered into; **the concept is a measure for understanding the past, not an authority for judging the present.**

---

## 33/63-64 — يَسْـَٔلُكَ ٱلنَّاسُ عَنِ ٱلسَّاعَةِ قُلْ إِنَّمَا عِلْمُهَا عِندَ ٱللَّهِ وَمَا يُدْرِيكَ لَعَلَّ ٱلسَّاعَةَ تَكُونُ قَرِيبًا · إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَعَنَ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ وَأَعَدَّ لَهُمْ سَعِيرًا

*Yasʾaluka'l-nāsu ʿani'l-sāʿah, qul innamā ʿilmuhā ʿinda'llāh, wa mā yudrīka laʿalla'l-sāʿata takūnu qarībā · Inna'llāha laʿana'l-kāfirīna wa aʿadda lahum saʿīrā*

"People ask you about the Hour. Say: Knowledge of it is with God alone. And what will make you know? Perhaps the Hour is near. · Indeed God has cursed the disbelievers and has prepared for them a blazing fire."

### The leaving of a question unanswered

**This verse was mentioned at `079-naziat.md`** and its pattern was treated at `097-kadr.md` and `090-beled.md`. I rely on those treatments.

**The finding recorded at `090-beled.md` works here exactly:**

> *"A consistent distinction in the Qurʾān: the pattern **مَا أَدْرَاكَ** (what made you know — the perfect) is used in places where an **explanation follows**. The pattern **مَا يُدْرِيكَ** (what will make you know — the imperfect) is used in places where **no explanation follows**, where the knowledge is left closed to man (for example about the time of the resurrection: al-Aḥzāb 33/63)."*

**This verse is one of the examples of that rule, and the rule is confirmed here:** the pattern is *mā yudrīka* and no explanation follows it — what follows is a possibility: *laʿalla'l-sāʿata takūnu qarībā.*

**And what was recorded at `097-kadr.md`: *"There the time of the resurrection is asked about and is not told."***

### إِنَّمَا عِلْمُهَا عِندَ ٱللَّهِ — restriction

**إِنَّمَا** — the particle of restriction. And for the second time in this sūra (33 and 63).

**The structure of the answer is worth recording:** the verse does not have him say "I do not know"; it has him say "**the knowledge is there**". **That is, the answer is not a declaration of ignorance but the giving of an address.**

I record this as my own reading.

### لَعَلَّ ٱلسَّاعَةَ تَكُونُ قَرِيبًا

**And there is an addition in the answer:** the time is not told, but the **possibility of its being near** is stated.

**This is a structure worth recording:** the verse does not give the knowledge, but neither does it leave the knowledge **without effect.** **That the time is unknown cannot be made a ground for postponing preparation.**

**قَرِيبًا — root ق-ر-ب.** Near. **And that the word comes masculine and not feminine** (although *al-sāʿa* is feminine) is recorded by the lexicographers and explained in several ways; it produces no difference in meaning.

### The sixty-fourth verse: a transition

**Here the sūra enters its final block.** Verses sixty-four to sixty-eight set up a scene, and that scene will close the matter of "obedience" which the sūra has been treating from the beginning.

**سَعِير — root س-ع-ر.** To catch fire, to blaze. *Suʿār* — the fire of hunger; *siʿr* — a price (that rises).

---

## 33/65-68 — خَٰلِدِينَ فِيهَآ أَبَدًا لَّا يَجِدُونَ وَلِيًّا وَلَا نَصِيرًا · يَوْمَ تُقَلَّبُ وُجُوهُهُمْ فِى ٱلنَّارِ يَقُولُونَ يَٰلَيْتَنَآ أَطَعْنَا ٱللَّهَ وَأَطَعْنَا ٱلرَّسُولَا۠ · وَقَالُوا۟ رَبَّنَآ إِنَّآ أَطَعْنَا سَادَتَنَا وَكُبَرَآءَنَا فَأَضَلُّونَا ٱلسَّبِيلَا۠ · رَبَّنَآ ءَاتِهِمْ ضِعْفَيْنِ مِنَ ٱلْعَذَابِ وَٱلْعَنْهُمْ لَعْنًا كَبِيرًا

*Khālidīna fīhā abadā, lā yajidūna waliyyan wa lā naṣīrā · Yawma tuqallabu wujūhuhum fi'l-nāri yaqūlūna yā laytanā aṭaʿna'llāha wa aṭaʿna'l-rasūlā · Wa qālū rabbanā innā aṭaʿnā sādatanā wa kubarāʾanā fa-aḍallūna'l-sabīlā · Rabbanā ātihim ḍiʿfayni mina'l-ʿadhābi wa'lʿanhum laʿnan kabīrā*

"Abiding therein for ever; they will find neither friend nor helper. · On the day their faces are turned about in the Fire they will say, 'If only we had obeyed God and obeyed the Messenger.' · And they will say: Our Lord! We obeyed our masters and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way. · Our Lord! Give them a double punishment and curse them with a great curse."

### The close of the axis of obedience

**In its first verse the sūra had laid down a prohibition of obedience:** *wa lā tuṭiʿi'l-kāfirīna wa'l-munāfiqīn.*

**And in the sixty-sixth and sixty-seventh verses the consequence of that prohibition's being broken is shown as a scene.**

**The root ط-و-ع occurs four times in these four verses:**

| Verse | Word | To whom |
|---|---|---|
| **66** | *aṭaʿna'llāh* | To God (if only) |
| **66** | *aṭaʿna'l-rasūl* | To the Messenger (if only) |
| **67** | *aṭaʿnā sādatanā* | To our masters (in fact) |
| **67** | *wa kubarāʾanā* | And to our great ones |

**And the "willingness" sense of the root — recorded at the sūra's first verse — is at work here with all its weight.**

**Because *ṭāʿa* is not something done under compulsion.** The sentence does not say "they forced us"; **it says "we obeyed".** And the subject of the verb is themselves.

**This is a fact that must be recorded, and I give it as my own reading:** the verse makes those speaking frame a sentence of excuse, and that sentence **refutes itself.** The verb of the sentence framed to throw the blame on another stays upon them.

### يَوْمَ تُقَلَّبُ وُجُوهُهُمْ — root ق-ل-ب

**And here is the most striking turn of a word in the sūra.**

**The root ق-ل-ب: to turn, to turn upside down.** And *qalb* (the heart) is from the same root — **because it is the thing that constantly turns, that passes from state to state.**

**This root was opened up in the fourth verse of the sūra:** *mā jaʿala'llāhu li-rajulin min **qalbayni** fī jawfih.*

**And in the sixty-sixth verse the same root returns as a verb:** *tuqallabu wujūhuhum* — "their faces are turned about".

| Verse | Word | What |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *qalbayn* | Two hearts — an impossibility |
| **10** | *al-qulūb* | Hearts reaching the throats |
| **12** | *fī qulūbihim maraḍ* | Diseased hearts |
| **26** | *fī qulūbihim al-ruʿb* | Hearts filled with fear |
| **32** | *fī qalbihī maraḍ* | A diseased heart |
| **51** | *mā fī qulūbikum* | What is in the hearts |
| **53** | *li-qulūbikum wa qulūbihinn* | The hearts of both parties |
| **66** | *tuqallabu wujūhuhum* | **Faces turned about** |

**The sūra closes with the face the root it opened with the heart.** And that the verb is passive is worth noting: *tuqallabu* — they are turned; they do not turn themselves.

**I record this as my own reading:** the fourth verse had said that there cannot be **two centres** inside a person. In the sixty-sixth verse the faces of those who obeyed in two directions at once are turned about. **There is a link between the two ends of the root, and this link touches the founding principle of the sūra.**

**This is an inference and not a transmitted report.** Its ground is the distribution of the root in the sūra.

### سَادَتَنَا وَكُبَرَآءَنَا — two words

**سَادَة — singular سَيِّد. Root: س-و-د.** A master, a leader. **The lexicographers differ about the root of the word;** on one view it is connected with *sawād* (a crowd, a dark mass): the one who stands at the head of a group.

**كُبَرَآء — singular كَبِير. Root: ك-ب-ر.** Great. **And *kibr* is from the same root.**

**That the two words are enumerated separately is worth recording:** one is a **position** (master), the other a **standing** (great one). **That is, there are two separate reasons for following: authority and prestige.**

I record this as my own reading.

**And one further note, per the method (`STYLE.md`):** the verse gives no names. **And no person or group today can be identified from this verse.** What the verse describes is a **form of relationship**: a person's handing over his own choice to another's position.

### ٱلرَّسُولَا۠ / ٱلسَّبِيلَا۠ — a difference of reading

**The difference of reading recorded for *al-ẓunūnā* in the tenth verse holds here too.**

The alif at the end of these three words — *al-ẓunūnā* (10), *al-Rasūlā* (66), *al-Sabīlā* (67) — **is read differently among the readers:** some read it both in continuous recitation and at a pause, some only at a pause, some not at all.

**It does not change the meaning;** it is a difference of reading bound up with the harmony of the verse-endings, and in the script of the muṣḥaf the alif is written.

**And that these three words fall at three separate places in the sūra is worth noting:** one in the middle of the siege, two in the final scene.

### رَبَّنَآ ءَاتِهِمْ ضِعْفَيْنِ — a request

**The root ض-ع-ف** was treated at the thirtieth verse: both weakness and doubling.

**And the expression *ḍiʿfayn* occurs in the sūra for the second time:**

| Verse | For whom | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **30** | The Prophet's wives (in a conditional clause) | The weight of the standing |
| **68** | Those who led astray | The weight of leading another astray |

**The same expression, for two different reasons.** And what joins the two is this: **being in a position that affects others.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that both occurrences of the expression concern a standing.

### What this says to the present

**The sentence of the sixty-seventh verse is one of the most often repeated excuses in human history:** *"We obeyed our masters and our great ones; they led us astray."*

**And the verse does not accept this excuse. The reason is said by the sentence's own verb:** *aṭaʿnā* — **we obeyed.**

**The finding recorded at `037-saffat.md` for a similar scene holds here too:** *"This is not a defence but a levelling. The other side was saying 'you led me astray'; the answer becomes 'yes, but we too were astray.' That is, the offence is admitted but the difference is not removed."*

**In al-Aḥzāb there is one step further:** here the other side's answer is not given. **The sentence stands one-sided and rots from within.**

**Its present-day form is plain:** a person's going along behind an institution, a leader, a group is a **choice** — and a choice, however natural it may look, is that person's own act. **The verse says that responsibility cannot be handed over.**

---

## 33/69 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَا تَكُونُوا۟ كَٱلَّذِينَ ءَاذَوْا۟ مُوسَىٰ فَبَرَّأَهُ ٱللَّهُ مِمَّا قَالُوا۟ وَكَانَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ وَجِيهًا

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū lā takūnū ka'lladhīna ādhaw Mūsā fa-barraʾahu'llāhu mimmā qālū, wa kāna ʿinda'llāhi wajīhā*

"O you who believe! Do not be like those who injured Moses. God cleared him of what they said. And he was of high standing with God."

### The last link of the axis of injury

**The root أ-ذ-ي occurs in this verse for the seventh and last time.**

**And the sūra closes the root by tying it to a past example.** In the forty-eighth verse the Prophet had been told "pay no heed to their injury"; in the fifty-third the discomfort given unawares was recorded; in the fifty-seventh and fifty-eighth a ruling was issued; in the fifty-ninth a precaution was proposed.

**The sixty-ninth verse adds a dimension of history to the matter:** this is nothing new.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the distribution of the root in the sūra.

### مِمَّا قَالُوا۟ — what it was that was said

**The verse does not give what it was that was said.** It says only that there was something said and that God cleared him of it.

**This is a datum of the text and must be recorded.** Various narratives are transmitted about this in classical exegesis; **I do not enter into them**, because the wording of the verse gives no content and the reports differ at the level of detail.

**And there is one further reason for this:** the function of the verse lies not in the **content** of the event but in its **structure.** The verse does not say "such-and-such a slander was made"; **it says "something was said and God cleared him of it".** The structure works independently of the content.

**And a link must be made with the preceding section:** the fifty-eighth verse had named injury over something not done *buhtān*. **The sixty-ninth verse gives an instance of the same structure in history.**

### بَرَّأَ — root ب-ر-أ

**To be free or quit of a thing, to slip clear, to be delivered.** *Barāʾa* — distance, being clear of liability. **This root was treated at `060-mumtehine.md`** (*buraʾāʾ* — 60/4).

**And another branch of the root: *bāriʾ* — the maker** (treated at `059-hasr.md`). The lexicographers join the two branches on the common ground of "one thing's being separated from another".

**The pattern: Form II (*barraʾa*)** — it conveys intensity and completeness: "he cleared him entirely".

### وَجِيه — root و-ج-ه

**And the primary sense of the root is the face: *wajh*. *Wajīh* — one who has a face, one held in esteem, honoured; one whose face is clear before a community.**

**And the choice of this word sets up a contrast with the preceding verse, and this is a fact of word order that must be recorded:**

| Verse | Word | The face |
|---|---|---|
| **66** | *tuqallabu **wujūhuhum*** | Faces turned about in the Fire |
| **69** | *kāna ʿinda'llāhi **wajīhā*** | His face clear before God |

**The same root, three verses apart, in two opposite states.** One the turning about of the face, the other the honour of the face.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two words are from the same root.

**And the Qurʾān uses this word once more:** *"The Messiah, Jesus son of Mary… of high standing (**wajīh**) in this world and in the hereafter"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/45).

### What this says to the present

**The verse gives a command: *lā takūnū ka'lladhīna…* — "do not be like them."**

**And the form of the command is worth recording:** the verse does not forbid a behaviour; **it forbids resembling a position.**

**The difference is this:** forbidding a behaviour requires that the behaviour be recognisable. **Asking that one not resemble a position requires that the person compare his own situation with an example.**

And comparison is harder, because a person generally does not call what he himself does by the name of that example.

**And the last sentence of the verse lays down a measure:** *wa kāna ʿinda'llāhi wajīhā.* **That is, what was said did not change the worth of the one it was said about.**

**This agrees exactly with the founding principle of the sūra:** the fourth verse said that speech does not change reality. **The sixty-ninth verse repeats the same principle for slander.**

**Slander too is a piece of speech, and it too stays in the mouth.** I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two verses set up the same structure (speech / reality).

---

## 33/70-71 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ ٱتَّقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَقُولُوا۟ قَوْلًا سَدِيدًا · يُصْلِحْ لَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ وَيَغْفِرْ لَكُمْ ذُنُوبَكُمْ وَمَن يُطِعِ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥ فَقَدْ فَازَ فَوْزًا عَظِيمًا

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanu'ttaqu'llāha wa qūlū qawlan sadīdā · Yuṣliḥ lakum aʿmālakum wa yaghfir lakum dhunūbakum, wa man yuṭiʿi'llāha wa rasūlahu fa-qad fāza fawzan ʿaẓīmā*

"O you who believe! Guard yourselves against God and speak a firm word. · Then He will set your works right for you and forgive you your sins. And whoever obeys God and His Messenger has attained a great attainment."

### The axis of speech closes here

**The sūra's axis of speech had opened in the fourth verse** (*qawlukum bi-afwāhikum*) and taken a measure in the thirty-second (*qawlan maʿrūfā*). **In the seventieth verse the final measure comes.**

| Verse | The construction | The quality |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* | Speech that stays in the mouth |
| **32** | *lā takhḍaʿna bi'l-qawl* | Bending speech |
| **32** | *qawlan maʿrūfā* | Recognised speech |
| **70** | *qawlan sadīdā* | **Firm speech** |

### سَدِيد — root س-د-د

**And the concrete sense of the root must be dwelt on, because the whole weight of the word lies there.**

**س-د-د: to close a breach, to stop it, to fill it.** *Sadd* — a dam, a barrier (at al-Kahf 18/94 as *saddan*). *Sidād* — the stopper that closes the gap in a thing.

**And the second branch of the root: *sadād* — accuracy, correctness, hitting the mark.** *Saddada'l-ramya* — he made the shot hit its mark.

**What joins the two branches?**

**The link the lexicographers record is this: both convey an *exact fit*.** A dam is what fits the breach exactly; an accurate shot is one that lands exactly on the target. **That is, *sadīd* means what has neither deficiency nor excess, what fills its place exactly.**

**And this describes what "firm speech" is, and I record it:**

*Qawlan sadīdā* is not merely "true speech". In the logic of the root: **speech that has no gap, that leaves no space, that hits its mark exactly.**

**Its opposite is not a lie — it is *breached* speech:** true but incomplete, true but open to misunderstanding, true but out of its time or its place.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the two branches of sense in the root.

**The Qurʾān uses the word once more, and the context there is instructive too:** *"Let them guard themselves against God and let them speak a firm word"* (al-Nisāʾ 4/9) — there the context is the property of orphans and the situation of those left behind. **In both places the word stands in a context of speech that *produces consequences*.**

### يُصْلِحْ لَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ — the outcome

**And the most striking link of the verse is here.**

**The seventieth verse is about *speech*; the seventy-first about *works*.** And the two are joined by a relation of condition and consequence: speak a firm word **so that** your works may be set right.

**أَصْلَحَ — root ص-ل-ح.** To make a thing useful, workable, serviceable. *Ṣalāḥ* — a thing's being in a state fit for its own work; the opposite of *fasād*.

**And what must be recorded is this:** the verse ties the setting right of speech to the setting right of **works**.

**This order looks reversed.** The expectation was: set your works right so that your speech may be true. **The verse says the opposite.**

**And the sūra as a whole supports this order:**

- **The fourth verse:** a false word was trying to establish a false reality.
- **The thirteenth verse:** a word of excuse was covering a flight.
- **The nineteenth verse:** sharp tongues spoke after the danger had passed.
- **The fifty-eighth verse:** a word of slander was loading on something that had not been done.

**Throughout the sūra the corruption of speech comes *before* the corruption of behaviour.** The seventieth verse begins the setting right from the same place.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the speech-behaviour orderings in the sūra.

### فَازَ فَوْزًا عَظِيمًا

**Again a cognate accusative.** The ninth reinforcement in the sūra.

**The root ف-و-ز:** to be delivered, to attain one's desire; and a branch the lexicographers record: *mafāza* — a desert. **The lexicographers differ on the link between the two senses;** on one explanation the desert was named "the place of deliverance" in the hope of deliverance from it (an auspicious naming). **This is not certain; I record it but build no meaning upon it.**

### What this says to the present

**The measure of the verse can be gathered into a single sentence: setting right begins with speech.**

**And the root of the word *sadīd* makes this concrete:** firm speech is speech that **leaves no breach.**

**Its practical counterpart is this:** it is not enough for a sentence to be true. The place left incomplete, the side left open to misunderstanding, the part left unsaid — all of these are breaches. **And a breach is later filled with something else.**

All the measures set out about speech at `049-hucurat.md` gather in this word: the loudness of a voice, the share of surmise, the effect of a nickname, the anatomy of backbiting. **All of them had to do with how a piece of speech leaves a breach.**

---

## 33/72 — إِنَّا عَرَضْنَا ٱلْأَمَانَةَ عَلَى ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱلْجِبَالِ فَأَبَيْنَ أَن يَحْمِلْنَهَا وَأَشْفَقْنَ مِنْهَا وَحَمَلَهَا ٱلْإِنسَٰنُ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ ظَلُومًا جَهُولًا

*Innā ʿaraḍna'l-amānata ʿala'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi wa'l-jibāli fa-abayna an yaḥmilnahā wa ashfaqna minhā wa ḥamalaha'l-insān, innahu kāna ẓalūman jahūlā*

"We offered the trust to the heavens, the earth and the mountains; they refused to carry it and shrank from it. And man took it up. He is indeed much given to wrong, much given to ignorance."

### The closing verse of the sūra

**This is the densest verse of the sūra, and it does the most unexpected thing a closing verse can do: it ties the whole subject of the sūra to man's position on the earth.**

For seventy-one verses concrete things have been recounted: a siege, a regulation about adoption, a code of household conduct, a garment, a measure for speech. **In the seventy-second verse the text suddenly changes altitude.**

**And this transition has a logic, and I record it as my own reading:** everything recounted through the sūra was a matter of **carrying**. A pledge given was to be carried (15, 23); a standing was to be carried (30-31); a boundary was to be carried (53); a word was to be carried (70). **The seventy-second verse takes carrying itself as its subject.**

### عَرَضْنَا — root ع-ر-ض

**To lay a thing out in its breadth, to show, to offer.** *ʿArḍ* — breadth (the opposite of *ṭūl*, length). *Maʿriḍ* — a place of display. *Iʿrāḍ* — turning away (turning aside).

**And the concrete sense of the root — laying out in breadth — builds the picture of the verse:** the trust was **spread out** before them. Not hidden, not imposed; set out.

**The Qurʾān uses the root in a comparable scene:** *"Then He **set them before** the angels"* (al-Baqara 2/31 — *thumma ʿaraḍahum ʿala'l-malāʾika*). **That scene was treated at `002-bakara.md`.**

**The parallel between the two scenes is worth recording:** in both there is a *setting before*, and in both a distinction is drawn between man and other beings.

### ٱلْأَمَانَة — root أ-م-ن

**The primary sense of the root: to be in safety, to be without fear.** *Amn* — security; *īmān* — trusting, giving security; *amīn* — trustworthy; *amāna* — what is left in trust.

**This root was treated at `002-bakara.md`, `049-hucurat.md` and `048-fetih.md`; I rely on those treatments.**

**And there is a word-web that must be recorded:** *amāna* and *īmān* are from the same root. **In the thirty-fifth verse of the sūra *al-muʾminīna wa'l-muʾmināt* were enumerated; in the seventy-second the same root returns as a burden.**

**That is, the sūra opens the root as an attribute and closes it as a responsibility.** I record this as my own reading.

### What the trust is — the disagreement

**The verse does not say what the trust is.** And in classical exegesis this is one of the most debated questions.

**I give the disagreement in a table. I do not impose a preference.**

| View | What the trust is | Its ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Obligation and responsibility** | The whole of religious obligation; being the addressee of commands and prohibitions | The continuation of the verse: the one who accepted is described as *ẓalūm* and *jahūl*; the enumeration of outcomes in verse 73 | The concept is wide and its limit is indefinite |
| **B. Reason and will** | The power of choice; the capacity to distinguish good from evil and to prefer | That the heavens and the mountains do not have this power; that obligation can be laid only upon a will | The word "reason" does not occur in the verse |
| **C. The obligatory acts / acts of worship** | Prayer, fasting and similar particular obligations | The sense of the word *amāna* as "what is left and must be returned" | It narrows the extent; it does not meet the weight of the verse |
| **D. Vicegerency / responsibility for the earth** | Man's position on the earth and his responsibility to cultivate it | The parallel with al-Baqara 2/30 | The word "vicegerent" does not occur in the verse |
| **E. Trusts between people** | What people leave with one another — property, a secret, an office | The lexical sense of the word; al-Nisāʾ 4/58 (*addu'l-amānāti ilā ahlihā*) | Its being offered to the heavens and the mountains is hard to explain |

**Two notes about this table:**

**One.** These views do not entirely exclude one another. A, B and D are readings that feed one another: responsibility is possible only if there is a will; and a position on the earth is a position of responsibility.

**Two.** **That the verse leaves the word indefinite is a datum of the text.** *Al-amāna* comes with the definite article — that is, it points to something known — but its content is not opened.

I record this as an observation, not as a preference.

### فَأَبَيْنَ أَن يَحْمِلْنَهَا — a comparison with Fuṣṣilat 41/11

**And now comes the strongest datum of the text for this verse.**

**At `041-fussilet.md` 41/11 the heavens and the earth were addressed:** *"He said to it and to the earth, 'Come, willingly or unwillingly'; and they both said, 'We come willingly'"* — **ٱئْتِيَا طَوْعًا أَوْ كَرْهًا قَالَتَآ أَتَيْنَا طَآئِعِينَ.**

**The point of word order recorded there was this, and I bring it over here:** *"The offer is made with two options (*ṭawʿan aw karhan*), and the answer comes with one: *ataynā ṭāʾiʿīn* — 'we come willingly.'"*

**Now let us set the two verses side by side.**

| | **Fuṣṣilat 41/11** | **al-Aḥzāb 33/72** |
|---|---|---|
| **Those addressed** | The heaven and the earth | The heavens, the earth and the mountains |
| **The verb** | *iʾtiyā* — "come" (a command) | *ʿaraḍnā* — "We offered" (a setting before) |
| **The option** | *ṭawʿan aw karhan* — willingly or by compulsion | No option is offered; the thing is set out |
| **The answer** | *ataynā **ṭāʾiʿīn*** — we came willingly | *fa-**abayna*** — they refused |
| **The accompanying state** | — | *wa ashfaqna minhā* — they shrank from it |
| **The outcome** | Obedience | The burden was taken by another |

**What emerges from this comparison is powerful, and I record it.**

**The same beings give two opposite answers to two different calls.**

**And the difference lies in the kind of call.**

**In Fuṣṣilat there was a *command*:** *iʾtiyā* — come. And the counterpart of a command is obedience. **The answer: we came willingly.**

**In al-Aḥzāb there is a *setting before*:** *ʿaraḍnā* — We offered. And the counterpart of a setting before is a **choice**. **The answer: we refused.**

**That is, the heavens and the earth obey a command but do not take a choice.**

**And this is the strongest clue the verse itself gives about what the trust is.** The trust is not something to be obeyed — it is **something to be taken up.** Complying with a command and taking on a burden are different things.

I record this as my own reading. **Its ground is the choice of verb in the two verses:** one *iʾtiyā* (a command), the other *ʿaraḍnā* (a setting before); one *ṭāʾiʿīn* (obedience), the other *abayna* (refusal).

**The picture that emerges when the two verses are read together is this:** the universe does flawlessly what is asked of it. The only thing it does not do is **choose what shall be asked.**

### أَبَيْنَ — root أ-ب-ي

**To refuse, not to accept, to decline.**

**And there is a note on the word:** *ibāʾ*, according to the lexicographers, is not an ordinary refusal; it carries the sense of **shrinking from a thing, keeping away from it.**

**The Qurʾān uses the root in several places:** *"Except Iblīs; he refused"* (al-Baqara 2/34 — *illā Iblīsa **abā***); *"God will not have it otherwise than to perfect His light"* (al-Tawba 9/32 — *wa yaʾba'llāh*).

**And the difference between the case of Iblīs and this verse is worth recording:** there *ibāʾ* is named together with arrogance (*abā wa'stakbara*). **Here it is named together with shrinking** (*abayna wa ashfaqna*).

**That is, the same verb can arise from two different inward states:** one arrogance, the other apprehension. **And the verse ties the heavens' refusal to the second.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the accompanying verbs in the two verses.

### وَأَشْفَقْنَ مِنْهَا — root ش-ف-ق

**And this word is the finest point of the verse.**

**ش-ف-ق: a thing's being thin, growing thin.** *Shafaq* — the thin band of sky at sunset, between day and night. *Shafaqa* — that which grows thin.

**And from this two senses arise, and both stand together in the word:**

| Sense | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **Fear, apprehension, shrinking** | Dreading a thing's bad outcome |
| **Tenderness, compassion** | Being anxious lest something bad befall someone |

**What joins the two: *growing thin*.** The lexicographers explain it thus: *ishfāq* is a state in which fear and concern are mixed at once — **not pure fear, but fear mixed with care.**

**The Qurʾān uses the root in this twofold sense:** *"And they **stand in awe** of their Lord"* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/28 — *min khashyatihī **mushfiqūn***); *"They are **apprehensive** about the Hour"* (al-Shūrā 42/18).

**And the word *shafaq* (84/16) was treated at `084-insikak.md`; I rely on that treatment.**

**Now the function of the word in this verse:**

**The verse does not depict the refusal of the heavens and the mountains as a *cowardice*.** The word used conveys a fine, concerned shrinking that reckons with the outcome.

**That is, the refusal is not a flight; it is the result of a *measuring*.** The weight of the burden was seen and it was understood that it could not be carried.

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the "growing thin" sense of the root and its uses in the Qurʾān.

### وَحَمَلَهَا ٱلْإِنسَٰنُ

**The root ح-م-ل** occurred in the fifty-eighth verse (*iḥtamalū buhtānan*) and I pointed there to this verse.

| Verse | Pattern | What is taken up | How |
|---|---|---|---|
| **58** | *iḥtamalū* (Form VIII) | *buhtān* and *ithm* | Unawares |
| **72** | *ḥamala* (the bare form) | *al-amāna* | By undertaking it |

**And the difference of pattern is worth noting:** Form VIII generally conveys an effort and an intent; the bare form conveys the act directly. **The lexicographers lay down no firm rule here; I record it but build no heavy meaning upon it.**

**ٱلْإِنسَٰن — root أ-ن-س** (it occurred in the fifty-third verse as *mustaʾnisīn*) **or ن-س-ي** (to forget). **The lexicographers divide into two and there is no settled preference.**

**And both derivations agree with this verse in an interesting way:**

- **From أ-ن-س:** man is the being that grows familiar, that draws near. **The one who takes the trust is the party that draws near.**
- **From ن-س-ي:** man is the being that forgets. **And the last word of the verse is *jahūl*.**

**I give this link as an observation; I adopt no preference for either derivation.**

**That the word comes with the definite article (*al-insān*) conveys the genus:** not a particular person but man as a kind.

### إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ ظَلُومًا جَهُولًا — blame or praise

**The last sentence of the verse is the place most spoken about. And the debate is this: are these two adjectives a censure, or an explanation, or a praise?**

**Both words are in the intensive form (*faʿūl*):**

| Word | Root | The plain form | The intensive form |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ظَلُوم** | ظ-ل-م | *ẓālim* | *ẓalūm* — much given to wrong |
| **جَهُول** | ج-ه-ل | *jāhil* | *jahūl* — much given to ignorance |

**The concrete sense of the root ظ-ل-م: darkness.** *Ẓulma* — darkness; and *ẓulm* — to put a thing somewhere other than its place. It was treated at `002-bakara.md`.

**The root ج-ه-ل:** not to know; and *jahl*, according to the lexicographers, conveys not only ignorance but **want of measure and rashness** — and this is also the sense the word *jāhiliyya* (which occurred in verse 33) carries.

**I give the disagreement in a table. I convey it impartially and impose no preference.**

| View | What it says | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Censure** | Man is censured for taking on a burden he cannot carry. The two intensive adjectives state a fault | That both words are used in a negative sense in the Qurʾān; the mention of punishment in verse 73 |
| **B. Explanation (not censure but cause)** | The adjectives explain why man was able to take the burden: he took it because he did not know the measure. This is not a reproach but an account | Its beginning with *innahu* — the sentence is built like a clause of ground |
| **C. Conditional censure** | The adjectives hold for the man who does not carry the trust, not for the one who carries it | That verse 73 has two poles: punishment and repentance |
| **D. That it has a side of praise** | Man's taking this burden is what separates him from the heavens and the mountains; the adjectives state the price of that courage | That the verb *ḥamala* does not come with a negative qualification; that the verse records man as the sole bearer |

**Three notes about this table:**

**One. I do not impose a preference.** All four views are represented in classical and modern exegesis.

**Two. I record two data that emerge from the wording of the verse:**

- The sentence begins with *innahu kāna* — **a pattern of ground or explanation.** The verse does not say "how badly he did"; it says "**because he is such**".
- And both adjectives are in the **intensive** form. In Arabic an intensive can convey either censure or the intensity of a quality.

**Three. The seventy-third verse sets a limit to the debate:** the verse enumerates two outcomes — punishment and the acceptance of repentance. **That is, the outcome of taking up the trust is not one-directional.**

**And this is a datum of the text in favour of views C and D.** I record this as an observation, not as a preference.

### The place of the verse in the sūra — the logic of the close

**And now the verse's link with the sūra as a whole.**

**In the fourth verse the sūra laid down a principle: a name does not change reality.** And that principle was saying that man can do easy things with speech and must do hard things with reality.

**The seventy-second verse gives that hardness its name: *amāna*.**

**And there is a link between the two verses, which I record as my own reading:**

| | **33/4** | **33/72** |
|---|---|---|
| **What** | *qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* | *al-amāna* |
| **Where it stays** | In the mouth | On the back |
| **Its cost** | None | There is one |
| **Who carries it** | No one | *al-insān* |

**The sūra opens with a word that stays in the mouth and closes with a burden carried on the back.**

**And the seventy verses between them describe the distance between these two ends:** the word given (7, 15), the word kept (23), the word broken (26), the excuse said (13), the slander said (58), the firm word (70).

I record this as my own reading; its ground is that the two verses stand at the two ends of the sūra and that both frame a matter of "carrying".

### What this says to the present

**One. The real contrast the verse sets up is between power and responsibility.**

The verse enumerates three things: the heavens, the earth, the mountains. **And all three are incomparably greater than man.** But it is they who shrink from carrying the trust.

**That is, the verse does not measure the power to carry by size.** The trust is not a weight; it is a **responsibility.** And responsibility is not carried by mass.

I record this as my own reading.

**Two. The measure of the word *ashfaqna*.**

The refusal of the heavens comes not from cowardice but from measuring. **And man's acceptance from not being able to measure.**

**This is the most honest side of the verse:** man did not take this burden knowing exactly what it was. The word *jahūl* says this.

**And its present-day form is familiar:** people most often take on responsibilities without knowing their weight. On entering a work, on giving a word, on building a life. **And the weight is understood afterwards.**

**The verse records this as a fault — but in the seventy-third verse it leaves the door open.**

**Three. What the comparison with Fuṣṣilat says to the present.**

The universe does flawlessly what is asked of it (41/11). **Man, by contrast, can choose — and precisely for that reason he can err.**

**And when the two verses are read together, they say something about man's position:** man's distinguishing feature is not his power but **his capacity to be wrong.** Without choice there is no error; and without error there is no responsibility.

I record this as my own reading, and I give it not as a theological claim but as a reading that emerges from the comparison of two verses.

---

## 33/73 — لِّيُعَذِّبَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُنَٰفِقِينَ وَٱلْمُنَٰفِقَٰتِ وَٱلْمُشْرِكِينَ وَٱلْمُشْرِكَٰتِ وَيَتُوبَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَى ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا

*Li-yuʿadhdhiba'llāhu'l-munāfiqīna wa'l-munāfiqāti wa'l-mushrikīna wa'l-mushrikāti wa yatūba'llāhu ʿala'l-muʾminīna wa'l-muʾmināt, wa kāna'llāhu ghafūran raḥīmā*

"This is so that God may punish the hypocrite men and the hypocrite women, the mushrik men and the mushrik women, and turn in forgiveness to the believing men and the believing women. God is Forgiving, Merciful."

### The outcome of the trust

**The verse is attached to the seventy-second by *li-* (the *lām* of causation):** "so that…". **That is, the outcome of the trust's being taken up.**

**And the outcome has two poles:** punishment and the acceptance of repentance.

**This sets a limit to the debate in the seventy-second verse**, and it was recorded there: the outcome of taking up the trust is not one-directional.

### Three pairs, six words

**And the word order of the verse must be recorded: three groups, all with the man-woman pair.**

| Group | Men | Women | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | *al-munāfiqīn* | *al-munāfiqāt* | Punishment |
| **2** | *al-mushrikīn* | *al-mushrikāt* | Punishment |
| **3** | *al-muʾminīn* | *al-muʾmināt* | The acceptance of repentance |

**And this closes a ring with the thirty-fifth verse of the sūra.**

| | **33/35** | **33/73** |
|---|---|---|
| **How many pairs** | Ten | Three |
| **What is enumerated** | Attributes | Outcomes |
| **Direction** | All positive | Two negative, one positive |
| **The close** | *maghfiratan wa ajran ʿaẓīmā* | *ghafūran raḥīmā* |

**The sūra uses the man-woman pair in four places: 35 (attributes), 36 (obligation), 58 (those injured), 73 (outcomes).** All four open the extent explicitly to both sides.

I record this as a datum of the text.

### The order of the outcomes

**And there is a point of word order:** the verse enumerates the two negative groups **first** and the positive group **afterwards**. And it ends with the acceptance of repentance.

**This is a repetition of the pattern recorded at `048-fetih.md`:** the Qurʾān often closes its hard sections with a statement of forgiveness.

**And in this sūra the same verse-ending comes for the fifth time:** *ghafūran raḥīmā* (5, 24, 50, 59 and 73 — five times).

### يَتُوبَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَى — the direction of the pattern

**The root ت-و-ب:** to turn. *Tawba* — a turning.

**And the interesting side of the pattern is this:** in Arabic *tāba ʿala'l-ʿabd* (God accepted the servant's repentance) and *tāba'l-ʿabdu ila'llāh* (the servant turned to God) — **the same verb, and when the particle changes the direction changes.**

**Here the particle is *ʿalā*:** *yatūba'llāhu **ʿala**'l-muʾminīn* — that is, the subject of the verb is God.

**And this completes the word order of the verse:** for two groups a verb of punishment (*yuʿadhdhiba*), for the third a verb of turning (*yatūba*). **Both verbs have the same subject.**

### On the close of the sūra

**And the last words of the sūra set up a link with its first verse.**

The sūra had begun with *ittaqi'llāh* — a search for protection. **And it ends with *ghafūran raḥīmā*** — a statement of forgiveness.

**That is, the sūra opens with the search for protection and closes with the source of protection.**

I record this as my own reading; its ground is the word choices of the first and the last verse.

---

## The sūra as a whole — looking back

Having finished the seventy-three verses, when one looks back at the sūra from the beginning it becomes visible that a text which looked scattered in fact turns around a single idea.

### One. The founding principle and its four applications

**The first sentence of the fourth verse — *"God has not made two hearts inside any man"* — is the key to reading the sūra.** And the sentence that follows turns it into a ruling: *dhālikum qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* — this is what you say with your mouths.

**The applications of the principle within the sūra:**

| Where | Which saying | What it could not establish |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | The ẓihār formula | Motherhood |
| **4-5, 37, 40** | The ascription of the adopted son | Lineage |
| **13** | *"Our houses are exposed"* | A legitimate excuse |
| **12** | *"The promise is nothing but delusion"* | An assessment of reality |
| **58, 69** | Slander | A person's worth |
| **67** | *"Our masters led us astray"* | Escape from responsibility |

**In six separate places, the same structure:** a saying is framed and cannot pass into reality.

**And what the sūra sets against it is each time an *act*:** to give birth (4), to ascribe to his father (5), to keep one's word (23), to give in marriage (37), to speak firmly (70), to take up a burden (72).

I record this as my own reading.

### Two. The two ends of the sūra

The sūra opens with a matter of speech and closes with a matter of burden.

| | **The opening (4)** | **The close (72)** |
|---|---|---|
| **What** | Speech that stays in the mouth | A trust carried on the back |
| **Its cost** | None | There is one |
| **Who can do it** | Everyone | The heavens and the mountains refused |

**And the distance between them is the whole content of the sūra:** the difference between what is easily said and what is hard to carry.

### Three. What the siege section does

Nineteen verses (9-27) are given to a military event, but **not a single military movement is described in the section.**

What is recounted: a wind, the state of the eyes, the state of the hearts, surmises, the sentences that were said, the increase of faith, the return in rage.

**And at the centre of the section stand two sentences:**

| Verse | Who | Looking at the same scene, what they said |
|---|---|---|
| **12** | The hypocrites and those with sickness in their hearts | *mā waʿadana'llāhu… illā ghurūrā* |
| **22** | The believers | *hādhā mā waʿadana'llāhu wa rasūluh* |

**The difference is not in the knowledge but in the conclusion drawn from the same data.** This is the strongest datum of the text in the sūra and it was recorded at the twelfth verse.

### Four. The word-webs in the sūra

**The sūra is unusually insistent on using the same root in different places with different functions.** I gather here what I recorded in separate places:

| Root | Where | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **د-ع-و** | 4 (*adʿiyāʾ*), 5 (*udʿūhum*), 46 (*dāʿiyan ila'llāh*) | The direction of the call is corrected twice |
| **ظ-ه-ر** | 4 (*tuẓāhirūna* — ẓihār), 26 (*ẓāharūhum* — support) | The same root, two entirely different senses; what joins them is the *back* |
| **و-ل-ي** | 5 (*mawālī*), 6 (*awlā*), 15 (*yuwallūna'l-adbār*), 17 (*waliyy*) | Nearness and turning the back — the two directions of the root |
| **ق-ل-ب** | 4, 10, 12, 26, 32, 51, 53, 66 | Opens with the heart and closes with the turning of the face |
| **س-ل-م** | 22 (*taslīm*), 35 (*muslimīn*), 44 (*salām*), 56 (*sallimū*) | From a state to an attribute, from an attribute to a recompense |
| **ص-د-ق** | 8, 22, 23, 24, 35 | Truthfulness asked about, truthfulness recompensed |
| **أ-ذ-ي** | 48, 53 (twice), 57, 58, 59, 69 | Seven occurrences: every face of injury |
| **ب-د-ل** | 23, 52, 62 | Word, relationship, divine working — unchangingness on three levels |
| **ح-م-ل** | 58 (*iḥtamalū*), 72 (*ḥamala*) | A burden taken without wanting it / a burden undertaken |
| **ح-ر-ج** | 37, 38, 50 | The removal of the same constriction, on three sides |
| **د-ن-و** | 28 (*dunyā*), 51 (*adnā*), 59 (*yudnīna*, *adnā*) | Nearness — as time, as movement, and as ground |

### Five. The cognate accusative as a grammatical instrument

**The sūra uses the pattern of reinforcing a verb with its verbal noun (the cognate accusative) nine times.** This is a high density among the sūras of the Qurʾān.

| Verse | The construction | What is reinforced |
|---|---|---|
| **11** | *zulzilū **zilzālan** shadīdā* | The violence of the shaking |
| **23** | *mā baddalū **tabdīlā*** | The absoluteness of not changing |
| **33** | *yuṭahhirakum **taṭhīrā*** | The completeness of the purification |
| **36** | *ḍalla **ḍalālan** mubīnā* | The plainness of the straying |
| **38** | *qadaran **maqdūrā*** | The certainty of the determining |
| **41** | *udhkuru'llāha **dhikran** kathīrā* | The muchness of the remembrance |
| **56** | *sallimū **taslīmā*** | The completeness of the submission |
| **61** | *quttilū **taqtīlā*** | The certainty of the sanction |
| **71** | *fāza **fawzan** ʿaẓīmā* | The greatness of the attainment |

**And this choice agrees with the content of the sūra, and I record it as my own reading:** from beginning to end the sūra takes as its subject **the distance between speech and reality.** And the cognate accusative is in Arabic the instrument for confirming a saying **with its own verbal noun** — it makes the sentence firm with the very act it expresses.

**That is, while it recounts that speech cannot pass into reality, the sūra frames the sayings that do pass into reality with the firmest grammatical pattern.**

### Six. The house, the central place of the sūra

The word **بيت** occurs again and again in the sūra, and each time with a different face:

| Verse | The house |
|---|---|
| **13** | *buyūtunā ʿawra* — the house claimed to be unprotected |
| **33** | *qarna fī buyūtikunna* — the house to be stayed in with dignity |
| **33** | *ahl al-bayt* — the people of the house |
| **34** | *mā yutlā fī buyūtikunna* — the house where the revelation is recited |
| **53** | *buyūt al-nabiyy* — the house not to be entered without leave |

**The sūra begins at the houses of a city under siege and goes as far as the door of a prophet's house.**

**And the difference between the two ends is worth recording:** in the thirteenth verse the house was an **excuse**; in the fifty-third it is a **boundary.** I give this as my own reading.

### Seven. The sūra's seven measures for the present

**One. Changing the name of a thing does not change its reality** (4-5). And naming is most often a tool in the hand of the stronger; the cost is paid by the one on whom the name is fixed.

**Two. An excuse has a recognisable structure** (13): it rests on verifiable ground, it frees the person from choice, and it puts someone else forward.

**Three. To be shaken is not to leave faith** (11, 23). The measure is not whether there was a shaking; it is whether the word was changed.

**Four. Exemplarity is conditional** (21). An example guides the person who is already looking in a direction; in one who is not looking it produces no effect. And in the root of the word *uswa* there is the binding of a wound: an example is the name for saying "this is a thing that can be done".

**Five. Some boundaries cannot be defended by the one they belong to** (53). That a person does not say he is uncomfortable does not mean that he is not uncomfortable. The objection may not come — because objecting has a cost of its own.

**Six. Setting right begins with speech** (70). The root of the word *sadīd* is the stopper that closes a breach: firm speech is speech that leaves no gap. And a breach is later filled with something else.

**Seven. Responsibility is not carried by mass** (72). The heavens, the earth and the mountains are incomparably greater than man, and they shrank from carrying the trust. The power to carry is not measured by size.

---

### Where this section does not speak with certainty

- **That verses 9-27 of the sūra came down after an event of siege** is widely reported in the classical sources, and it was given in this commentary **in the language of "it is transmitted"**. The length of the siege, the names of the parties, the numbers, the existence and the form of the entrenchment were not presented as certain knowledge, **because they are not in the Qurʾān.** **No tribal or personal name was given.** The only settlement names the Qurʾān gives are **Yathrib** (13, in the mouths of those calling for flight) and **al-Madīna** (60), and the only personal name it gives is **Zayd** (37).
- **On the setting of the "two hearts" sentence in 33/4** three explanations were given in a table; the third (that it was an answer to a particular saying) was conveyed with the note "it is transmitted" and **was not presented as certain knowledge.** No reading of the sentence as a **scientific miracle** was entered into; the note in `STYLE.md` that "no hunting for scientific miracles is done" was observed.
- **On *ẓihār*, reliance was placed on `058-mucadele.md`** and the detail given there was not repeated. **The ruling on the preservation of the tie of lineage was conveyed; no legal detail was entered into;** the differing rulings of the schools on care, inheritance and guardianship **are not the subject of this commentary** and no preference was adopted.
- **The classical explanations of the extent of the ruling in the word *awlā* at 33/6** were given **in a table of four items.** **No legal conclusion was constructed and no preference was imposed.** Nor was any ruling issued on the extent of the maternal standing of the Prophet's wives.
- **The difference of reading in *lā muqāma lakum* at 33/13** (*muqām* / *maqām*) was recorded and **no preference was adopted.**
- **The difference of reading in the verse-ending alifs at 33/10, 66 and 67** (*al-ẓunūnā*, *al-Rasūlā*, *al-Sabīlā*) was recorded; it was stated that **it does not change the meaning.**
- **On the expression *qaḍā naḥbah* at 33/23** two explanations were given in a table and no preference was imposed. **The names mentioned in the classical sources in connection with this verse were not written.**
- **Four notes were entered explicitly about 33/26-27:** the verse recounts **a single, historical event**; the ruling is tied not to an identity but to an **act** (*ẓāharūhum*); the Qurʾān uses the same expression (*ahl al-kitāb*) in entirely different ways in other contexts; and **al-Mumtaḥina 60/8 was cited as a boundary verse.** **No wholesale ruling was framed about any religion, lineage or community, and current politics was not entered.**
- **The disagreement over the extent of *ahl al-bayt* at 33/33** was given impartially, **in a table with three views and their grounds.** **No preference was imposed, no insinuation was made against any group, no polemic was entered.** That the context before and after concerns the wives was recorded as a **datum of the text**; and it was recorded separately that **this does not by itself settle the debate.** **The material of the reports was not entered into;** no name, wording or source was given. It was recorded that the verse cannot be used to exalt one group or to exclude another.
- **The difference of reading in the word *qarna* at 33/33** (ق-ر-ر / و-ق-ر) was given in a table and no preference was adopted. **No legal ruling was issued** on the extent of the command (whether it is for its addressees alone or a general measure); two data of the text were recorded and it was stated that they do not settle the debate.
- **On *al-jāhiliyyati'l-ūlā* at 33/33** three explanations were given; **the application of the third (an allusion to a second ignorance) to contemporary events was not entered into.**
- **The reading of the word order at 33/35 — that the repetition itself carries information — was recorded explicitly as my own reading** and its four grounds (that the inclusive masculine form was an alternative, rhythm, length, the kind of attributes) were set out. The break of symmetry in the last two pairs was given as an **observation about style** and no ruling was drawn from it.
- **The imputations circulating in the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* and story-telling literature about *tukhfī fī nafsika* at 33/37 were NOT ENTERED INTO.** That these imputations were **rejected** in classical exegesis was recorded **in the language of "it is transmitted"**, and **I kept within what the wording of the verse says.** Two explanations resting on the wording were given in a table and **no preference was imposed.** The woman's name was not given; the details of the reports about how the marriage was made and how it ended were not entered into.
- **For the question of the places in the Qurʾān where a choice of the Prophet's is corrected**, reliance was placed on `080-abese.md`; the list there and the note that this is **an observation and not a proof** were not repeated but pointed to.
- **The difference of reading *khātam* / *khātim* at 33/40** was recorded in a table; it was stated that **the two readings come out at the same place** and no preference was adopted. **The theological debate over the extent of the construction was not entered into and no insinuation was made against any group.** That the distinction between *rasūl* and *nabī* is disputed was recorded and no ruling was issued.
- **No legal ruling was issued** about the expression *mā malakat aymānuhum* at 33/50. The Qurʾān's other notes on the same subject (the setting forward of manumission as a good deed and an expiation) were recorded **without concealment**; it was stated that the question has been long debated in the history of Islamic thought and **goes beyond the limits of this commentary.**
- **No legal ruling was issued** on the extent of the *min warāʾi ḥijāb* regulation at 33/53 and on its present-day applications. Whether the particle *min* is partitive or explicative (33/59) was given as two readings and no preference was imposed.
- **What the *ṣalāt* at 33/56 corresponds to according to its three subjects** was conveyed in a table with the classical distinction; **no preference was imposed.** It was recorded that the derivations offered for the concrete sense of the root ص-ل-و **are not certain** and no meaning was built upon them.
- **On the expression *yuʾdhūna'llāh* at 33/57** three explanations were given and no preference was imposed; and the **note of tanzīh** entered at `057-hadid.md` and at `002-bakara.md` was preserved, with al-Shūrā 42/11 cited as the limit.
- **No legal ruling was issued about the *jilbāb* at 33/59.** It was recorded that the descriptions the lexicographers give **carry no full certainty**; **the ground the verse itself gives (*an yuʿrafna fa-lā yuʾdhayna*) was brought forward;** it was stated in a table that the rulings the schools draw from this verse differ, and **the note "it is not binding" was entered.** **Current debates and politics were not entered.**
- **Four notes were entered for the sanction at 33/60-61:** that the verse is **conditional**, that it concerns an **act**, that no conclusion about anyone can be drawn from it today, and that **no legal ruling at all was drawn from it.**
- **The application of the concept of *sunnat Allāh* to contemporary events was not entered into**; the measure recorded at `048-fetih.md` was preserved: the concept is a measure for understanding the past, not an authority for judging the present.
- **The disagreement over what the *amāna* is at 33/72 was given in a table with five views and their grounds**, and no preference was imposed. **The debate over whether the adjectives *ẓalūm* and *jahūl* are praise or censure was given impartially, with four views**; two data emerging from the wording of the verse and the limit set by verse 73 were recorded **as observations**, not as a preference.
- **On the root of the word ٱلْإِنسَٰن** (أ-ن-س / ن-س-ي) **no certainty was claimed**; no preference was adopted for either derivation.
- **On the link of the root *fawz* with *mafāza* (a desert)** and on the link of the word *aṣīl* with the sense "root" **no certainty was claimed**; they were recorded but no meaning was built upon them.
- **The following readings are mine and were not presented as transmitted:** that the fourth verse is the key to reading the sūra; that the cost of naming is paid by the one on whom the name is fixed; the transfer of authority from the mouth to the heart (4-5); that in removing one bond the sūra always establishes another (5, 6, 40); that the name *al-aḥzāb* names the enemy as a temporary composite; that the siege section is not military but inward; that the *waʿada* contrast between 12 and 22 is the spine of the section; that the two faces of *shuḥḥ* come from a single disposition (19); the portrait of "being far off and asking for news" (20); the weight of the root *uswa* in the context of the siege and why the condition of exemplarity was laid down (21); the distinction between being shaken and changing (11 and 23); that a standing is neither privilege nor burden but the increase of both at once (30-31); that the repetition at 33/35 itself carries information; that the principle and the application were set thirty verses apart (4-5 and 37); that the place where the name is written into the Qurʾān is the place where the ruling is established (37); that the seal is not a part of the text but the sign of its completion (40); the three states of the eye (10, 19, 51); that *mustaʾnisīna li-ḥadīth* is a fine observation for three reasons, and that some boundaries cannot be defended by the one they belong to (53); that *buhtān* names the offence by the state of the one wronged (58); the web of roots between *murjifūn* and *zulzilū* (11 and 60); the position of the root ق-ل-ب at the two ends of the sūra (4 and 66); that the *ṭāʾiʿīn* / *abayna* contrast between Fuṣṣilat 41/11 and 33/72 gives a clue about what the trust is; that *ashfaqna* depicts the refusal as the result of a measuring; that responsibility is not carried by mass; and the agreement of the density of cognate accusatives with the content of the sūra. **The data of the text on which they rest (roots, patterns, particles, orders of verses, verse-endings) were shown separately each time.**
